112 Portals

विज्ञानभैरव Vijñāna Bhairava Tantra · all 112 verses on one page

This is the whole text in one document — every verse in Devanagari and IAST, both English registers, the practice notes, the word-by-word gloss and, where it has been read, Śivopādhyāya’s commentary. It carries no script and no pictures, so it prints, searches and copies cleanly. The reader is here →

Where this text comes from

Preface

The book opens with the Goddess losing patience. She has been given answers to the most important questions there are — that the ultimate is the ninefold, or the mass of sounds, or the three-headed form, or the triad of powers, the point and the resonance — and she says that none of these answers has settled anything: her doubt remains. Bhairava tells her she is right. All those forms, he says, are insubstantial as a conjuror's show, described for people whose minds wander and who are fond of elaborate ritual, the way a mother offers a sweet to get a child to do something. So she asks the only question left after an answer like that: by what means, then? The hundred and twelve are his reply.

It is a Kashmirian text and belongs to the Śaiva stream that later flowered as the Trika. Before that, dating is inference — somewhere between the seventh and the ninth century is the usual estimate, arrived at from the company the text keeps rather than from anything in it. No author is named. It presents itself instead as an extract: in the last verses the Goddess says she has now grasped the essence of the Rudrayāmala Tantra, a large work that has not survived, and whose name on printed books today belongs to other things. Whether this was ever a part of such a tantra, or claimed the descent to give itself standing, can no longer be determined.

The hundred and twelve run from verse twenty-four to verse one hundred thirty-five, one after another, in no order that anyone has managed to reduce to a system — a breath, then a sound, then a posture, then a grief. The count is the text's own: a hundred teachings of the waveless have been spoken in brief, it says afterwards, and twelve besides. The line that follows is of utmost importance. Engaged in even one of these, a person becomes Bhairava. You don't need all of them, and you don't need a sequence. One. You need only one. Out of the hundred and twelve you can find one that works for you.

Its later life is thinner than its reputation suggests. The great Kashmiri commentators of the tenth and eleventh centuries knew it and quoted it, but the commentary Kṣemarāja began on it breaks off after twenty-three verses, and the running commentary that carries a reader through all hundred and twelve was written by Śivopādhyāya some seven hundred years later. It reached print in 1918, in the Kashmir Series of Texts and Studies, and reached readers outside India only in the second half of the twentieth century. It is an old book that has been read continuously by very few people.

Because his is the only commentary that runs the whole way, we have given it a prominent place, and have translated it here from scans of the original handwritten Sanskrit. Śivopādhyāya called it the Uddyota, the illumination, and he works the way a commentator of his tradition works: gloss the words, name what the verse leaves unnamed, and say plainly when a line can be taken two ways. Where he says something that bears on a portal, it stands in the margin beside it, in our translation of his Sanskrit — and each note says which plate it came off, so that a reader who doubts it can go and look. His voice is not the text's own. Some seven hundred years lie between the verse and the gloss, he is sometimes reading the tantra through a later lens, and where his reading and ours part company the margin says so.

There is no shortage of good modern teachings on the Vijñāna Bhairava Tantra. It has been translated and expounded more than once, and some of those teachings are broad and deep, offered as the fruit of a life transformed by profound engagement with the text and the practice it inspires. This edition emerges differently, not from the study of the text but from practicing for decades Kriya Yoga, many of the techniques of which are alluded to in the Vijñāna Bhairava Tantra. This edition began as a private study tool, engaging with the text less as a teaching to be received than as a mirror to hold up to what had already developed — to gain perspective on the trodden path, to open what is not yet fully open, and to have something to share with a friend. That is still what it is for, and it will go on changing as the understanding it answers to ripens.

One last thing, since it sits oddly with a website. The closing verses forbid exactly this offering. Do not reveal it, Bhairava says — not to the disciple of another, not to the cruel, not to anyone without devotion at a teacher's feet; give away your village, your kingdom, your family, give your very breath, before you give this away. That injunction was made for a world in which a teaching went from a mouth to an ear, and in which the wrong hearer was a danger chiefly to himself. That cat has been let out of the bag by other teachers.

How to use the hundred and twelve

For practitioners

The count is permission, not a curriculum. After the last of them the text counts itself — a hundred teachings of the waveless, and twelve besides — and then adds a line well worth keeping: engaged in even one of these, a person becomes Bhairava. Not all of them, and not in order. So expect most of these doors to stay shut. A hundred and eleven closed doors is not a failure of practice or of reading. Take one at a time, and give it more than a single sitting before deciding it is not yours. Very likely you will encounter one or more of the teachings that resonate with you.

States are not stages, and it helps to keep them apart. Almost every argument this book seems to start with itself comes apart once you ask whether a verse is describing something that visits or something that stays. Any of these portals can give the state — Bhairava present, entire, now, and to someone with no training at all. What none of them gives in an afternoon is the developmental stage: the settling in which that presence is the ordinary ground rather than the weather, and in which the locus of identity transcends and includes the former daily life. The text speaks freely of both and marks neither, which is why one verse can promise everything at once and the next assume a lifetime. The tradition has its own form of the distinction, in the three states and the fourth beyond them. The language of states and stages is modern and brought in from outside; we use it because it makes the rest of the book easier to read.

The whole book is one instruction, and so is each verse. It is worth holding the possibility that these are not a hundred and twelve techniques but a hundred and twelve presentations of one thing — that the text entire is the vehicle of a transmission, and that each verse is that same vehicle entire. On such a reading the difficulty is never the mechanics. If what the verse carries is received, the rest of it is plain; if it is not, no amount of getting the procedure right will supply it. This is why a portal can open on a first reading and stay shut through a hundred careful sittings, and why the shut ones are not a scoring of your practice.

These were written for people who already had a teacher. The silences are not gaps in the text; they are the places where the mouth of a teacher was supposed to be. One verse names a sequence of twelve and does not say which twelve; another presupposes five seals and explains none of them. What is worth knowing is that a commentator sometimes stands in for a personal teacher — Śivopādhyāya names the twelve, and names the five, and where he does the margin passes it on with the scanned plate it came off, so you can take it as his and check it. Where no one supplies anything, leave the silence alone and be careful of trying to fill it with your analytical mind musings. Just sit with the silence and perhaps something will emerge for you, maybe a shift of consciousness, or perhaps not for that verse.

Your own practice is a legitimate way to read this. Kṣemarāja's commentary on the book breaks off after twenty-three verses, and Śivopādhyāya's, the one that carries a reader through all hundred and twelve, was written some seven hundred years later. Where a verse names something you already know from the inside, you are experiencing a commentary it never had. That cuts both ways: the recognition is yours, and is not by itself evidence about the verse. That's fine. Perhaps your own recognition is better for you than understanding the verse.

Read the ends of the verses. At the end of that. At the end of its dissolving. At the end of the out-breath. Releasing, releasing, at the last. Over and over the fruit is found not in the technique but at the point where the technique gives out. Any practice with a shape is superb at many things and structurally vulnerable to one: there is always a next round, and the instrument that carries you also carries you past the moment it was built to produce. Linger a little longer in the space where nothing comes next.

Notice who is doing it. Consider the verbs that name the result — is disclosed, shines forth, shows, rises, glides forth. Almost nowhere does the practitioner achieve anything; the subject of the sentence is the śakti, or the state, and the practitioner is the place where it happens. What you do is the technique, exactly and with effort. What follows the technique is not a further thing you do, and treating it as one is a reliable way to lose it.

The verb is almost never “imagine”. Twenty of these verses turn on bhāvayet and its relatives, and another sixteen on cintayet, dhyāyet, smaret or a verb of looking: contemplate, cultivate, meditate on, call to mind, gaze. Not one of them means make-believe. Bhāvanā is the causative of the verb to be, and it asks you to bring something into being by dwelling on it — which is not the same act as producing a picture, and in this book the thing dwelt on is usually reported as found rather than fabricated. Śivopādhyāya glosses the ray at the fifth portal as the breath you already have; the point of fire at the fourteenth is what appears when the eyes have been pressed and let go; the blackness at the sixty-fifth is seen, he says, not imagined. So the practice notes here say see where they might have said picture, and the difference is practical rather than fussy: attention spent manufacturing an image is attention unavailable to notice what is actually there. Where a portal genuinely does ask you to construct something — the body burned, the world burned, the universe emptied, a deity with four arms — the note says so outright, and the text marks it with a different word, vikalpa, the constructing thought. That is worth sitting with, because vikalpa is the same faculty the book elsewhere tells you to stop. The few constructive portals take the instrument the rest are dismantling and spend it.

A good many of these need no seat, no hour and no preparation. The verge of a sneeze. Terror, grief, the ground dropping away underfoot. The onset of hunger and its end. The threshold where the outer world has gone and sleep has not yet come. Attention laid in the joining of seer and seen rather than on either one. None of this is performed; these states arrive, whether or not you are practising. The preparation is availability — be aware of the named possibilities, and when one of them takes you, fall into the gap instead of moving past it. Learn to notice and let go.

The book contradicts itself, and means to. Wherever the mind goes, one verse says, that is already the state of Śiva, so let it go. Thirteen doors later the same four words open a verse that tells you to drop whatever it lands on, instantly. Desire is led back to its source in one place and ridden in another. Nothing here ranks them. This is an anthology of doorways assembled by people who cared whether a door opened, not whether the doors could be arranged into a system. Find out which reading is yours in the moment rather than determining it in advance.

Some of these want an experienced teacher, and the book does not supply one. The plainest risk is not injury but disorientation. Sustained practice of powerful techniques can loosen a person from ordinary life and produce dissociation from it — which is in a sense what they are for, and which is exactly why most people carrying duties and responsibilities are better served by not having sudden or semi-permanent shifts in their state of consciousness. Teachers in living lineages often decline students who are dealing with mental health difficulties, for the same reason: the practices are strong. Where we think a particular portal carries real risk we have said so in the note beneath it, but that is a poor substitute for someone who can watch you over time. Where to look for such a person is a fair question and we will answer it once: the Kriya Yoga lineage teaches practices that closely resemble a number of these, and kriya.org is a way to reach one branch of that tree. If you are working on your own, be gentle with yourself, and be especially careful of long stretches spent holding the breath or breathing in a pattern that is not natural to you. If you are unsure, the doors that ask nothing of the body are not lesser doors.

What we have done to it

About this edition

The numbers on the doors are ours; the verse numbers are the book's. The Vijñāna Bhairava is one continuous poem of a hundred and sixty-odd verses, and the hundred and twelve do not begin at its beginning. The Goddess asks her question and Bhairava answers, and the first portal is verse 24. So a door's number is always its verse number less twenty-three — portal 1 is verse 24, portal 112 is verse 135 — and both are printed at the head of every entry so that a reader holding another edition can find the same line in it. The useful consequence is that distances agree: when a note says a practice returns six portals back, it is also six verses back, and either count will get you there.

One Sanskrit line, several honest English ones. Sanskrit of this kind is compressed past anything English can carry in a single pass. A compound may be read as the light of awareness or as awareness, which is light, with nothing in the grammar to decide between them; a word may hold two senses at once and be meant to; a case ending may make the practitioner the one who acts or the place where something happens. So translators of this text arrive at genuinely different verses, and they are not all being careless — the multiplicity is in the original, and a translation is a choice made and then, usually, not shown. Here two are shown. The received line is held to what the words will license and keeps the text's own difficulty. The plain line beside it says the same thing in ordinary English: warmer, and never a different reading. Where the line truly does admit two readings, the note gives both and declines to choose.

Where we disagree we say so, and where we borrow we say that too. Two things happen in these notes that a reader is owed warning of. The first is disagreement — with the verse now and then, more often with the commentary. Where our reading and Śivopādhyāya's part company the margin says which is which and leaves the disagreement standing rather than quietly settling it. The second is borrowing. Some of what is said here comes from outside this tradition altogether, to shed light on this tradition: a distinction from a modern writer on development, a line from a Zen teacher, a sentence from the Bible. Those are ours and not the text's, and where one appears it is named as an import instead of being allowed to pass as the tradition's own.

The sorting into paths is editorial. Some notes place a portal among the ways — the way of the individual, the way of power, the way of Śiva. The threefold scheme is genuinely Kashmiri, but it belongs to later systematic writing, and the hundred and twelve carry no such labels in the text. Ours are assigned by us. Where Śivopādhyāya assigns one the margin reports it, and he and we do not always agree. Take the label as a reading aid and never as a reason to think a door is not for you.

001Where the two breaths arise

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 24 · dhāraṇā 1 · Breath · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → subtle

ऊर्ध्वे प्राणो ह्य् अधो जीवो विसर्गात्मा परोच्चरेत्

उत्पत्तिद्वितयस्थाने भरणाद् भरिता स्थितिः

ūrdhve prāṇo hy adho jīvo visargātmā paroccaret
utpattidvitayasthāne bharaṇād bharitā sthitiḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Above, prāṇa; below, the life-breath — and the supreme, whose self is that letting-go, rises in utteranceat the place where each is born — fill there, and the state stands filled

Translation · colloquial

The breath rises and goes out, it sinks and comes in, and the great one, who is that letting-go itself, is saying herself in the turn.Each of the two is born somewhere. Stay full at those two places, and what you are stands full.

Practice

Sit with the breath and feel it as one energy uttering herself: rising and out as prāṇa, sinking and in as the life-breath. Visarga is the emission — the letting-go with which each breath releases into the other. Each breath is born at a place: where the out-breath starts, where the in-breath starts. Rest attention full in those two places. Filled there, the verse says, your state stands filled. Śivopādhyāya locates those two places, where the verse does not: the heart, and the dvādaśānta, the point about twelve fingers out beyond the nostrils where the out-breath spends itself. Take that as an offer rather than a correction — the verse gives no anatomy, and he is reading it eight centuries downstream. He also hears the two breaths as two letters, ha going out and sa coming in, so that the breath is already saying haṃsa, 'I am He', without anyone deciding to say it. If that is audible to you, the practice is to notice it rather than to recite it.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya explains this half-line six times over, and then sorts his own six explanations by path: the first and second are śāmbhava, the fourth and fifth śākta, the third and sixth āṇava. So the first of the 112 is not one technique but the same instant taken at three depths — which is why he can spend five plates on a verse that seems to say one simple thing. He also does what the verse refuses to do and names the two places: the heart, and the dvādaśānta twelve fingers out. And he hears the two breaths as ha and sa, the breath already sounding haṃsa, 'I am He' — so that what is to be recognised is not produced by the practice but only noticed in it.

Commentary

The breath turns twice — at the top of the in-breath, at the bottom of the out-breath — and this verse asks you to put your attention in the turn. You are not doing anything to the breath. You are attending to something it is already doing on its own, and about a third of the portals that follow work the same way. The two places named are junctures, not organs. The text gives no anatomy at all, here or anywhere. It comes first because it is the most available. Everyone has the breath, and the turn is happening now whether or not you notice it. Watch the verb the verse chooses: you do not enter this state or achieve it, you fill it, the way attention fills a vessel by going into the place. That much is the mechanism, and it is why so many of the verses after this one can afford to be a single line.

Word by word

ūrdhve — above, upward (loc. sg.); prāṇaḥ — the outgoing breath (m. nom. sg.); hi — for, indeed (emphatic particle); adhaḥ — below, downward (indecl.); jīvaḥ — the life-breath, here the descending in-breath (m. nom. sg.); visarga+ātmā — whose self is visarga, the emission [the two-pointed release, ḥ] (bahuvrīhi, nom. sg., qualifying parā); parā — the supreme one, the supreme śakti (f. nom. sg.); uccaret — rises, is uttered (opt. 3 sg., ud-√car); utpatti+dvitaya+sthāne — in the pair of places of arising (n. loc. sg.); bharaṇāt — from filling, from steady holding (n. abl. sg.); bharitā — filled, brimming (ppp f. nom. sg.); sthitiḥ — abiding, the state (f. nom. sg.)

002The breath not turning back

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 25 · dhāraṇā 2 · Breath · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → subtle

मरुतो ऽन्तर् बहिर् वापि वियद्युग्मानिवर्तनात्

भैरव्या भैरवस्येत्थम् भैरवि व्यज्यते वपुः

maruto 'ntar bahir vāpi viyadyugmānivartanāt
bhairavyā bhairavasyettham bhairavi vyajyate vapuḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

The breath, within or without — when it does not turn back from the pair of voidsthus, Bhairavī — through Bhairavī — Bhairava's body is disclosed

Translation · colloquial

The breath goes out, the breath goes in, and at each end is an emptiness it does not hurry back from.Stay where it does not turn, and through her the body of Bhairava is uncovered.

Practice

Follow the breath to its two ends: outside, after the out-breath; inside, after the in-breath. At each end there is an empty place where the breath has stopped and not yet turned back. Do not hurry it around the corner. Stay in the void where it rests. When the breath forgets to return, what stands disclosed there, the verse says, is the body of Bhairava. Śivopādhyāya is emphatic that the pause worth having is the one that arrives by itself — twice he calls it 'accomplished without effort' — and that it lasts an instant, not a held breath. He also points out that this pause is already happening, in you and in every animal, all day and all night, and that what makes it liberating is not performing it but recognising it as your own nature. So there is nothing here to achieve. There is only something to catch yourself already doing. And it does not stop when the breath starts again. What stands disclosed at the end of one breath can be noticed persisting through the whole of the cycle — through the in-breath, through the out-breath, through the next turning. Noticed, and not held: the moment you reach for it you are doing something again, and the pause was worth having precisely because nothing was being done. The turnings come all day, in you and in every animal. They are where it is first caught sight of, not where it lives.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya calls the pause at each end of the breath अयत्नसिद्धम्, 'accomplished without effort', and says it lasts an instant — a tuṭi, a lava. He is ruling out breath-retention in as many words. He then makes the larger claim: these eight turnings of the breath are already accomplished in every creature, beasts and birds included, all day and all night, without anyone's effort; they become liberating only when known as one's own nature. The second portal, on his reading, adds nothing to what you are already doing. It asks you to notice it.

Commentary

The same two junctures as the first portal, with the instruction turned around. There you noticed the turn; here you decline it, and let the pause at each end go on being a pause. Read carelessly this becomes breath-retention. It is not. Nothing is held, nothing is forced, and the moment you are keeping the breath out you have left the practice. What is asked is that you stop hurrying the breath around the corner and see how long the corner is. The second line is the theology of the whole book, stated at the second door: it is through Bhairavī that Bhairava is disclosed. Not the still point instead of her, but her stillness as the disclosure. The Goddess asked the question; she is also the means of its answer.

Word by word

marutaḥ — of the breath, the wind (m. gen. sg.); antaḥ — within (indecl.); bahiḥ — without, outside (indecl.); vā api — or also, whether... or (particles); viyat+yugma+anivartanāt — from [its] not turning back from the pair of skies, the two voids (n. abl. sg. of cause; viyat — sky, empty space; yugma — pair; anivartana — non-returning; ni-√vṛt construes with an ablative, here compounded); bhairavyā — through Bhairavī, the śakti (f. instr. sg.); bhairavasya — of Bhairava (m. gen. sg.); ittham — thus, in this way (indecl.); bhairavi — O Bhairavī (f. voc. sg., the Goddess addressed); vyajyate — is manifested, disclosed (pres. pass. 3 sg., vi-√añj); vapuḥ — body, essential form (n. nom. sg.)

Open portal 002 in the reader →

003Standing still in the middle

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 26 · dhāraṇā 3 · Breath · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle

न व्रजेन् न विशेच् छक्तिर् मरुद्रूपा विकासिते

निर्विकल्पतया मध्ये तया भैरवरूपता

na vrajen na viśec chaktir marudrūpā vikāsite
nirvikalpatayā madhye tayā bhairavarūpatā

Translation · literal Sanskrit

When the śakti whose form is breath goes neither out nor in — opened outin the madhya, by the thought-free state — through her, the bearing of Bhairava's form

Translation · colloquial

When the power that moves as breath neither leaves nor enters, but opens out where it stands,there in the middle, with nothing being made of it, she gives you Bhairava's own form.

Practice

This is not forced. What you contribute is the thought-free state: let the mind fall quiet of naming and choosing. As it deepens there are moments when the breath stands still on its own — not held, not pushed out, resting in the middle. Madhya is that centre between the two movements. Do not grab the stillness. Resting there with her, the verse says, you wear Bhairava's form. Śivopādhyāya adds the posture the verse leaves out. He says the stillness is entered through the Bhairavī mudrā, and quotes its definition: the aim within, the gaze without, the eyes neither closing nor opening. Sitting with the eyes open and soft, not blinking deliberately, looking at nothing in particular while the attention rests inward — that is his way into this verse, and it is worth trying against the eyes-closed version to see which one lets the breath go quiet first.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

The verse says only that the breath stops; Śivopādhyāya says how one comes to be sitting when it does. He quotes the definition of the Bhairavī mudrā — 'the aim within, the gaze without, free of the closing and opening of the eyes' — and makes it the door to this portal: eyes open, unblinking, attention turned inward. He also reads madhya technically, as the abode of the central channel, where the verse says only 'the middle'. And he closes by classing this verse and the one before it as the āṇava stage — where we have read this one as śākta. The disagreement is worth seeing: the paths are a way of sorting practices, not a property the practices carry.

Commentary

Most breath practice quiets the breath in order to quiet the mind. This verse runs the arrow the other way. It asks for the quiet mind first — thought-free, not naming, not choosing — and reports that the breath then stops of its own accord, going neither out nor in. Many people find the mind the harder of the two to quiet, and do better running it the usual way round — settle the breath, and let the mind follow. Anyone who has sat for more than a few months has met a spontaneous cessation of breathing, where the breath simply forgets to happen. What the verse contributes is to say that the gap is not an oddity to be noted but a place to abide. Madhya, the middle, is the central term of this school and it will keep returning; here it has its plainest sense, the centre between two movements. The difficulty is not technical. It is that noticing the stillness with any satisfaction ends it.

Word by word

na vrajet — does not go out (opt. 3 sg., √vraj, negated); na viśet — does not enter (opt. 3 sg., √viś, negated); śaktiḥ — the energy (f. nom. sg.); marut+rūpā — having the form of breath (f. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi); vikāsite — when [it] has unfolded, opened out (loc. sg. absolute, ppp of vi-√kas); nirvikalpatayā — through the state free of thought-constructs (f. instr. sg., abstract noun from nirvikalpa); madhye — in the middle, the madhya (loc. sg.); tayā — through her (f. instr. sg., the śakti); bhairava+rūpatā — the condition of having Bhairava's form (f. nom. sg., abstract noun)

Open portal 003 in the reader →

004The quiet at the breath's end

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 27 · dhāraṇā 4 · Breath · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → subtle

कुम्भिता रेचिता वापि पूरिता वा यदा भवेत्

तदन्ते शान्तनामासौ शक्त्या शान्तः प्रकाशते

kumbhitā recitā vāpi pūritā vā yadā bhavet
tadante śāntanāmāsau śaktyā śāntaḥ prakāśate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

When she is held in, or emptied out, or filled fullat the end of that she is named the stilled one; through the śakti, the still one shines forth

Translation · colloquial

Whether the breath is held, or let all the way out, or drawn all the way in,at the end of it she is called the quiet one, and through her the quiet one shines out.

Practice

Breathe deliberately once: hold the breath, or empty it right out, or fill it full. Then attend to the end — the moment the effort ceases and the breath's energy dies down. That dying-down is the practice; there she is called the stilled one. Rest in the stilling itself, not in the next breath, and let what shines there show itself.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya passes over this verse in five lines and adds no technique to it, which is itself informative in a commentary that spent five plates on the first portal. His one contribution is a verb: the stilled kumbhaka arises at the end of the effort — it is not the retention you performed. He calls the stilling the subsiding of difference, and the one who shines there beyond name and form.

Commentary

Hold the breath, or empty it right out, or fill full. It is the one early breath verse that asks for something deliberate, and the deliberate part is not the practice. The practice is a precondition to the cessation of the practice: an effort that has been made can then be seen to subside, and the subsiding is what you are here for. The later haṭha manuals build an entire discipline on kumbhaka (breath retention) and its cousins. This text spends one line on the technique and three words on the result, which tells you where it thinks the value is. She is named the stilled one at the end of the effort, not during it. Two things the verse does not say: how long, and how hard. Forced retention of the breath is one of the instructions in this book that can do real harm quickly, depending on your health. Go gently, and let your interest be in the dying-down, the abatement, rather than in intense practice of the technique. As a general rule, mild discomfort — the equivalent of a stretch that challenges you a little but does not hurt — should be the limit of how hard you push without a competent teacher's supervision.

Word by word

kumbhitā — held in, retained [as in kumbhaka] (ppp f. nom. sg., qualifying the breath-śakti); recitā — emptied out, expelled (ppp f. nom. sg.); vā api — or also; pūritā — filled in, inhaled full (ppp f. nom. sg.); vā — or; yadā — when (indecl.); bhavet — she may be (opt. 3 sg., √bhū); tat+ante — at the end of that (loc. sg.); śānta+nāmā — having the name 'stilled', 'pacified' (bahuvrīhi, nom. sg.); asau — she, that one (pron. nom. sg.); śaktyā — through the śakti (f. instr. sg.); śāntaḥ — the still one [Bhairava] (m. nom. sg.); prakāśate — shines forth, becomes manifest (pres. mid. 3 sg., pra-√kāś)

Open portal 004 in the reader →

005A ray rising from the root

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 28 · dhāraṇā 5 · Light · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

आमूलात् किरणाभासां सूक्ष्मात् सूक्ष्मतरात्मिकाम्

चिन्तयेत् तां द्विषट्कान्ते शाम्यन्तीम् भैरवोदयः

āmūlāt kiraṇābhāsāṃ sūkṣmāt sūkṣmatarātmikām
cintayet tāṃ dviṣaṭkānte śāmyantīm bhairavodayaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

From the root up she gleams like a ray, her nature subtler than the subtlecontemplate her growing still at the end of the twice-six — the dawning of Bhairava

Translation · colloquial

From the root of the body she gleams upward like a ray of light, finer than anything you would call fine.Watch her come to rest twelve fingers past the head: that stilling is Bhairava dawning.

Practice

Sit. See her rising from the root of the body as a single ray, growing finer as she climbs — finer than anything the senses could hold. Follow her to the point twelve finger-widths above the head, the twice-six, and watch her come to rest there. Where she stills, the verse says, Bhairava dawns. Stay with the stilling, not with what you are seeing. Śivopādhyāya says plainly who she is: the breath-power, the same energy the four portals before this one worked with. So the ray is not a new object to imagine but the breath followed along the body's axis, from the seat of birth upward. And he is exact about the ending: what discloses Bhairava is the melting away of the meditated form — including, he insists, the subtlest form, the one your own concentration has just made most vivid. The clearer she becomes, the more there is to let go of.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya identifies the ray as मरुच्छक्ति, the breath-power — the same energy the preceding portals worked with, now traced along the axis of the body from the seat of birth upward. So this is not a new imaginative object but the old one, followed further. And he is precise about the end: what discloses Bhairava is अगलनात्, the melting away of the meditated form — expressly including the subtlest form, the one raised up by the very intensity of the meditation. The image is not perfected. It is dissolved.

Commentary

The easy mistake here is to imagine and visualise what you are being invited to perceive. This verse introduces the axis that a dozen later verses take for granted: root of the body below, dvādaśānta twelve finger-breadths above the head. What is easy to miss is that 'subtler than the subtle' is a description and not praise. The ray refines as it rises, so if a bright vivid line is still there at the top, you have stopped following the verse and started decorating it. The image of the ray is scaffolding, and following it as it refines is what sublimes ordinary awareness into stillness. Where the ray ends — where she grows still — is the point of the practice, and the verse's last two words say what dawns there without asking you to see that too. And nothing is said about the centres the ray passes: no colours, no petals, no count. That apparatus belongs to later texts.

Word by word

ā-mūlāt — from the root [of the body] upward (abl. sg. with ā); kiraṇa+ābhāsām — gleaming like a ray of light (f. acc. sg., bahuvrīhi); sūkṣmāt — than the subtle (abl. of comparison); sūkṣmatara+ātmikām — whose nature is subtler still (f. acc. sg., agreeing with kiraṇābhāsām; KSTS prints the feminine, where the e-text had °ātmikam); cintayet — one should contemplate, dwell on in thought (opt. 3 sg., caus. of √cint); tām — her (f. acc. sg., the śakti); dviṣaṭka+ante — at the end of the twice-six [twelve finger-breadths, the dvādaśānta] (loc. sg.); śāmyantīm — subsiding, growing still (pres. part. f. acc. sg., √śam; KSTS, and the vivṛti glosses dvādaśānte śāmyantīm — the e-text's śyāmyantīm would be 'growing dark'); bhairava+udayaḥ — the arising, the dawning of Bhairava (m. nom. sg.)

Open portal 005 in the reader →

006Lightning from centre to centre

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 29 · dhāraṇā 6 · Light · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

उद्गच्छन्तीं तडिद्रूपाम् प्रतिचक्रं क्रमात् क्रमम्

ऊर्ध्वे मुष्टित्रयं यावत् तावद् अन्ते महोदयः

udgacchantīṃ taḍidrūpām praticakraṃ kramāt kramam
ūrdhve muṣṭitrayaṃ yāvat tāvad ante mahodayaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

[Contemplate her] rising as lightning — centre after centre, step by stepupward, as far as three fists — and there at the end, the great dawning

Translation · colloquial

Watch her go up like lightning, centre after centre, one step at a time,as far as three fists above you, and there at the end, the great dawn.

Practice

The same ascent, now sudden. See her as lightning, flashing upward from centre to centre — each cakra a rung, taken in order, one leap at a time. Let the flash climb to three fists above the head and no further. At that end-point, let it fade. What the verse promises there it calls the great dawning; your work is to leave room for it. Śivopādhyāya fixes the two ends of the climb: it runs from the kanda, the bulb below the navel, to the brahmarandhra at the crown — and then the last stretch, to the three fists above, which he says is the same dvādaśānta the verse before ended at. So the two portals end in the same place. He calls that point 'the one that fills the universe', which is a way of saying it is not really a distance at all.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

He treats this verse as an expansion of the one before rather than a second technique — एतदेव प्रपञ्चयति, 'he expands on just this'. He fixes the span of the ascent from the kanda to the brahmarandhra, and identifies the verse's 'three fists' with the dvādaśānta of portal 5, so both climbs end at the same point. That point he calls विश्वपूरकम्, the one that fills the universe.

Commentary

The previous portal climbed; this one flashes. Same ascent, different tempo, and the pairing is deliberate — this text repeatedly gives a technique twice, slow and sudden, and leaves you to find out which suits your temperament. Neither is the better version. Here the centres are named for the first and nearly the only time: praticakram, centre after centre. The text conspicuously does not say how many there are or what they contain, and anyone who arrives expecting the familiar six with their petals and syllables is bringing later equipment to an earlier room. Three fists above the head is roughly the twelve finger-breadths of the verse before, measured by another hand. The text is not fussy about the distance, only that you stop there.

Word by word

udgacchantīm — rising up (pres. part. f. acc. sg., ud-√gam; object of cintayet carried over from the previous verse); taḍid+rūpām — having the form of lightning (f. acc. sg., bahuvrīhi; KSTS taḍid- for the e-text's taḍit-); prati-cakram — at each cakra, centre after centre (adverbial accusative); kramāt kramam — step by step, stage after stage (abl. + acc., distributive idiom); ūrdhve — above, in the upper region (loc. sg.; KSTS, glossed ūrdhve muṣṭitrayaṃ dvādaśāntadhāma — the e-text had the adverb ūrdhvam); muṣṭi+trayam — a triad of fists, three fist-breadths (n. acc. sg., accusative of extent); yāvat — until, as far as (indecl.); tāvat — to that point, then (indecl., correlative); ante — at the end (loc. sg.); mahā+udayaḥ — the great arising, the great dawn (m. nom. sg.)

Open portal 006 in the reader →

007Releasing the twelve stations

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 30 · dhāraṇā 7 · Sound · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → causal

क्रमद्वादशकं सम्यग् द्वादशाक्षरभेदितम्

स्थूलसूक्ष्मपरस्थित्या मुक्त्वा मुक्त्वान्ततः शिवः

kramadvādaśakaṃ samyag dvādaśākṣarabheditam
sthūlasūkṣmaparasthityā muktvā muktvāntataḥ śivaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

The twelvefold sequence, rightly parted by the twelve soundsthrough gross, subtle, and supreme standing — releasing, releasing, at the last: Śiva

Translation · colloquial

Twelve stages in order, each one cut clean from the next by its own sound,taken coarse, then fine, then finest, and let go, let go, until at the last there is Shiva.

Practice

The verse gives a sequence of twelve, each step marked by its own syllable, and tells you to release each as it completes — muktvā muktvā, letting go and letting go — through its gross, subtle, and supreme mode. Sound each stage, refine it, release it, and carry nothing forward. When the last is released, what remains is Śiva. Śivopādhyāya names the twelve, and they are the stations of the ascent this book has been climbing since portal 5: the tip of the birth-place, the root, the bulb, the navel, the heart, the throat, the palate, the point between the brows, the brow, the aperture at the crown — and then two that are no longer in the body, śakti and the all-pervading. The twelve syllables he takes as the twelve vowels, from the first sound to the visarga. And he says what the three modes are for: first the station is made distinct, then it is felt to throb, then it turns to light. That is gross, subtle, supreme — three depths of one station, not three practices. Then it is let go and you begin the next. Whether to take his list is your decision. The verse withholds it, and a thousand years of readers have proposed others.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

The verse names twelve stations and never says which twelve, and readers have proposed lists for a thousand years. Śivopādhyāya simply gives one: the tip of the birth-place, root, bulb, navel, heart, throat, palate, brow-point, brow, the aperture at the crown, and then śakti and the all-pervading, which are past the body. The twelve syllables he takes as the twelve vowels, from the first to the visarga, setting the four 'neuter' vowels aside. And he unpacks the three modes as three depths of a single station — first made distinct, then throbbing, then turned to light. His answer is not the verse's answer, and it is not the only one on offer; but it is the answer this edition's own commentator gives, and it is a coherent one.

Commentary

Twelve stations, each one parted by its own syllable — and the verse never says which twelve. Candidates have been proposed for a thousand years and none of them is decisive. The honest position is that this was teacher's knowledge, and it is not in the text. What survives is the shape, and the shape is clear enough to work with: plumb each stage in its gross form, then its subtle form, then its causal form, and then let it go. Muktvā muktvā — releasing, releasing. The ladder is kicked away at every rung, not only the last, which is why the verse can end with Śiva instead of a thirteenth station. So you can practise it without knowing the syllables. Release, release, release, in each Cakra.

Word by word

krama+dvādaśakam — the twelvefold sequence (n. acc. sg.); samyak — rightly, completely (indecl.); dvādaśa+akṣara+bheditam — differentiated by the twelve akṣaras, the twelve syllables (n. acc. sg., ppp of √bhid in compound); sthūla+sūkṣma+para+sthityā — through [its] gross, subtle, and supreme mode of standing (f. instr. sg.); muktvā muktvā — releasing, then releasing again (gerund of √muc, repeated distributively); antataḥ — at the end, finally (indecl.); śivaḥ — Śiva (m. nom. sg.)

Open portal 007 in the reader →

008Filling to the crown

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 31 · dhāraṇā 8 · Light · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → causal

तयापूर्याशु मूर्धान्तं भङ्क्त्वा भ्रूक्षेपसेतुना

निर्विकल्पं मनः कृत्वा सर्वोर्ध्वे सर्वगोद्गमः

tayāpūryāśu mūrdhāntaṃ bhaṅktvā bhrūkṣepasetunā
nirvikalpaṃ manaḥ kṛtvā sarvordhve sarvagodgamaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Fill swiftly with her to the crown of the head; break through by the bridge of the knitted browsmake the mind free of thought-constructs: above everything, the rising of the all-pervading

Translation · colloquial

Fill quickly with her to the crown of the head, and break through there by the bridge your knitted brows make;let the mind stop making anything, and above everything the one that is everywhere rises.

Practice

Draw the breath-energy up quickly, filling the body to the crown of the head. At the barrier there, use the brows: knit them, and let that contraction be the bridge over which the energy breaks through. Then drop every effort of thought and make the mind construction-free. What rises after that rises above everything and is everywhere at once. Your work ends at the brow; the rising is not yours. Śivopādhyāya names this the khecarī mudrā — not the tongue technique of the later haṭha manuals, but this piercing of the brows — and his image for setu is a dam breaking a flow of water, which settles what kind of bridge is meant. He also attaches a condition the verse does not: the breaking-through is done 'by the method of the guru's instruction'. He is the only commentator on these first eight portals to require a teacher, and he requires one here. Take that as it is meant. Everything above is what the words say; whether to do it from a page is a different question, and his answer is no.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya calls this the khecarī mudrā — the older Trika sense of the term, the piercing of the brows, not the tongue technique of the later haṭha manuals. His simile for setu settles the metaphor: it breaks the barrier 'as a dam breaks a flow of water'. And he attaches a condition the verse does not have — the breaking-through is done गुरूपदेशयुक्त्या, by the method of the guru's instruction. Of the first eight portals this is the only one he will not let stand on its own words.

Commentary

Śivopādhyāya's metaphor repays attention, and it is worth being exact about how much of it is his. He writes that the piercing of the brows breaks through the crown as a dam breaks a flow of water. The dam is his. Reading it as a structure that holds water back in order to send it where you want is ours, and neither he nor the verse says that. What both do say is that something gives way. The knit of the brows is the small deliberate contraction that gives the rising energy something to break against. Everything up to the brow is technique. Then the verse changes register — make the mind free of construction, and what rises rises above everything and is everywhere. That last clause is not a further instruction. It is a statement about something that is no longer yours to do, and the practice fails exactly to the degree that you try to do it. One caution, and it is Śivopādhyāya's rather than ours: of the first eight portals this is the only one he will not let stand on its own words. He attaches a condition the verse does not have — the breaking-through is done gurūpadeśayuktyā, by the method of the guru's instruction. Tayā, with her, is the breath, so this is breath-work whatever else it is called, and forced manipulation of the breath is the likeliest way to come to harm here. There is no dosage in the verse. Assume less is meant than you think.

Word by word

tayā — with her, the breath-śakti (f. instr. sg.); āpūrya — having filled (gerund, ā-√pṝ); āśu — swiftly (indecl.); mūrdha+antam — to the end of the head, the crown (m. acc. sg.); bhaṅktvā — having broken [through] (gerund, √bhañj); bhrū+kṣepa+setunā — by the bridge of the throwing-together of the brows (m. instr. sg.; kṣepa — casting, contraction; setu — bridge, dam); nirvikalpam — free of thought-constructs (n. acc. sg., predicative); manaḥ — the mind (n. acc. sg.); kṛtvā — having made (gerund, √kṛ); sarva+ūrdhve — in what is above all (loc. sg.); sarvaga+udgamaḥ — the rising up of the all-pervading (m. nom. sg.)

Open portal 008 in the reader →

009The peacock's five circles

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 32 · dhāraṇā 9 · Body · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

शिखिपक्षैश् चित्ररूपैर् मण्डलैः शून्यपञ्चकम्

ध्यायतो ऽनुत्तरे शून्ये प्रवेशो हृदये भवेत्

śikhipakṣaiś citrarūpair maṇḍalaiḥ śūnyapañcakam
dhyāyato 'nuttare śūnye praveśo hṛdaye bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

The five voids — through the bright-patterned circles of a peacock's feathersfor the one meditating so, entry would open — into the unsurpassed void, into the heart

Translation · colloquial

Five emptinesses, held the way the bright rings in a peacock's feathers are held, one colour nested inside another.Meditate like that and a door opens: into the emptiness with nothing above it, into the heart.

Practice

Take the eye of a peacock feather: rings of colour nested one inside another. Meditate on the five voids that way — five hollows, one within the next, each subtler than the last, held in the mind as those bright circles. You are using colour to think emptiness. When the five have been passed through, entry comes of itself: into the void with nothing beyond it, which the verse calls the heart. Śivopādhyāya offers two ways to take the five, and you can try either. Spatially: above, below, the middle, and the two sides — the five directions a body sits in. Or by the senses: the five fields of sound, touch, form, taste and smell, which he calls void because in their bare state, before they are made into anything, there is nothing there to hold. He also reads the heart as where you meditate rather than where you arrive, which is worth taking literally — sit with the attention resting in the chest and let the five open from there.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya does what the verse declines to do and lists the five voids — twice, differently. Spatially: above, below, the middle, and the two sides. Or by the senses: the five fields of sound, touch, form, taste and smell, void because as bare sensa they are unmanifest. He also etymologises the verse's 'circles' as the senses themselves, 'what take up the essential sap'. And he reads the heart as the place one meditates from rather than the place one enters — the line permits both, and this edition's translation keeps the doubling.

Commentary

Colour used to conjure emptiness. It is a strange enough instruction to be worth slowing down over. The five voids are not listed, here or anywhere in the text, and the proposals made since are guesses dressed as knowledge. What is given is their arrangement: nested one inside the next like the rings in the eye of a peacock feather, each subtler than the one containing it. So the practice has a form even without the contents. You pass inward through hollows, and you do not stop at the first that feels final. Two words at the end carry the weight. Praveśa, entry — you are let in, you do not arrive. And hṛdaya, heart, which in this school is never the organ and never sentiment, but the centre in which the whole thing is held together. You can also feel your way through each of the five senses to the void beneath it — the eternal stillness that underlies and pervades whatever the sense is reporting.

Word by word

śikhi+pakṣaiḥ — by the feathers of the peacock (m. instr. pl.; śikhin — the crested one, the peacock); citra+rūpaiḥ — of variegated, many-coloured form (instr. pl.); maṇḍalaiḥ — by the circles, the eye-rings (n. instr. pl.); śūnya+pañcakam — the pentad of voids (n. acc. sg.); dhyāyataḥ — of/for the one meditating (pres. part. m. gen. sg., √dhyai); anuttare — in the unsurpassed, that with nothing higher (n. loc. sg.); śūnye — in the void (n. loc. sg.); praveśaḥ — entry (m. nom. sg.); hṛdaye — in the heart (n. loc. sg.); bhavet — would come about (opt. 3 sg., √bhū)

Open portal 009 in the reader →

010Anywhere at all

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 33 · dhāraṇā 10 · Space · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle

ईदृशेन क्रमेणैव यत्र कुत्रापि चिन्तना

शून्ये कुड्ये परे पात्रे स्वयं लीना वरप्रदा

īdṛśena krameṇaiva yatra kutrāpi cintanā
śūnye kuḍye pare pātre svayaṃ līnā varapradā

Translation · literal Sanskrit

By just such a sequence, thinking — anywhere at allon a void; on a wall; on some worthy vessel — dissolves of itself, and grants the boon

Translation · colloquial

By exactly these same graded steps, thinking can be set down anywhere at all,on empty air, on a wall, on some worthy vessel, and it melts of its own accord, and gives you what you came for.

Practice

The method generalises. Choose any resting place for the mind — empty air, a blank wall, another person — and give thinking a single object, by graded steps, as the earlier verses did. Then let the thinking thin out on its own. You do not end it; it ends. The promise lies in that self-dissolving: attention that has died into its object is what grants the gift. Śivopādhyāya makes the move sharper than 'any object will do'. He says to contemplate the whole twelvefold ascent — the stations from the root to the crown and past it — in the outer void, or in a wall, exactly as in the body of your own self. So what is being generalised is not the attention but the body: you find your own axis in the empty air in front of you. And where the verse says 'a worthy vessel' he takes it to mean a person — a disciple of spotless mind — not a bowl. Doing this practice with another human being in front of you is a different undertaking from doing it with a wall, and he offers both.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

This is the verse where the text stops and says the object was never the point. Śivopādhyāya makes it more specific than that: contemplate the twelvefold ascent from root to crown in the outer void or in a wall exactly as in your own body — the axis is what gets transferred, not merely the attention. And 'a worthy vessel' he reads as a person: a disciple of spotless mind. Sanskrit pātra means both a bowl and a fit recipient, and he takes the second.

Commentary

Nine portals in, the text stops and tells you what it has been doing. The object was never the point — take any object, empty air, a blank wall, a good bowl, and run the same graded attention into it. Wall-gazing has its own long life elsewhere, notably in Chan, and there is no need to suppose any connection between traditions. A wall is available to everyone and does the same nothing for all of us. What is precisely stated is the ending: svayaṃ līnā, the thinking dissolves of its own accord. You do not stop it. The single most common way to lose this practice is to try — to notice the thinning and take charge of it.

Word by word

īdṛśena — by such, of this kind (m. instr. sg.); krameṇa — by the sequence, the graded method (m. instr. sg.); eva — precisely, indeed (particle); yatra kutra api — wherever at all (indeclinable phrase); cintanā — thinking, directed contemplation (f. nom. sg.); śūnye — on a void, on empty [space] (loc. sg.); kuḍye — on a wall (n. loc. sg.); pare — on a surpassing, excellent (loc. sg.); pātre — vessel, worthy object (n. loc. sg.); svayam — of itself, of its own accord (indecl.); līnā — dissolved, merged (ppp f. nom. sg., agreeing with cintanā); vara+pradā — boon-granting (f. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi)

Open portal 010 in the reader →

011Mind inside the skull

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 34 · dhāraṇā 11 · Space · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

कपालान्तर् मनो न्यस्य तिष्ठन् मीलितलोचनः

क्रमेण मनसो दार्ढ्यात् लक्षयेत् लक्ष्यम् उत्तमम्

kapālāntar mano nyasya tiṣṭhan mīlitalocanaḥ
krameṇa manaso dārḍhyāt lakṣayet lakṣyam uttamam

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Set the mind inside the skull and stay, the eyes closedlittle by little, as the mind grows steady, sight the highest mark

Translation · colloquial

Set the mind down inside the skull and stay there, the eyes closed.Little by little, as the mind settles into stillness, sight the highest mark.

Practice

Set the mind down inside the skull, as if lowering it into the dark bowl of the head, and remain there. Do not look for anything at first; the instruction is to stay. Steadiness is the instrument: as the mind settles into stillness, a seeing becomes possible, and by degrees you discern the highest mark. Let it be found rather than hunted. Śivopādhyāya reads 'eyes closed' twice over. Outwardly: kūṇita, narrowed to a slit rather than shut — the ordinary posture of this literature, worth trying against a full closing. Inwardly, and this is his real gloss: saṃkucita-bheda-dṛṣṭi, the seeing of difference contracted. The eyelid is the small version of what is actually being asked. He also gives the mark a description the verse refuses — a self-luminous light in the aperture at the crown — and cites Patañjali for it. Take that as his, not the verse's. Sitting down expecting a light is precisely the way to manufacture one.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reads 'eyes closed' as saṃkucita-bheda-dṛṣṭi, the seeing of difference contracted — the eyelid being the outward half of it; he also glosses it kūṇita, narrowed to a slit rather than shut. And he takes the skull by an old etymology in which ka is the supreme Śakti and pāla the protector Śiva, so that 'inside the skull' means inside the conjunction of the two. Where the verse names the mark only as 'highest', he describes it: a self-luminous light in the aperture at the crown, on which concentration, meditation and absorption are accomplished in turn — citing Patañjali, 'on the light in the crown, the vision of the perfected ones'. The verse's silence about what is seen may well be deliberate.

Commentary

Attention put in a place rather than on an object, which is what dhāraṇā means at its most literal: a holding in a location. The place is odd and the oddness is useful. The inside of your own skull is somewhere you have never looked, and setting the mind down in that dark bowl has a disorienting quality that keeps the practice from going comfortable. The verse is unusually frank about the first stretch: nothing is promised except staying. Firmness comes by degrees, and only out of firmness does any seeing arise. The mark to be sighted is never described, only called highest. That is deliberate. A described mark is a thing to manufacture, and someone who sits down knowing what they are supposed to see will see it.

Word by word

kapāla+antaḥ — inside the skull (adverbial; kapāla — skull, cranium); manaḥ — the mind (n. acc. sg.); nyasya — having set down, placed (gerund, ni-√as); tiṣṭhan — standing, remaining (pres. part. m. nom. sg., √sthā); mīlita+locanaḥ — whose eyes are closed (m. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi); krameṇa — gradually, in due order (instr. sg., adverbial); manasaḥ — of the mind (n. gen. sg.); dārḍhyāt — from the firmness, the steadiness (n. abl. sg.); lakṣayet — one should sight, discern (opt. 3 sg., caus. of √lakṣ); lakṣyam — the mark, what is to be sighted (n. acc. sg.; KSTS, glossed uttamaṃ lakṣyaṃ lakṣayet — the e-text's laṣyam is not a word); uttamam — highest, utmost (n. acc. sg.)

012The lotus-fibre channel

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 35 · dhāraṇā 12 · Light · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

मध्यनाडी मध्यसंस्था बिससूत्राभरूपया

ध्यातान्तर्व्योमया देव्या तया देवः प्रकाशते

madhyanāḍī madhyasaṃsthā bisasūtrābharūpayā
dhyātāntarvyomayā devyā tayā devaḥ prakāśate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

The central channel stands in the midmost, her form the sheen of a lotus-stem fibremeditated on as the goddess who is inner sky — through her the god shines forth

Translation · colloquial

The channel that runs up the middle stands in the midmost, and she has the sheen of the thread inside a lotus stem.Meditate on her as the goddess who is inner sky, and through her the god shines out.

Practice

Find the middle channel, the nāḍī that runs up the exact centre of the body. See her as fine and pale as the thread inside a lotus stalk — and hollow: meditate on her inside as open sky. Hold both at once, utter fineness and utter spaciousness, until the channel is nothing but inner sky. Through her, the verse says, the god comes to light. You do not have to fetch him. Śivopādhyāya puts the middle at the heart, and starting there is easier than trying to find a line through the whole body at once. He also says the thing that makes the two qualities stop fighting each other: inside the channel there is nothing but a void whose nature is the sky of consciousness, and it is out of that void that the breath-power comes. So the hollowness is not a space the goddess occupies. It is her. Follow the breath in until you are attending to where it comes from rather than to it.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya names the channel suṣumnā and puts 'the midmost' at the middle of the heart. His central remark is about the hollowness: within the channel there is nothing but a void whose self is the sky of consciousness, and it is out of that void that the breath-power issues. The emptiness is not something the goddess contains — it is what she is. Note also what he does not do. Reading this verse from fully inside the tradition, he reaches for marut-śakti, the breath-power carried forward from the verses before, and never once for a coiled serpent.

Commentary

The channel is as fine as the pale thread inside a lotus stalk, and it is open sky within. Thread and sky, held at once — and the difficulty is that the two pull against each other. Most people can hold one and lose the other; the practice is both together, until fineness and spaciousness stop being a contradiction. What is not here is worth as much as what is. No serpent, no coiled power at the base, no waking of anything. The whole kuṇḍalinī apparatus that later readers bring to a verse like this is absent, and the channel is simply where she stands. And once more the grammar does the doctrine: it is through her that the god shines forth. He is not fetched. She is not a conduit to something better than herself.

Word by word

madhya+nāḍī — the central channel (f. nom. sg.; nāḍī — subtle channel); madhya+saṃsthā — standing in the middle (f. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi); bisa+sūtra+ābha+rūpayā — with a form like the sheen of the fibre-thread in a lotus stem (f. instr. sg.; bisa — lotus stem; sūtra — thread; ābhā — gleam, likeness); dhyātā — meditated upon (ppp f. nom. sg., √dhyai, agreeing with nāḍī); antar+vyomayā — who is inner sky, inner space (f. instr. sg., bahuvrīhi); devyā — by the goddess (f. instr. sg.); tayā — by her (f. instr. sg.); devaḥ — the god (m. nom. sg.); prakāśate — shines forth (pres. mid. 3 sg., pra-√kāś)

013Sealing the sense doors

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 36 · dhāraṇā 13 · Light · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → subtle

कररुद्धदृगस्त्रेण भ्रूभेदाद् द्वाररोधनात्

दृष्टे बिन्दौ क्रमाल् लीने तन्मध्ये परमा स्थितिः

kararuddhadṛgastreṇa bhrūbhedād dvārarodhanāt
dṛṣṭe bindau kramāl līne tanmadhye paramā sthitiḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

With the weapon of hands stopping the eyes; by piercing at the brow; by barring the doorsa bindu is seen and by degrees dissolves; in the middle of that, the supreme abiding

Translation · colloquial

The hands over the eyes for a weapon, a piercing at the brow, every door barred:a point of light is seen, and by degrees it goes, and in the middle of its going is the highest place to stand.

Practice

Use the hands to close the head's openings — eyes, ears, nostrils, lips — so that sight is stopped at its own gate. Press attention into the point between the brows until it gives way. In the darkness a bindu appears: a compact point of light. Watch it without touching it. It fades by degrees; follow it in. In the middle of its vanishing is the abiding the verse calls supreme. Śivopādhyāya gives the hand position the verse assumes and does not describe: thumbs first, then forefingers, then the rest in order, the two hands between them stopping every opening in the face. He also says what makes the point fade — one-pointedness drawing on it, by degrees. So the watching is not passive; the narrowing of attention is the mechanism, and there is nothing else to do. And he reads the brow not as a place to pierce but as a knot. His word for what becomes of it is vidāraṇa, a rending — harsher than the knot image leads you to expect, and his rather than ours. Nothing in the practice asks you to supply that force. You supply the one-pointedness; the knot gives way or it does not. In his reading the bindu dissolves into the sky of consciousness, and the 'middle' you end in is the midst of that, not the middle of the vanishing point.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

The verse assumes a hand position it never describes. Śivopādhyāya describes it: thumb, forefinger and the rest in sequence, the two hands stopping every opening of the face — the word for the eyes standing in for the whole set. He reads the brow not as a point to pierce but as a knot to be torn open, and he names the mechanism of the fading: one-pointedness drawing on the bindu, by degrees. He then construes the verse's three phrases three different ways over, and settles on none, so this translation keeps the order and the ambiguity the Sanskrit has.

Commentary

The hands do physical work here, and what appears is described as seen rather than imagined. Sealing the outward channels is an old and widespread practice. The classical name is pratyahara, withdrawal, and the later hatha manuals build a whole family of mudras on the same principle: close the doors and the pressure goes inward. This one can be read alongside the breath practices already given, and the sealing done while you work with the breath. Two cautions, since the verse gives none. Practising alone, let the hands rest on the eyes rather than press them. Sustained pressure on the eyeball raises the pressure inside it and can slow the heart, and anyone with glaucoma, a retinal condition, high myopia or recent eye surgery should close the lids and leave it there. And when the sealing is combined with breathwork, it is your capacity and comfort with the breath that sets the limit, not the seal. What is distinctive here is the ending. The bindu is not to be held, brightened or interpreted; the instruction is to stay with it while it fades, and the abiding is located in the departure. Notice what the verse omits: how long, how hard, and what to do if nothing comes. That silence runs through all 112 and is not damage in transmission. It is the part a teacher was there for.

Word by word

kara+ruddha+dṛś+astreṇa — with the weapon that is the eyes [and openings] stopped by the hands (instr. sg. compound; kara — hand; ruddha — blocked, ppp of √rudh; dṛś — eye, sight; astra — weapon); bhrū+bhedāt — from the piercing of the brow[-centre] (m. abl. sg.; bheda — splitting, piercing); dvāra+rodhanāt — from the blocking of the doors, the sense-openings (n. abl. sg.); dṛṣṭe — when seen (ppp loc. sg., locative absolute); bindau — the bindu, the point [of light] (m. loc. sg., locative absolute); kramāt — gradually (abl. as adverb); līne — when dissolved (ppp loc. sg., locative absolute); tat+madhye — in the middle of that (loc. sg.); paramā — supreme (f. nom. sg.); sthitiḥ — abiding, station (f. nom. sg.)

014A point of subtle fire

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 37 · dhāraṇā 14 · Light · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle → causal

धामान्तःक्षोभसम्भूतसूक्ष्माग्नितिलकाकृतिम्

बिन्दुं शिखान्ते हृदये लयान्ते ध्यायतो लयः

dhāmāntaḥkṣobhasambhūtasūkṣmāgnitilakākṛtim
binduṃ śikhānte hṛdaye layānte dhyāyato layaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Its shape a tilaka-mark of subtle fire, born of stirring within the seat of lightfor the one meditating on that bindu — at the crown's end; in the heart; at the end of its dissolving — dissolution

Translation · colloquial

A point shaped like the mark on a forehead, made of fine fire, born of a stirring deep in the seat of light.Meditate on it at the crown, and in the heart, and at the very end of its melting: and you melt.

Practice

A point small and exact as a tilaka-mark, made of subtle fire, arisen of itself from a stirring deep within the seat of light. Hold it at the crown's end, then in the heart. Do not brighten it; let it burn down. Attend to the very end of its going out: as the point dissolves, the one meditating dissolves with it. Śivopādhyāya reads the seat of light as the light in the eye itself, and the point as what arises there under pressure — the same hands, still on the same eyes, as the portal before. So this need not be imagined at all; it can be the thing that appears when the eyes have been pressed and released. His other reading is a lamp guttering at the moment it goes out, and the point that arises from that flicker. Either way the object is given rather than invented, which changes only one thing about the practice, and changes it completely: you cannot make more of it without going back and pressing again. Don't. The instruction is to attend to it going. He puts the crown's end at the dvādaśānta — twelve finger-widths above the head, the station portal 5 ended at, not the breath's outer point of portal 1 — and says what the dissolution dissolves into — the principle of light, in a culmination where conceiving has melted off.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reads the seat of light as the brightness residing in the eye, and the point as a pressure phosphene — arisen 'by intense pressing and the like', of the form of a ray of the eye. On his reading this portal is the continuation of the one before it, hands still on the eyes, and nothing here is imagined. His alternative is a lamp at the moment it gutters out. He puts the crown's end at the dvādaśānta, twelve fingers out, and names what the dissolving dissolves into: the principle of light, in a culmination where conceiving has melted away.

Commentary

Something is made — here a point of subtle fire, exact as a mark on the forehead — for the sole purpose of being watched as it diminishes. This is the third of the endings-practices, after the breath's dying-down and before the silence at the end of OM, and the family resemblance is the teaching. The instruction not given is the one the hand reaches for: do not brighten it. A bindu you are feeding is a bindu that will not finish. Since Śivopādhyāya reads the point as what arises under pressure on the eyes, our caution at the portal before applies here unchanged. But read him carefully: he is accounting for where the point comes from, not issuing a fresh instruction. On his reading the hands are already where the portal before left them, and what this verse asks is what to do with what has appeared — not how to make more of it. The verse says laya twice in three words, at the end of its dissolving, dissolution, and that repetition is the mechanism: attention that has genuinely gone into an object goes wherever the object goes. This is the same principle the text will use later for music and for pleasure, stated most nakedly here, where the object is nothing but a spark you evoked.

Word by word

dhāma+antaḥ+kṣobha+sambhūta+sūkṣma+agni+tilaka+ākṛtim — having the shape of a tilaka-mark of subtle fire arisen from stirring within the dhāman, the seat of light (m. acc. sg., bahuvrīhi; dhāman — abode, seat of light; kṣobha — stirring, agitation; sambhūta — arisen, ppp of sam-√bhū; tilaka — the forehead mark; ākṛti — shape); bindum — the bindu, the point (m. acc. sg.); śikhā+ante — at the end of the śikhā, the crown-tuft [or the flame-tip] (loc. sg.); hṛdaye — in the heart (n. loc. sg.); laya+ante — at the end of [its] dissolving (loc. sg.); dhyāyataḥ — for the one meditating (pres. part. m. gen. sg., √dhyai); layaḥ — dissolution (m. nom. sg.)
