015The unstruck sound

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 38 · dhāraṇā 15 · Sound · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle → causal

अनाहते पात्रकर्णे ऽभग्नशब्दे सरिद्द्रुते

शब्दब्रह्मणि निष्णातः परम् ब्रह्माधिगच्छति

anāhate pātrakarṇe 'bhagnaśabde sariddrute
śabdabrahmaṇi niṣṇātaḥ param brahmādhigacchati

Translation · literal Sanskrit

In the unstruck [sound] — unbroken, rushing like a river — in the ear, a fit vesselone bathed in the brahman that is sound reaches the supreme brahman

Translation · colloquial

There is a sound nothing strikes, unbroken, running like a river, and the ear is a vessel fit to hold it.Steep yourself in that sound until you are nothing but steeped, and you arrive at what is highest.

Practice

Sit where it is quiet and listen inward, not for any sound that something makes. The anāhata is the unstruck sound: nothing strikes to produce it, and it does not break off. When the ear has grown fit for it, it runs on like a river heard from inside. Do not examine it. Enter it and stay, as you would stay in water. The immersion itself is the practice. Śivopādhyāya is worth hearing on the question everyone asks, which is whether there is really a sound. He records that some read the verse to mean the ears are precisely not the vessel and the thing is apprehended only within — and then he says where the practitioners come down: in point of fact it is heard, as an object of the ear. He also says it sounds day and night without a break, which changes what you are doing: nothing is going to start. You are coming within earshot of something already running. And he says it is not merely uncaused but self-arisen, risen up of its own sap, and that it has a name — Nāda the Lord. Where our reading has you bathe in it, his has you do the three stages in it, concentration, meditation, absorption. Either way the instruction is to stay.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya records that the tradition argued about whether this sound is actually heard. The compound can be read 'in which the ears are the vessel' or, divided otherwise, 'in which the ears are not the vessel' — apprehended only within. He gives both, then reports where the weight fell: the practitioners say it is in fact heard, as an object of the ear. He also says it sounds day and night without interruption, so nothing begins when you sit; and that it is risen of its own sap, not merely uncaused. He names it Nāda the Lord.

Commentary

We are talking about the OM sound — the unstruck sound. Nothing strikes to make it, so it never stops. Listening inward for it is a very old and very widely promulgated practice, and there is a whole later discipline, nāda-yoga, built on this verse's territory. One plain thing this text will not tell you: sit in a quiet room and most people hear something, and much of what they hear is the body — blood, and in a great many people a permanent ringing that a physician would name in a minute. The verse is indifferent to that adjudication and so should you be. The unstruck sound is everywhere and mostly unnoticed, and you can find it beneath and within whatever you hear of the body. Its instruction is not to identify the sound but to bathe in it, to marinate in it; niṣṇāta means bathed, and the practice is immersion, not analysis. The moment you begin examining what you are hearing, you are on the bank rather than in the water.

Word by word

anāhate — in the unstruck [sound] (loc. sg., agreeing with an implied nāda/śabda; an-āhata, not struck); pātra+karṇe — in the ear [that is a] fit vessel (m. loc. sg.; pātra, worthy recipient); a+bhagna+śabde — whose sound is unbroken (loc. sg., bahuvrīhi agreeing with the same); sarit+drute — rushing like a river (loc. sg.; sarit, river + druta, ppp of dru, run/flow); śabda+brahmaṇi — in the brahman that is sound (n. loc. sg.); niṣṇātaḥ — immersed, adept (m. nom. sg., ppp of ni+snā, lit. bathed in); param — supreme (n. acc. sg.); brahma — brahman (n. acc. sg.); adhigacchati — he reaches (3rd sg. pres. act., adhi+gam)

Open portal 015 in the reader →

016The silence after OM

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 39 · dhāraṇā 16 · Sound · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle → causal

प्रणवादिसमुच्चारात् प्लुतान्ते शून्यभावनात्

शून्यया परया शक्त्या शून्यताम् एति भैरवि

praṇavādisamuccārāt plutānte śūnyabhāvanāt
śūnyayā parayā śaktyā śūnyatām eti bhairavi

Translation · literal Sanskrit

From the full voicing of the praṇava or its like — from contemplating the empty as the drawn-out vowel endsby the empty, supreme śakti one goes to emptiness, Bhairavī

Translation · colloquial

Sound the syllable all the way out, and as the long vowel dies away, hold the empty.Then an empty and supreme power carries you into emptiness, Bhairavi.

Practice

Sound OM once, slowly, and let the last vowel stretch long past its ordinary length. When it gives out, do not start again. Attend to the empty place the sound leaves behind, and rest there. You are not asked to hold anything; the verse says an empty, supreme śakti — power — carries you into emptiness. Your part is the full utterance and the willingness to stay in what follows. Śivopādhyāya is explicit that OM is one option among several — the Vedic praṇava is OM, the Śaiva HŪṂ, the māyā-praṇava HRĪṂ, and the tantras teach more. Use whichever you have. He gives a good image for the length: like the ringing of an empty vessel, a resonance left to run out rather than a note pushed with the breath. And he says the tail is not undifferentiated. As the sound thins, it passes through subtle phases in order — bindu, the half-moon, and finer ones after — and the whole reason for the protraction is to give them time to form. The last of them, he says, cannot be uttered at all. That is where the practice is going, and it is why you do not start again.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reads 'or its like' widely: the Vedic praṇava is OM, the Śaiva HŪṂ, the māyā-praṇava HRĪṂ, and the tantras teach many more. His remark on the length is an instruction in itself — the protracted vowel should ring like an empty vessel, three morae, left to run out. And the silence is not undifferentiated: as the sound thins it passes through subtle resonances in order, bindu, half-moon and finer, which is what the length is for. The last of them, he says, is impossible to utter — a void whose form is undivided consciousness, and which nevertheless becomes the whole.

Commentary

You can test this one in the next minute: sound OM once, let the final vowel run long, and when it gives out do not start again. Of the first twenty it is the most immediately doable. The text is technically exact about the length — pluta is a real grammatical category, the three-mora vowel, held past any ordinary syllable — because the point is to make the ending unmistakable. Everything is in the silence that the sound leaves shaped behind it. What keeps this from being a nihilism is the phrase most easily skated over: it is by an empty and supreme śakti that one goes to emptiness. The emptiness is a power. It is not an absence you fall into but something active that takes you, and your whole contribution is the full utterance and the willingness not to fill the gap.

Word by word

praṇava+ādi+samuccārāt — from the full utterance of the praṇava (OM) and the like (m. abl. sg.; sam+uccāra, complete voicing); pluta+ante — at the end of the prolated [vowel] (loc. sg.; pluta, the three-mora prolonged vowel); śūnya+bhāvanāt — from contemplation of the empty (abl. sg.; the text's bhāvānāt read as bhāvanāt, see notes); śūnyayā — empty (f. inst. sg.); parayā — supreme (f. inst. sg.); śaktyā — by the śakti (f. inst. sg.); śūnyatām — emptiness (f. acc. sg.); eti — he goes to (3rd sg. pres. act., i); bhairavi — O Bhairavī (f. voc. sg.)

Open portal 016 in the reader →

017Before and after a letter

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 40 · dhāraṇā 17 · Sound · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle → causal

यस्य कस्यापि वर्णस्य पूर्वान्ताव् अनुभावयेत्

शून्यया शून्यभूतो ऽसौ शून्याकारः पुमान् भवेत्

yasya kasyāpi varṇasya pūrvāntāv anubhāvayet
śūnyayā śūnyabhūto 'sau śūnyākāraḥ pumān bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Feel out the beginning and the end of any letter whateverby the empty [śakti] emptied, that person becomes one whose form is emptiness

Translation · colloquial

Take any letter at all, and feel out the edge before it and the edge after it.Emptied by the empty one, that person comes to have the shape of emptiness.

Practice

Take any letter of speech — any sound at all. Say it once, aloud or in the mind, and attend to its two edges: the moment before it arises, the moment after it stops. The letter itself is not the object; the emptiness on either side of it is. Attended to cleanly, the one attending takes on the shape of what is attended to. Śivopādhyāya makes the near edge much more findable. He glosses it as the wish to utter — the impulse, caught in the instant before anything is sounded. So the two edges are not the same kind of thing. The far one is a sound stopping, which is easy to notice; the near one is the intention to speak, which is not, and which is where most of this practice actually lies. And he says to contemplate the edges as pervaded by the void, rather than merely to attend to them: they are not the object either, they are where it shows through. What falls away, on his account, is not the person but the conceit of being a knower identified with a body.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

The shortest comment in this stretch, and it turns on one word. Śivopādhyāya glosses the letter's beginning as ucciccārayiṣā — the wish to utter, the impulse caught before anything is sounded. So the near edge is not silence but intention. He adds that the two edges are to be contemplated as pervaded by the void rather than attended to for themselves, and that what falls away at the end is the conceit of being a knower identified with the body.

Commentary

Yasya kasyāpi — of any letter whatever. Two words, and they are what make this verse remarkable. There is no seed syllable to be received, no initiation, no lineage requirement; the sound may be one you invented on the way to the kitchen, because the sound is not the object. Its edges are. Where earlier portals gave you the ending of a breath or of a note, this one gives both edges of the smallest sonic thing there is, and asks you to feel out the silence the letter comes from as well as the one it goes into. And here the text states its own working principle outright, having only implied it so far: attend to emptiness and you become empty; the one attending takes the form of what is attended. Everything in this book that promises to turn a person into something rests on that sentence.

Word by word

yasya kasya api — of any whatever (m. gen. sg., indefinite phrase); varṇasya — of a letter, a speech-sound (m. gen. sg.); pūrva+antau — the beginning and the end (m. acc. du., dvandva: what precedes and where it ceases); anubhāvayet — let him attend to, bring into felt experience (3rd sg. opt. caus., anu+bhū); śūnyayā — by the empty [śakti] (f. inst. sg., noun unexpressed); śūnya+bhūtaḥ — become empty (m. nom. sg.); asau — that one (m. nom. sg., pron.); śūnya+ākāraḥ — whose form is emptiness (m. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi); pumān — the person (m. nom. sg., puṃs); bhavet — becomes (3rd sg. opt.)

018The end of a long note

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 41 · dhāraṇā 18 · Sound · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

तन्त्र्यादिवाद्यशब्देषु दीर्घेषु क्रमसंस्थितेः

अनन्यचेताः प्रत्यन्ते परव्योमवपुर् भवेत्

tantryādivādyaśabdeṣu dīrgheṣu kramasaṃsthiteḥ
ananyacetāḥ pratyante paravyomavapur bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

In the drawn-out sounds of stringed and other instruments, resting fixed in their sequencewith mind on nothing else, at the very end of each — one's body becomes the supreme sky

Translation · colloquial

In the long sounds of a plucked string, or any instrument like it, staying with them as they follow one another,with the mind on nothing else, at the very end of each note your body becomes the highest sky.

Practice

Listen to music whose notes are long — a bowed or plucked string is the verse's example. Give the whole mind to each note as it follows the last, refusing every other object. Then attend at the very end of a note, the point where sound gives way. What receives the note when it dies is a wideness; the verse calls it the supreme sky, and says you become its body. Śivopādhyāya says why the notes have to be long: length is the cause suited to producing a mind on nothing else. Only a note that goes on lets an unbroken stream of attention form, and only an unbroken stream leaves anything behind when it stops. He is also precise about the moment: absorption comes at the end, when the sound has ceased and no other object has yet arisen. That is the whole condition. If the mind has already moved to the next note, or to the fact that a note has ended, the gap has been filled and there is nothing to enter. His instruments are all long-decay plucked strings — the hundred-stringed, the seven-stringed vīṇā, the gourd vīṇā — and he treats the notes as belonging to a mode, a mūrchanā, so that each follows the last without your choosing it.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya gives the working part of this verse in one sentence: length is the cause appropriate to producing a mind on nothing else. Only a long note builds an unbroken stream of attention, and only that leaves a gap when it stops. He is exact about the moment too — absorption comes when the sound has ceased and no other object has yet arisen. His instruments are all long-decay strings: the hundred-stringed, the seven-stringed vīṇā, the gourd vīṇā. And he takes the verse's 'sequence' as a musical term, the mūrchanā, so that each note follows the last without your having to choose it.

Commentary

This is the book's other music verse, and it wants something quite different from the one further on. There, the mind melts into the song and stays; here it waits at the far end of each note for the moment the sound gives out. Pratyante, at the very end of each — so this happens dozens of times in one piece of music, not once at the close. The verse names strings for a good reason. A plucked or bowed note has an audible dying edge, and most of what comes out of a speaker today is compressed until it has none. A struck bowl, a plucked string, a bowed drone will all do it. What the verse claims is oddly precise, and you can check it: the silence a long note falls into is not the same silence as before it began. It has a size.

Word by word

tantrī+ādi+vādya+śabdeṣu — in the sounds of stringed and other instruments (m. loc. pl.; tantrī, string; vādya, instrument, what is played); dīrgheṣu — long, drawn out (loc. pl.); krama+saṃsthiteḥ — from settled abiding in their succession (f. abl. sg.; krama, sequence + saṃsthiti, remaining fixed); ananya+cetāḥ — with mind on nothing else (m. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi); prati+ante — at the very end [of each sound] (loc. sg.); para+vyoma+vapuḥ — having the supreme sky as body (m. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi; vyoman, sky, space; vapus, body, form); bhavet — he becomes (3rd sg. opt.)

019Sounding a mantra to its end

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 42 · dhāraṇā 19 · Sound · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → causal

पिण्डमन्त्रस्य सर्वस्य स्थूलवर्णक्रमेण तु

अर्धेन्दुबिन्दुनादान्तः शून्योच्चाराद् भवेच् छिवः

piṇḍamantrasya sarvasya sthūlavarṇakrameṇa tu
ardhendubindunādāntaḥ śūnyoccārād bhavec chivaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Any piṇḍa mantra whole, [voiced] through the sequence of its gross lettersending in half-moon, bindu, nāda — by the voicing into emptiness one becomes Śiva

Translation · colloquial

Take a whole seed-sound and voice it through its plain letters, in order,and let it thin to the half-moon, the point, the fading hum: voice it out into emptiness and you are Shiva.

Practice

Take a compact seed mantra and voice its audible letters in order, without hurry. Then follow the sound inward through its tail: the vowel closes into a half-heard nasal hum, gathers to a point, thins to bare vibration, and gives out. Ride the utterance all the way into that emptiness, and do not pull back at the threshold. If you have not been given a seed mantra, you are not shut out of this one. Śivopādhyāya says the piṇḍa mantra is OM — or so'ham, which is the same thing, since so'ham with its two consonants dropped leaves OM — and that it counts as a piṇḍa mantra because it is already distributed through the body from the navel upward. It is the mantra the breath says without anyone reciting it. He also walks the tail out much further than three steps, and puts each step somewhere: a at the navel, u at the heart, m at the mouth; then the bindu between the brows, the half-moon at the forehead, and above that a series growing finer — nirodhinī, nāda, nādānta, śakti, vyāpinī, samanā — up through the crown, ending at unmanā, the mind-beyond, which he says is what the verse means by 'emptiness'. Note the order: bindu first, then half-moon. The verse as printed has them the other way round and he says plainly that it should be read his way. The three gross letters take a full beat each; everything above the bindu takes half a beat; above the last there is no utterance left. Twelve beats for the whole ascent is his measure.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

The verse looks as though it needs something you were given. Śivopādhyāya says otherwise: the piṇḍa mantra is OM, or so'ham — which is OM with its two consonants dropped — and it counts as compacted because it is already laid out through the body from navel to crown. It is the mantra the breath says unrecited. He also extends the tail far past the three the verse names, and gives each step a seat in the body: a at the navel, u at the heart, m at the mouth, the bindu between the brows, the half-moon at the forehead, then nirodhinī, nāda, nādānta, śakti, vyāpinī, samanā, and last unmanā at the end of the crown-lock — which he says is what 'emptiness' means here. He notes the order should run bindu, half-moon, nāda rather than as the verse prints it, and he times the ascent: a full beat for each of the three gross letters, half a beat for each stage above the bindu, twelve beats in all.

Commentary

This is the first portal that assumes something you may not have. A piṇḍa mantra is a compacted seed syllable given in a lineage, and the text takes for granted that you were handed one. The verse before it deliberately needed nothing of the kind; the pairing is either a courtesy or an accident, but it means no one is shut out. What is described in the tail — half-moon, bindu, nāda — is not mysticism but the phonetics of a nasalised syllable heard closely. Anyone can find the ladder by humming a long m and refusing to stop: the vowel closes to a hum, the hum narrows to a point, the point thins to a vibration with no location, and then there is nothing. The whole practice is in not pulling out at that last stair, which is where the body wants its next breath.

Word by word

piṇḍa+mantrasya — of a piṇḍa mantra, a compacted seed-syllable (m. gen. sg.; piṇḍa, lump, mass); sarvasya — of any, whole (m. gen. sg.); sthūla+varṇa+krameṇa — through the sequence of its gross letters (m. inst. sg.); tu — and, but (particle); ardha+indu+bindu+nāda+antaḥ — ending in the half-moon, the bindu, and the nāda (m. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi on the implied utterance/utterer); śūnya+uccārāt — from the voicing [that issues] into emptiness (m. abl. sg.; uccāra, upward utterance); bhavet — he becomes (3rd sg. opt.; sandhi bhavec chivaḥ = bhavet śivaḥ); śivaḥ — Śiva (m. nom. sg.)

020Space in every direction

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 43 · dhāraṇā 20 · Space · śāktopāya · a means of mind · gross → subtle

निजदेहे सर्वदिक्कं युगपद् भावयेद् वियत्

निर्विकल्पमनास् तस्य वियत् सर्वम् प्रवर्तते

nijadehe sarvadikkaṃ yugapad bhāvayed viyat
nirvikalpamanās tasya viyat sarvam pravartate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

In your own body contemplate space in every direction, all at oncethe mind without constructs — and everything unfolds for you as space

Translation · colloquial

In your own body hold open space in every direction, all at once.With the mind making nothing of it, everything unfolds for you as space.

Practice

Sit still and contemplate open space within your own body — in every direction at once. Not the head, then the chest, then the back: all of it together, above, below, before, behind, on both sides. Held this way, the mind has no foothold for building one image after another. When its constructing stops, the verse says, everything — not the body only — unfolds as space. Śivopādhyāya has one word for this verse and it is the right one: akrama, without succession. The four portals before this were all sequence — letters in order, stations in order, morae counted out. This one is defined by having none, and the whole difficulty is that the mind will try to take the directions one at a time. He also lists only four of them, in front, right, left and behind, which is a ring rather than a sphere and is considerably easier to hold all at once. Start there if the full six keeps collapsing into a sequence.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Two lines of comment, and one word in them. Śivopādhyāya glosses 'all at once' as akrama, without succession — set against the krama, the strict sequence, on which the four portals before this one were built. He also names only four directions, in front, right, left and behind: a ring rather than a sphere, which is easier to hold whole. And he reads 'the mind without constructs' as one-pointedness brought in at the start rather than as what results.

Commentary

Three verses now run on one piece of engineering, and it is worth naming, because it explains all of them. The mind constructs serially — one thing, then the next thing. Yugapad, simultaneously, is the word that appears in each of the three, and the demand for everything at once is a demand the constructing faculty cannot meet. It does not stop because you told it to. It stops because it has been given a job with no serial form. In this first of the three the whole body is the site: space above, below, before, behind and to both sides, held together and not toured. The reward clause is larger than the practice, as it often is here — not the body only but everything unfolds as space — and the honest reading is that the verse is describing what has happened to some people, not issuing a guarantee.

Word by word

nija+dehe — in one's own body (loc. sg.); sarva+dikkam — belonging to all directions (n. acc. sg., adj. with viyat); yugapad — simultaneously (indecl.); bhāvayet — let him contemplate (3rd sg. opt. caus., bhū); viyat — space, sky (n. acc. sg.); nirvikalpa+manāḥ — whose mind is without constructs (m. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi; vikalpa, thought-construct); tasya — for him (m. gen. sg.); viyat — space (n. nom. sg.); sarvam — all, everything (n. nom. sg.); pravartate — comes about, unfolds (3rd sg. pres. mid., pra+vṛt)

021Emptiness at back and base

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 44 · dhāraṇā 21 · Space · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle

पृष्ठशून्यं मूलशून्यं युगपद् भावयेच् च यः

शरीरनिरपेक्षिण्या शक्त्या शून्यमना भवेत्

pṛṣṭhaśūnyaṃ mūlaśūnyaṃ yugapad bhāvayec ca yaḥ
śarīranirapekṣiṇyā śaktyā śūnyamanā bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Whoever contemplates at once the emptiness above and the emptiness belowby the śakti that takes no support from the body, that one's mind becomes empty

Translation · colloquial

Hold the empty space above you and the empty space below you, both at once.A power that needs no body to lean on will empty your mind.

Practice

Hold two emptinesses at once: one above, one below. Śivopādhyāya reads the pair as a vertical axis rather than as parts of the body — the emptiness overhead and the emptiness beneath. Both together, not alternating between them: the simultaneity is the practice. Held in two places at once, the mind cannot settle into either. The śakti at work here takes no support from the body, and he makes that the point of it — when what you are is no longer resting on the body, her independence of the body is simply what is left. Let her do the work, and the mind itself goes empty.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reads the pair vertically — ऊर्ध्वाधः, ‘above and below’ — rather than as the back and the base of the body (plate 0048). One verse earlier, at 43, he uses pṛṣṭha for the horizontal ‘behind’, so the vertical sense here is chosen rather than careless.

Commentary

Two places, and the choice of places is not arbitrary. The back and the base are the two regions of yourself you cannot see, cannot easily attend to, and spend your life with your attention turned away from. Putting emptiness in exactly those two, at the same moment, asks for something the ordinary body-sense is not built to do. That is the point, and the difficulty is honest difficulty rather than a failure of concentration. The phrase to sit with is the one describing the power at work: śakti that takes no support from the body. The practice is located in the body throughout, and the thing doing the work is explicitly not. The text feels no need to resolve that, and neither should you while you are doing it.

Word by word

pṛṣṭa+śūnyam — the emptiness at the back (n. acc. sg.; pṛṣṭa for pṛṣṭha, back); mūla+śūnyam — the emptiness at the root, the base (n. acc. sg.; the received line renders this pair 'above' and 'below'. Śivopādhyāya takes pṛṣṭha and mūla as the two ends of a vertical axis rather than as two places on the body, and the translation follows him. The words on their own say back and root); yugapad — simultaneously (indecl.); bhāvayet — should contemplate (3rd sg. opt. caus.); ca — and (particle); yaḥ — whoever (m. nom. sg., rel. pron.); śarīra+nirapekṣiṇyā — not dependent on the body (f. inst. sg., agreeing with śaktyā; nir+apekṣin, without regard to); śaktyā — by the śakti (f. inst. sg.); śūnya+manāḥ — empty-minded (m. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi); bhavet — he becomes (3rd sg. opt.)

022Three emptinesses at once

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 45 · dhāraṇā 22 · Space · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle

पृष्ठशून्यं मूलशून्यं हृच्छून्यम् भावयेत् स्थिरम्

युगपन् निर्विकल्पत्वान् निर्विकल्पोदयस् ततः

pṛṣṭhaśūnyaṃ mūlaśūnyaṃ hṛcchūnyam bhāvayet sthiram
yugapan nirvikalpatvān nirvikalpodayas tataḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Contemplate, steadily, emptiness above; emptiness below; emptiness in the heartall at once, free of constructs — and from that the construct-free stands revealed

Translation · colloquial

Steadily, and all at once, hold three empty places: above you, below you, and in the heart.Nothing is being made of them then — and what was never made stands plain.

Practice

As in the last dhāraṇā, but with a third place, and Śivopādhyāya names what the three are: above is the knower, below is the known, and the heart between them is the act of knowing itself. Empty all three at once — there is then no one to know, nothing to be known, and no knowing going on in between. Of the three he weights the middle: the heart's emptiness, he says, is the greater here. Steadily is how, not what — it qualifies the act, not the emptiness. And what comes of it does not arrive: he reads the verse's dawning as a becoming-manifest, so the construct-free state is not produced by the practice but uncovered by it, having been there the whole time. Do not look for it; keep the three.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

He names what the three empty places are: above is the knower, below the known, and the heart between them the act of knowing — पृष्ठे ऊर्ध्वे प्रमातृरूपं … मूलमपि अधोदेशात्मकं प्रमेयं … हृदयमध्येऽपि प्रमाणात्मके (plate 0048). Of the three he weights the middle. He also reads the verse’s dawning as उदय अभिव्यक्तिः — a becoming-manifest, not a coming-into-being.

Commentary

The same instruction with a third site, and the third is the heart — which in this text is never sentiment and never the organ, but the centre where the whole is held. So the escalation is not merely arithmetic. Two peripheral emptinesses become two and a centre, and the figure closes. The only new word is sthiram, steadily. This is not a flash; it is to be held. Whether two places or three is right for a given person is not a ranking, and reading it as one is the standing temptation of a numbered book. Take whichever number saturates your attention completely, since saturation is the whole mechanism — and note that the verse does not say the construct-free state is produced. It says it dawns, from that, of itself.

Word by word

pṛṣṭa+śūnyam — the emptiness at the back (n. acc. sg.); mūla+śūnyam — the emptiness at the base (n. acc. sg.; as at the portal before, the received line renders the pair 'above' and 'below' on Śivopādhyāya's reading of the axis. The words on their own say back and base); hṛt+śūnyam — the emptiness in the heart (n. acc. sg.; sandhi hṛcchūnyam); bhāvayet — let him contemplate (3rd sg. opt. caus.); sthiram — steadily (n. acc. sg., adverbial); yugapat — simultaneously (indecl.; sandhi yugapan before n-); nirvikalpatvāt — because of the absence of thought-constructs (n. abl. sg., abstract in -tva); nirvikalpa+udayaḥ — the arising of the construct-free [state] (m. nom. sg.); tataḥ — from that, thence (indecl.)

023Emptiness in one spot

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 46 · dhāraṇā 23 · Space · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

तनूदेशे शून्यतैव क्षणमात्रं विभावयेत्

निर्विकल्पं निर्विकल्पो निर्विकल्पस्वरूपभाक्

tanūdeśe śūnyataiva kṣaṇamātraṃ vibhāvayet
nirvikalpaṃ nirvikalpo nirvikalpasvarūpabhāk

Translation · literal Sanskrit

In one place in the body contemplate, a bare moment, emptiness alonewithout constructing a thought; construct-free, one shares in the construct-free nature

Translation · colloquial

In one spot in the body, for a bare moment, hold that there is nothing there at all.Do not make a thought of it. Free of that making, you come to share the nature of what is free of it.

Practice

Choose one place in the body. For a bare moment, no longer, contemplate that in that spot there is nothing at all: emptiness only, where flesh was. Do it without turning it into a thought about emptiness — a flash, not an argument. The moment done cleanly, free of constructs, leaves you sharing the nature of what is free of them. Śivopādhyāya reads the place as the seat of the limited knower rather than as any patch of tissue, so the spot to pick is wherever you feel yourself to be located — and he says how to empty it: by the directional contemplation of the portal three back, above and below and around, now brought down to one station and one instant. His remark on the second word is the one to carry in. 'Free of construction', where it describes the practitioner, he glosses as one in whom no fear has arisen at the apprehension that there is no support. Something in a person flinches when the ground under attention is taken away. Not flinching is the qualification, and it is the reason a moment is enough and also the reason a moment can be hard.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reads 'the body's place' as the station of the limited knower — the seat of whatever in you is doing the perceiving — rather than any particular patch of flesh, and he says the emptying is done by the directional contemplation of the twentieth portal brought to a single spot. His gloss on the middle 'construct-free', the one describing the practitioner, is the sentence to keep: one in whom no fear has arisen through the apprehension that there is no support.

Commentary

After three verses demanding everything at once, here is one demanding almost nothing: a single small place, for a single moment, empty. The economy is the instruction. A bare moment — kṣaṇamātram — is short enough that no thought about emptiness has time to form, and the verse says twice over that the contemplation must be free of construction, which means it has to be finished before the mind can get a description going. This one sits on the border of the third kind of means. It keeps a support, a patch of the body, so we have filed it with the second; but everything that makes it work is the instantaneity, and a practitioner who has met the sudden verses later in the book will recognise the family. For the same reason it is unusually easy to do badly. A moment spent thinking about a spot being empty is not this verse at all.

Word by word

tanū+deśe — in one region of the body (m. loc. sg.; tanū, body + deśa, place); śūnyatā — emptiness (f. nom. sg. where acc. śūnyatām is expected as object — see notes); eva — alone, only (particle); kṣaṇa+mātram — for a mere moment (n. acc. sg., adverbial); vibhāvayet — let him contemplate vividly (3rd sg. opt. caus., vi+bhū); nirvikalpam — without constructing (n. acc. sg., adverbial); nirvikalpaḥ — construct-free (m. nom. sg.); nirvikalpa+svarūpa+bhāk — partaking of the construct-free own-nature (m. nom. sg.; bhāj, sharing in)

024The body's stuff as space

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 47 · dhāraṇā 24 · Space · śāktopāya · a means of mind · gross → subtle

सर्वं देहगतं द्रव्यं वियद्व्याप्तं मृगेक्षणे

विभावयेत् ततस् तस्य भावना सा स्थिरा भवेत्

sarvaṃ dehagataṃ dravyaṃ viyadvyāptaṃ mṛgekṣaṇe
vibhāvayet tatas tasya bhāvanā sā sthirā bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

All the stuff the body holds, pervaded by space, gazelle-eyed one —contemplate it so, and that contemplation grows steady

Translation · colloquial

Everything the body is made of, shot through with space, doe-eyed one:hold it that way, and the holding itself grows steady in you.

Practice

Consider everything the body contains — flesh, fluid, bone, breath — and contemplate it all pervaded by space, the way water soaks through sand. Nothing is removed; everything is opened. The verse asks for vividness, not theory: feel the porousness rather than assert it. And it makes a plain promise: contemplated so, the contemplation itself grows steady. Śivopādhyāya opens his comment by naming who this one is for: the person in whose heart the emptiness of the portal before would not settle at once. That one was a moment on a single spot; this is the slow route through the whole inventory, bone and flesh and the rest, taken piece by piece and distinctly. So if the sudden version keeps failing, this is not a lesser practice, it is the prescribed next move. What gets laid down, on his reading, is a disposition rather than a skill — the leaning toward emptiness becomes habitual, and that is what the verse means by steady.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya opens by saying who this is for: one in whose heart the emptiness of the verse before did not settle in an instant. The portal before is the sudden version, a moment on a single spot; this is the graded one, going through the body's contents item by item — and the two are meant to be taken in that order. He also says what becomes steady is a disposition rather than an ability: the leaning toward emptiness, laid down as a habit.

Commentary

Nothing is closed, held or moved here. You contemplate, and that is the whole of it. It works on the most solid thing you have and asks you to see it as porous, and the verse is careful to ask for that vividly rather than to ask you to agree: not the proposition that the body is mostly space, which is cheap, but the felt version, which is not. Notice who is being spoken to. 'Gazelle-eyed one' is the Goddess, addressed by name, and half the charm of this text is that it is a conversation between lovers about how to disappear. What it promises is modest and believable: what steadies is the contemplation itself. Do it and it gets easier. You can check that for yourself.

Word by word

sarvam — all (n. acc. sg.); deha+gatam — contained in the body (n. acc. sg.); dravyam — substance, stuff (n. acc. sg.); viyat+vyāptam — pervaded by space (n. acc. sg.; vyāpta, ppp of vi+āp); mṛga+īkṣaṇe — O gazelle-eyed one (f. voc. sg., bahuvrīhi; the Goddess addressed); vibhāvayet — let him contemplate vividly (3rd sg. opt. caus., vi+bhū); tataḥ — then (indecl.); tasya — his (m. gen. sg.); bhāvanā — the contemplation (f. nom. sg.); sā — that (f. nom. sg.); sthirā — steady (f. nom. sg.); bhavet — becomes (3rd sg. opt.)

025The skin as a wall

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 48 · dhāraṇā 25 · Body · śāktopāya · a means of mind · gross → causal

देहान्तरे त्वग्विभागम् भित्तिभूतं विचिन्तयेत्

न किंचिद् अन्तरे तस्य ध्यायन्न् अध्येयभाग् भवेत्

dehāntare tvagvibhāgam bhittibhūtaṃ vicintayet
na kiṃcid antare tasya dhyāyann adhyeyabhāg bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Within the body consider the layer of skin a [mere] wallmeditating — nothing at all inside it — one comes to share in what cannot be meditated on

Translation · colloquial

Inside the body, take the layer of skin for a wall and nothing more.Meditate that there is nothing at all behind it, and you come to share in what no meditation can reach.

Practice

Consider the skin that bounds your body as a wall — a thin partition, nothing more. A wall around what? Meditate that inside it there is nothing at all. Stay with the nothing, not with the wall. What you arrive at is not one more object for meditation; the verse says you come to share in what no meditation can take as its object. Śivopādhyāya is exact about the wall, and both his adjectives are instructions. It is an outer wall, seen the way you would see the side of a building from the street, not a lining felt from inside. And it is inert — dead masonry, not living tissue. He chooses the skin, he says, because it is the body's densest part: the strongest case anyone could make for solidity, which is why demoting that one settles the rest. What subsides, on his account, is the claim of the subtle body — the city of eight that carries a person from life to life — to be the one who knows. And he notes that this happens while you are still meditating, which is the whole oddity of the verse: the practice delivers you to something no practice can take hold of.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya makes both halves of the wall an instruction: an outer wall, seen as from the street rather than felt as a lining, and an inert one, dead masonry rather than living tissue. He picks the skin because it is the body's densest part — the strongest case for solidity there is, which is why demoting it settles everything softer. What subsides, he says, is the puryaṣṭaka's claim to be the knower: the subtle body of eight, the part of a person that was thought to carry over from one life to the next.

Commentary

The verse before this asked you to see the body as porous. This one goes further and asks you to see it as a container with nothing in it — the skin demoted to a partition, and the partition enclosing no contents. What makes it more than a striking image is the word it ends on. Adhyeya: not-to-be-meditated-on. A meditation that succeeds by delivering you to what no meditation can take as an object, so that the practice cancels its own category on completion. That move is not incidental to this book. It is the shape of its argument, and it will be made again at the very last verse. The practical instruction hidden in the note is easy to lose: stay with the nothing, not with the wall. The wall is only there to make the nothing something you can attend to.

Word by word

deha+antare — within the body (loc. sg.; antara, interior); tvak+vibhāgam — the portion that is skin (m. acc. sg.; tvac, skin + vibhāga, division, layer); bhitti+bhūtam — being a [mere] wall (m. acc. sg.; bhitti, wall, partition); vicintayet — let him consider (3rd sg. opt., vi+cint); na kiṃcit — nothing at all (n. sg.); antare — inside (loc. sg.); tasya — of it (gen. sg.); dhyāyan — meditating (m. nom. sg., pres. act. part. of dhyai; sandhi dhyāyann); a+dhyeya+bhāk — sharing in what cannot be meditated on (m. nom. sg.; a-dhyeya, gerundive of dhyai negated, + bhāj); bhavet — he becomes (3rd sg. opt.)

026Sinking into the heart lotus

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 49 · dhāraṇā 26 · Feeling · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

हृद्याकाशे निलीनाक्षः पद्मसम्पुटमध्यगः

अनन्यचेताः सुभगे परं सौभाग्यम् आप्नुयात्

hṛdyākāśe nilīnākṣaḥ padmasampuṭamadhyagaḥ
ananyacetāḥ subhage paraṃ saubhāgyam āpnuyāt

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Eyes dissolved in the heart's space, gone into the middle of the closed lotus-cupfortunate one — with mind on nothing else, one reaches the highest fortune

Translation · colloquial

Eyes gone down into the space of the heart, sitting in the middle of a lotus closed like cupped hands,with the mind on nothing else, lucky one, you come into the greatest luck there is.

Practice

Let the eyes close and sink, and the other senses with them, into the space inside the heart. Enter the middle of the heart-lotus, its two halves closed over you like cupped hands. Keep the mind on nothing else — that is the whole instruction. The fortune the verse promises is found inside the closed cup, not by prying it open. Śivopādhyāya finds the heart's space in the breath: the station in the interval between the out-breath and the in-breath, which is where the very first portal began. So you can arrive at this one by breathing rather than by locating anything. He also says what the two lotuses are, and it is worth having. The upper is the means of knowing, the lower is the known, and the middle where you sit is the knower — pure consciousness, your own form. On that reading 'mind on nothing else' is not an effort of exclusion; once you are standing at the knower there is nothing else to be on. He says the cup is shut because the round of existence closes and opens like an eye, and this is the closing half. It does not stay shut: he ends by saying the mind that settles into the light of consciousness sees the whole world suffused with it.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya locates the heart's space in the breath — the station in the interval between the out-breath and the in-breath, which is where the first portal of all began. And he says what the two lotuses are: the upper is the means of knowing, the lower is the known, and the middle you sink into is the knower, pure consciousness, your own form. The cup is shut, he says, because existence contracts and expands like an eye closing and opening, and this is the closing. It does not stay closed — he ends with the mind that has settled into the light of consciousness seeing the whole world suffused with it.

Commentary

Almost everything later iconography does with the heart-lotus opens it. This verse closes it. Sampuṭa is two bowls fitted rim to rim — the shape of hands cupped over something small — and you are asked to be inside it with the halves shut. The feeling of the practice is enclosure rather than expansion, and that is worth knowing in advance, because a practitioner expecting a blossoming will spend the sitting trying to make the wrong thing happen. The Sanskrit contains a small joke that is also the argument. The Goddess is addressed as subhagā, fortunate one, and what the practice yields is saubhāgya, fortune. She is being told that she will get what she already is. And the senses are not shut out by force here — nilīna, dissolved, melted in with the eyes.

Word by word

hṛdi+ākāśe — in the space in the heart (loc. sg.; sandhi hṛdyākāśe); nilīna+akṣaḥ — his eyes [and senses] dissolved [into it] (m. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi; nilīna, ppp of ni+lī, melted into; akṣa, eye, sense-organ); padma+sampuṭa+madhya+gaḥ — gone into the middle of the closed lotus-cup (m. nom. sg.; sampuṭa, two bowls fitted rim to rim; -ga, going/gone to); ananya+cetāḥ — with mind on nothing else (m. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi); subhage — O fortunate one (f. voc. sg.; the Goddess); param — highest (n. acc. sg.); saubhāgyam — fortune, blessedness (n. acc. sg.); āpnuyāt — he would attain (3rd sg. opt., āp)

027Twelve finger-widths around the body

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 50 · dhāraṇā 27 · Awareness · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

सर्वतः स्वशरीरस्य द्वादशान्ते मनोलयात्

दृढबुद्धेर् दृढीभूतं तत्त्वलक्ष्यम् प्रवर्तते

sarvataḥ svaśarīrasya dvādaśānte manolayāt
dṛḍhabuddher dṛḍhībhūtaṃ tattvalakṣyam pravartate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

On every side of one's own body, by the mind's dissolving in the dvādaśāntafor one of firm understanding the aim — reality — comes forth and holds firm

Translation · colloquial

All around your own body, twelve fingers out, let the mind dissolve.For one whose understanding has gone firm, what is actually there comes forward and holds.

Practice

The dvādaśānta is the 'end of twelve' — a point twelve finger-widths out from the body. Here it surrounds you: a shell of still space a short span from the skin, on every side at once. Let the mind dissolve outward into that surrounding distance and stay dissolved. For one who holds this firmly, the verse says, the aim — reality itself — comes forth and holds firm in return. Śivopādhyāya puts it closer than a shell. There is a twelve-finger point, he says, even in the spaces between the pores — everywhere the body ends, and the body ends everywhere — because consciousness enters everywhere. So this need not be done as a distance held off the skin at all; it can be done at the skin, at any part of it, and found to be the same measure. He also glosses the dissolving as viśrānti, coming to rest. Nothing is being dispersed. The mind arrives somewhere and stops, and what then unfolds does not come toward you but spreads outward.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya puts the twelve-finger point closer than a shell around the body. There is one, he says, even in the spaces between the pores — wherever the body ends, which is everywhere — and the reason is that consciousness enters everywhere. He glosses the mind's dissolving as viśrānti, coming to rest: nothing is dispersed, the mind arrives somewhere and stops. And he reads the firmness as belonging to the mind rather than to what is found, so the reciprocity in the last line may be a gift of the Sanskrit's ambiguity rather than of the verse.

Commentary

The dvādaśānta has moved. At the fifth portal it was a point twelve finger-breadths above the head; here it is a distance on every side of the body, a shell of still space just off the skin. Anyone hoping to pin it to one anatomical spot will be disappointed, and the disappointment is the lesson: it is a measure, not an organ, and this text uses it wherever a measured remove from the body is wanted. The direction is unusual too. Most of these hundred and twelve move attention inward; this one puts it outside you and asks it to dissolve there. The verse's last line is a reciprocity, and the same word does both halves of it: to the firm, reality comes forth and stands firm. Steadiness in the practitioner and steadiness in what is found are described as one event.

Word by word

sarvataḥ — on every side (indecl.); sva+śarīrasya — of one's own body (n. gen. sg.); dvādaśa+ante — in the dvādaśānta, the 'end of twelve' [finger-widths] (m. loc. sg.); manas+layāt — from the dissolving of the mind (m. abl. sg.; laya, dissolution); dṛḍha+buddheḥ — for one of firm understanding (m. gen. sg., bahuvrīhi); dṛḍhī+bhūtam — become firm (n. nom. sg.; cvi-formation with bhū); tattva+lakṣyam — the aim, the mark, that is reality (n. nom. sg.; tattva, thatness + lakṣya, what is aimed at); pravartate — comes forth (3rd sg. pres. mid., pra+vṛt)

Open portal 027 in the reader →

028Casting the mind out

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 51 · dhāraṇā 28 · Awareness · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

यथा तथा यत्र तत्र द्वादशान्ते मनः क्षिपेत्

प्रतिक्षणं क्षीणवृत्तेर् वैलक्षण्यं दिनैर् भवेत्

yathā tathā yatra tatra dvādaśānte manaḥ kṣipet
pratikṣaṇaṃ kṣīṇavṛtter vailakṣaṇyaṃ dinair bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

In any way, in any place — cast the mind into the dvādaśāntamoment by moment; with the churning worn thin, within days comes a state unlike any other

Translation · colloquial

Any way at all, anywhere at all, throw the mind out to the point twelve fingers beyond you.Moment after moment, as the churning wears thin, within days something comes that is like nothing else.

Practice

No posture, no set place, no method: however you are, wherever you are, throw the mind out to the dvādaśānta — twelve finger-widths beyond the body — and when it wanders back, throw it again, moment by moment. Each throw cuts a churning short. As the churning wears thin, the verse promises, within days something arrives that is unlike anything you know. Keep throwing; do not measure. Śivopādhyāya reads both halves of 'in any way, in any place' more narrowly than they sound. 'In any way' he glosses as whatever mode of consciousness's own spreading-out has arisen of its own accord — so you are not picking a method, you are using the movement already under way. 'In any place' he bounds to whichever of the stations the book has already named, which after the previous verse is effectively anywhere on the skin. And 'cast the mind' he glosses simply as make it one-pointed; the throwing is a figure, not a strain. On the promise he is dry: 'within days' means in not much time. Which is the best answer to the temptation to count.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya takes the clock off the promise: 'within days', he says, means only in a little time. On the two open words he is narrower than they sound — 'in any way' is whatever mode of consciousness's spreading-out has arisen of its own accord, so you use the movement already under way rather than choosing one; and 'in any place' means at whichever of the stations already named. 'Cast the mind' he glosses simply as make it one-pointed. And he reports a variant that turns the line into a proportion: in whatever manner and wherever you cast the mind, in that manner and there the difference comes.

Commentary

No posture, no place, no preparation, in any manner, wherever you are. It is the most portable instruction in the book, and it is also the only one of the hundred and twelve that names a timescale. Within days. Everything else in this text is silent about how long anything takes, so the exception stands out — and it is a trap. A promise with a clock on it makes you measure, and the measuring is exactly the churning the practice is meant to wear down. The mechanism is attrition, not suppression. Each throw of the mind out to the dvādaśānta cuts one fluctuation short before it can finish, and the claim is simply that a habit interrupted often enough goes thin. Nothing is being fought. That is what lets this one fit inside an ordinary working day without any of the day being set aside.

Word by word

yathā tathā — in whatever manner (indecl. correlatives); yatra tatra — in whatever place (indecl. correlatives); dvādaśa+ante — in the dvādaśānta (m. loc. sg.); manaḥ — the mind (n. acc. sg.); kṣipet — let him cast, throw (3rd sg. opt., kṣip); prati+kṣaṇam — moment by moment (n. acc. sg., adverbial); kṣīṇa+vṛtteḥ — for one whose mental churning has worn thin (m. gen. sg., bahuvrīhi; kṣīṇa, ppp of kṣi, wasted away + vṛtti, fluctuation); vailakṣaṇyam — an otherness, a state without common mark (n. nom. sg., abstract of vilakṣaṇa, unlike); dinaiḥ — within days (n. inst. pl.); bhavet — arises (3rd sg. opt.)

Open portal 028 in the reader →

029The body burned by time

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 52 · dhāraṇā 29 · Space · śāktopāya · a means of mind · gross → causal

कालाग्निना कालपदाद् उत्थितेन स्वकम् पुरम्

प्लुष्टम् विचिन्तयेद् अन्ते शान्ताभासस् तदा भवेत्

kālāgninā kālapadād utthitena svakam puram
pluṣṭam vicintayed ante śāntābhāsas tadā bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

By the fire of time, risen up from the foot of time, your own city —imagine it burned; then, at the end, one appears as stillness

Translation · colloquial

Picture this city you call your body burned by the fire of time, risen up out of time's own foot;and at the end of the burning, what stands there looks like stillness.

Practice

Sit, and bring to mind kālāgni, the fire of time — the fire that ends a world-age. Let it rise from the foot and climb through your own body, the city you live in, until you have seen the whole of it burned. This is deliberate imagining; stay with it to the end. When nothing of the body-image remains, what appears is stillness — rest there as that. Śivopādhyāya is precise about where the fire starts: the big toe of the right foot, which is where Kālāgnirudra is placed in the old rite of purifying the body, and the fire is that Rudra rather than an impersonal blaze. He also changes the picture in one word. 'Your own city', he says, is your own body as guggulu — the resin burned as incense before a god. Not a city razed: a lump of incense on the coals, which gives its fragrance only while it is being consumed. Anyone who finds this portal frightening should have his image rather than ours. He gives a formula to say while it burns — om, the fire syllables, 'I burn the body', homage — which belongs to his ritual setting more than to the verse, but it tells you the practice was once spoken aloud and not only imagined.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya starts the fire at the big toe of the right foot, where the old rite of purifying the body places Kālāgnirudra — and for him the fire is that Rudra, not an impersonal blaze. His reading of 'your own city' is the thing to carry away: the body is guggulu, the resin burned as incense before a god. Not a city razed but an offering, which gives its fragrance only in being consumed. He also gives the formula spoken while it burns, and files the whole portal as a means of the individual where we file it as a means of energy.

Commentary

Violent imagery in a text that has so far been gentle, and it needs its terms. Kālāgni is the fire at the end of an age, not a personal punishment, and puram — city — is the standing figure for the body as a place one inhabits rather than a thing one is. Burning a city you are living in is a different act from destroying yourself, and the verse depends on the difference. Said plainly: this is a rehearsal for dying, undertaken while there is time to profit from it, and the tradition considers that a kindness. This text was written more than a thousand years ago, before anything like our psychological understanding existed, and it shows here. Be very careful with this one: it can cause or aggravate suicidal ideation or distress, and it can go badly for anyone carrying grief or unhealed trauma. There is no screening here of any kind. It is also not a daily practice — the verse asks for one complete burning, attended to the end, not a habit of imagining oneself destroyed. Proceed with great caution.

Word by word

kāla+agninā — by the fire of time (m. inst. sg.); kāla+padāt — from the foot [or station] of time (m. abl. sg.); utthitena — risen, arisen (ppp, inst. sg., agreeing with kālāgninā); svakam — one's own (n. acc. sg.); puram — city [figure for the body] (n. acc. sg.); pluṣṭam — burnt, scorched (ppp of √pluṣ, n. acc. sg.); vicintayet — one should think out, consider fully (3rd sg. opt. of vi-√cint; the received line reads this as deliberate imagining, and that reading is ours. It comes from what is contemplated — a body already burnt — and from the portal that follows, which names vikalpa as the instrument in the verse itself. The verb alone will not give it); ante — at the end (m. loc. sg.); śānta+ābhāsaḥ — one whose appearance is stillness (bahuvrīhi, m. nom. sg.); tadā — then (adv.); bhavet — one becomes (3rd sg. opt. of √bhū)

Open portal 029 in the reader →

030The whole world burned

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 53 · dhāraṇā 30 · Space · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

एवम् एव जगत् सर्वं दग्धं ध्यात्वा विकल्पतः

अनन्यचेतसः पुंसः पुम्भावः परमो भवेत्

evam eva jagat sarvaṃ dagdhaṃ dhyātvā vikalpataḥ
ananyacetasaḥ puṃsaḥ pumbhāvaḥ paramo bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Just so: meditate the whole world burned by constructing thought;in one whose mind holds nothing else, a person's highest state arises

Translation · colloquial

Now just the same for the whole world: meditate it burned, and let the thought do the burning.In one whose mind holds nothing else, the highest state a person can come to comes up.

Practice

Carry the same fire outward. Picture the entire world burned, in just the way you burned the body — and notice the verse's honesty: the burning is done by vikalpa, the mind's own constructing thought. That is the tool, not a flaw. Let no second image share the mind. Held to one thing only, the highest state of a person arises of itself. Śivopādhyāya says plainly that this is not a new practice: it is the portal before, made firm, and the only difference is how far the fire goes. So do not set it up again from the beginning — continue. He also takes 'all' to mean what stands in the body and what stands outside it together, which is why he can call the two one practice. And on the last line: 'the highest state of a person' he glosses as the Bhairava-nature of the unmeasured knower — the exact opposite of the limited grasper he named in the twenty-third portal. That is the axis the whole stretch is turning on.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya says this is not a new portal but the one before made firm — the same contemplation, and the only difference is that the fire now takes the whole world. 'All', for him, means what stands in the body and what stands outside it, taken together. And 'the highest state of a person' he glosses as the Bhairava-hood of the unmeasured knower, the exact negation of the limited grasper he found in the twenty-third portal. On vikalpataḥ, the word our note makes most of, he says nothing whatever.

Commentary

Vikalpataḥ — by constructing thought. One word here contradicts most of the book, and it is the most interesting thing in the verse. Nearly every other portal wants vikalpa gone. This one hands it the job. The world is to be burned deliberately, in imagination, with the same faculty that spends the rest of its life building the world up, and the burning is not less effective for being made up. Later Kashmir Śaiva writers make this explicit and give it a name, the purified construct: a thought used to undo the habit of thinking. It is worth noticing because it rescues the imaginative practices in this book from the charge of being mere fantasy. They are fantasy. That is what they are for. What they are aimed at is the habit of taking the ordinary construction of a world as something other than fantasy.

Word by word

evam — thus, in this way (adv.); eva — just, precisely (particle); jagat — world (n. acc. sg.); sarvam — whole, all (n. acc. sg.); dagdham — burnt (ppp of √dah, n. acc. sg.); dhyātvā — having meditated (gerund of √dhyai); vikalpataḥ — by means of constructing thought (adverbial ablative of vikalpa); ananya+cetasaḥ — whose mind is on nothing else (bahuvrīhi, m. gen. sg.); puṃsaḥ — of the person (m. gen. sg.); pum+bhāvaḥ — the state of [being] a person (m. nom. sg.); paramaḥ — highest, supreme (m. nom. sg.); bhavet — arises, comes to be (3rd sg. opt. of √bhū)

Open portal 030 in the reader →

031Principles growing subtler

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 54 · dhāraṇā 31 · Space · śāktopāya · a means of mind · gross → causal

स्वदेहे जगतो वापि सूक्ष्मसूक्ष्मतराणि च

तत्त्वानि यानि निलयं ध्यात्वान्ते व्यज्यते परा

svadehe jagato vāpi sūkṣmasūkṣmatarāṇi ca
tattvāni yāni nilayaṃ dhyātvānte vyajyate parā

Translation · literal Sanskrit

In your own body, or in the world as well, subtle and subtler still —the tattvas, whichever they are — meditate them home to rest; at the end she is revealed, the supreme herself

Translation · colloquial

In your own body, or in the world just as well, take the things it is made of, the fine and the finer still,and meditate them all the way home to rest; at the end she shows herself, the highest one.

Practice

Take the tattvas — the constituent principles the tradition counts in body and world — and follow them from gross toward subtle, each subtler than the last. Watch each one settle into the one it rests on. Work in your own body, or in the world, as you please. When the series has run out, at the end, she who is supreme is revealed; you do not produce her. Śivopādhyāya supplies the inventory the verse leaves open, and it is the Sāṃkhya one: prakṛti, the great one, the I-maker and the rest above; earth and the rest below, with flesh and the body's substances as their modifications. He also says what the settling is modelled on — dissolution into their own respective causes, as happens in the state of death. That binds this portal to the two burnings before it. You burned the body, you burned the world, and now you watch what is left return to where it came from, in the order it would take if you were dying. It is the quietest of the three and the most thorough. His gloss on the ending agrees with the verse's own grammar: at the very end the supreme Goddess becomes manifest. Nothing has been achieved.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya supplies the inventory: prakṛti, the great one and the I-maker above, earth and the rest below, with flesh and the body's substances as their modifications. And he says what the settling is modelled on — dissolution into their own respective causes, as in the state of death. That makes this the third movement of a single arc: the body burned, the world burned, and now the constituents returning to their sources in the order dying would take them. He reports a variant on which the principles are contemplated as already dissolved, as good as destroyed.

Commentary

Yāni — whichever they are. The text names the tattvas and then pointedly declines to count them, exactly as it declined to name the five voids and the twelve stations. The Śaiva thirty-six are the obvious frame and this verse fits them well, but it does not require them, and a practitioner who holds some other inventory of what the world is made of may use that one. The instruction is not analysis. It is watching: each principle settling into the one it rests on, gross giving way to subtle, subtle to subtler, until the series runs out of places to rest. What the verse does at the end is what this book almost always does — the verb goes passive. She is revealed. Not attained, not realised, not reached. The same word was used at the second portal for Bhairava's body, and it is the grammar of the whole text.

Word by word

sva+dehe — in one's own body (m. loc. sg.); jagataḥ — of the world (n. gen. sg.); vā — or; api — even, as well; sūkṣma+sūkṣmatarāṇi — subtle and subtler (n. nom./acc. pl., with comparative -tara); ca — and; tattvāni — the tattvas, constituent principles (n. nom./acc. pl.); yāni — which, whichever (n. pl. relative pronoun); nilayam — resting place, [place of] dissolution (m. acc. sg.); dhyātvā — having meditated (gerund of √dhyai); ante — at the end (m. loc. sg.); vyajyate — is made manifest, is revealed (3rd sg. pres. passive of vi-√añj); parā — the supreme [she] (f. nom. sg.)
