032The śakti grown faint

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 55 · dhāraṇā 32 · Awareness · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

पीनां च दुर्बलां शक्तिं ध्यात्वा द्वादशगोचरे

प्रविश्य हृदये ध्यायन् मुक्तः स्वातन्त्र्यम् आप्नुयात्

pīnāṃ ca durbalāṃ śaktiṃ dhyātvā dvādaśagocare
praviśya hṛdaye dhyāyan muktaḥ svātantryam āpnuyāt

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Meditate on the śakti, full and faint, in the range of the twelve;enter the heart and meditate there — freed, one attains self-rule

Translation · colloquial

Meditate on the power, full and then gone faint, out in the space twelve fingers beyond you;then come into the heart and meditate there, and set free, you come into your own rule.

Practice

Attend to the śakti at the range of the twelve, the dvādaśānta. Meditate it first as full and thick, and then let it grow lean — faint, almost failing. Then enter the heart and keep meditating there. What is attained is svātantrya — standing on your own. Śivopādhyāya is unhesitating about what this śakti is: it is the breath. And he reads the two adjectives as a sequence rather than a pair — full at first, and then by degrees becoming subtle — done by the method of prāṇāyāma. So this is a breath practice with an ordinary technique behind it: lengthen and refine the breath out at the twelve-finger point until it is barely there, then bring the meditation into the heart. On the last word he is as strong as the verse: not that one attains autonomy but that one comes to be of the form of the autonomous Lord, which in this school is what Śiva is and not something Śiva has.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya says without hesitation that the śakti here is the breath, and he reads the two adjectives as stages rather than as a pair held together: full and thick at first, then by degrees grown lean — done, he says, by the method of prāṇāyāma. That is a plainer instruction than ours and it is the plainer reading of the Sanskrit. And on the ending he is emphatic: one comes to be of the form of the autonomous Supreme Lord.

Commentary

Attend to the same power both thick and failing. That pair of adjectives is the odd instruction here, and it is not one or the other — full at its height and faint at its guttering, which for anyone taking the breath as the śakti in question means the strong middle of a breath and the almost-nothing at its end. Two conditions of one thing, and the practice lives in holding them as one thing. Then the verse moves you from the periphery to the centre, out at the range of the twelve and then into the heart, which is the direction most of this book's spatial practices run. What is promised at the end is not the usual word. Svātantrya — self-rule, autonomy — is this school's name for Śiva's own defining attribute, the thing that makes him what he is. It is offered here to the practitioner, and that is a larger claim than liberation.

Word by word

pīnām — thick, full (f. acc. sg.; KSTS pīnāṃ, glossed prāṇaśaktim ādau pīnāṃ-pīvarām, ttaḥ krameṇa kṛśāṃ — the e-text's pināṃ is not a word); ca — and; durbalām — weak, faint (f. acc. sg.); śaktim — the śakti, the power [of breath] (f. acc. sg.); dhyātvā — having meditated (gerund of √dhyai); dvādaśa+gocare — in the range of the twelve, the dvādaśānta (m. loc. sg.); praviśya — having entered (gerund of pra-√viś); hṛdaye — in the heart (n. loc. sg.); dhyāyan — meditating (pres. act. participle, m. nom. sg.); muktaḥ — freed (ppp of √muc, m. nom. sg.); svātantryam — self-rule, autonomy (n. acc. sg.); āpnuyāt — one would attain (3rd sg. opt. of √āp)

Open portal 032 in the reader →

033The course of worlds

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 56 · dhāraṇā 33 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · gross → causal

भुवनाध्वादिरूपेण चिन्तयेत् क्रमशो ऽखिलम्

स्थूलसूक्ष्मपरस्थित्या यावद् अन्ते मनोलयः

bhuvanādhvādirūpeṇa cintayet kramaśo 'khilam
sthūlasūkṣmaparasthityā yāvad ante manolayaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Step by step, contemplate the whole of it as the course of worlds and the rest,through the gross, the subtle, the supreme state — until at the end the mind dissolves

Translation · colloquial

Step by step, hold the whole of it as a run of worlds and everything that goes with them,coarse, then fine, then finest, until at the end the mind dissolves.

Practice

Think the whole of things through in order: the course of worlds first — bhuvana-adhvan, the graded path running through every level of manifestation — then whatever follows it. Move step by step, gross to subtle to supreme, skipping nothing. This is sequential, deliberate thinking, and you ride it as far as it goes. Its end is not a conclusion; it is the mind dissolving. Śivopādhyāya gives the map the verse assumes. There are six courses, three of them what is meant and three of them what means it: worlds, principles and portions on one side; words, mantras and syllables on the other. Each earlier one pervades the later as clay pervades a pot; each later one sits inside the earlier as a tree sits in its seed — so working through any one of them works through all six, and that is why the old manuals could take any single course as the whole rite. And he says what the practice actually is, which needs none of the map: dissolve the grosser into the gross, the gross into the subtle, the subtle into the supreme, which is awareness and nothing else. He adds that this makes the portal one of the emptiness contemplations, arrived at by dissolving rather than by voiding. The sentence to carry in is his one caution. In truth, he says, this power flashes with no sequence at all, and then out of sheer freedom displays sequence as well — the way a city appears in a mirror. You go in order because the order is being shown to you, not because things are built in stages. Which is also why the verse ends where it does: the mind dissolves rather than arrives.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya supplies the whole apparatus. Six courses: worlds, principles and portions on the side of what is meant; words, mantras and syllables on the side of what means it. Each earlier pervades the later as clay pervades a pot, each later sits in the earlier as a tree in its seed — which is why purifying one course purifies all six. He also states the practice in a line, and it needs none of the map: dissolve the grosser into the gross, that into the subtle, that into the supreme, which is awareness and nothing else. And he gives the caution that makes the whole thing safe to attempt — in truth this power flashes with no sequence at all, and displays sequence out of its own freedom, the way a city appears in a mirror.

Commentary

The adhvans are the courses: the graded paths running through every level of manifestation. They are a whole genre of Śaiva contemplation, worked out at enormous length in the ritual manuals, and this verse compresses the entire apparatus into a line and a half on the assumption that you already have the map. Most readers now do not, and no honest note can supply it in a paragraph. Of everything here, this is the practice hardest to lift out of its own century. What is portable is the shape: think the whole of things through in order, level by level, gross to subtle to supreme, refusing to skip. Then notice where it ends. Not in understanding, not in a completed system — the mind dissolves. The most scholastic instruction in the book is built to consume the learning it requires.

Word by word

bhuvana+adhva+ādi+rūpeṇa — in the form of the course of worlds and the rest (n. inst. sg.); cintayet — one should contemplate (3rd sg. opt. of √cint); kramaśaḥ — step by step, in sequence (adv.); akhilam — the whole, the entire [universe] (n. acc. sg.); sthūla+sūkṣma+para+sthityā — by way of the gross, subtle, and supreme states (f. inst. sg.); yāvat — until (conj.); ante — at the end (m. loc. sg.); manas+layaḥ — dissolution of the mind (m. nom. sg.)

Open portal 033 in the reader →

034The edges of the universe

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 57 · dhāraṇā 34 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

अस्य सर्वस्य विश्वस्य पर्यन्तेषु समन्ततः

अध्वप्रक्रियया तत्त्वं शैवं ध्यात्वा महोदयः

asya sarvasya viśvasya paryanteṣu samantataḥ
adhvaprakriyayā tattvaṃ śaivaṃ dhyātvā mahodayaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

At the outermost limits of this whole universe, on every side,by the way of the courses, meditate on the reality that is Śiva's — a great dawning

Translation · colloquial

At the far edges of this whole universe, on every side,meditate by that same laying-out on the reality that is Shiva's, and a great dawn comes.

Practice

Go to the edges. Hold this whole universe in attention and reach its outermost limits, on every side at once, using the same graded course as before. At those limits, meditate on what remains there: the reality that is Śiva's. The verse gives no technique past that point — only that a great dawning follows. Stand at the rim and look. Śivopādhyāya reads the limits as the ends of the courses rather than the rim of space: you follow the graded series of the portal before out to where it stops, and what you find at the end of it is that there is nothing of the world apart from Śiva. Then, he says, let the form of the world go and meditate Śiva alone. He also gives the technique the verse seems to withhold, and it needs none of the apparatus. What is signified is light; what signifies it is the awareness of that light; the two rub together like fire-sticks. Meditate the astonishment of feeling both powers at once — the opening and the closing in the same instant. The great arising he glosses simply as the light showing itself, which is a showing-forth of what was already there and not an event that starts.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reads 'the limits' as the ends of the courses, not the rim of space: you follow the graded series out to where it stops and find nothing of the world left over from Śiva — then let the world's form go and meditate Śiva alone. And he gives a technique where the verse seems to give none. What is meant is light, what means it is the awareness of that light, and the two rub together like fire-sticks: meditate the astonishment of experiencing both powers, the opening and the closing, in one and the same moment. The great arising, he says, is simply the light showing itself.

Commentary

The companion to the last, running the other way: not inward through the levels but out to the rim of everything, held on every side at once. It is the same figure the twenty-seventh portal drew twelve finger-breadths off the skin, now at the scale of the universe — a boundary attended from inside. Whether a modern cosmology can be made to hold still at its edges the way this one could is a real question, and the verse does not help with it. What it wants is the reaching, and the reaching is possible in any picture of the world large enough to have a far side. Once you are at the limits there is no further technique, only the instruction to meditate on what is there and the report that a great dawning follows. The verse takes you to the rim and stops.

Word by word

asya — of this (gen. sg. demonstrative); sarvasya — whole, entire (n. gen. sg.); viśvasya — of the universe (n. gen. sg.); paryanteṣu — at the outermost limits (m. loc. pl.); samantataḥ — on every side, all around (adv.); adhva+prakriyayā — by the procedure of the courses (f. inst. sg.); tattvam — the reality (n. acc. sg.); śaivam — belonging to Śiva (n. acc. sg.); dhyātvā — having meditated (gerund of √dhyai; input reads dhyatvā, evidently for dhyātvā); mahā+udayaḥ — great arising, great dawning (m. nom. sg.)

Open portal 034 in the reader →

035The universe become empty

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 58 · dhāraṇā 35 · Space · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

विश्वम् एतन् महादेवि शून्यभूतं विचिन्तयेत्

तत्रैव च मनो लीनं ततस् तल्लयभाजनम्

viśvam etan mahādevi śūnyabhūtaṃ vicintayet
tatraiva ca mano līnaṃ tatas tallayabhājanam

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Imagine this universe, great Goddess, become empty;and the mind dissolved right there: then one is a vessel for absorption into that

Translation · colloquial

Imagine this whole universe, great Goddess, gone empty;and the mind melted right there. Then you are a vessel for melting into that.

Practice

Imagine this universe as having become empty — not each thing removed one by one, but the whole of it, as one act. This one is deliberate construction, and meant to be: the verb is vicintayet, the same one that burns the body six portals back. Then let the mind dissolve right there, inside that emptiness, not watching from anywhere else. Nothing further is done. One who stays so becomes a vessel — a thing shaped to receive absorption into that. Śivopādhyāya adds one word and it raises the bar. 'Become empty' he glosses as having arrived at the void beyond the void — not the universe cleared of its contents, which would still leave the emptiness there to stand in, but an emptiness with nothing under it either. And the dissolving he glosses as becoming of that very nature, which is why the verse does not need to say what happens next. He also marks this portal as the opening of a run: from here, he says, the teaching goes by way of resting on outer emptiness. So it is the first of a group, not a single sudden verse standing alone.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya glosses 'become empty' as having arrived at the void beyond the void — not the universe cleared of its contents, which would leave the emptiness itself to stand in, but an emptiness with no further ground. And he marks this verse as the head of a run: from here, he says, the teaching goes by way of resting on outer emptiness. The dissolving he glosses simply as becoming of that very nature.

Commentary

Set beside the two portals before it, this one is defined by what it refuses. Those went step by step, level by level, skipping nothing. This one asks for the whole universe empty in a single act — no inventory, no sequence, no working through. Whether that is possible for a given person on a given day is exactly the difference between the graded practices and the sudden ones, and the text puts them next to each other without ranking them. The word to sit with is bhājana, vessel. You are not told that you will attain absorption; you are told you become a thing shaped to receive it. That distinction runs quietly through the whole book and it is the difference between practice and result. Almost everything here that a person can actually do is preparation of a vessel. The filling is not on the list of instructions.

Word by word

viśvam — the universe (n. acc. sg.); etat — this (n. acc. sg.); mahā+devi — great Goddess (f. voc. sg.); śūnya+bhūtam — become empty (n. acc. sg.); vicintayet — one should think out, consider fully (3rd sg. opt. of vi-√cint; as at portal 29, the received line's 'imagine' is our reading of what is being contemplated rather than the force of the verb); tatra — there (adv.); eva — precisely, right (particle); ca — and; manaḥ — the mind (n. nom./acc. sg.); līnam — dissolved (ppp of √lī, n. sg.); tataḥ — then, thereupon (adv.); tat+laya+bhājanam — a vessel for absorption into that (n. nom. sg.)

Open portal 035 in the reader →

036Gazing into a jar

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 59 · dhāraṇā 36 · Space · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → subtle

घटादिभाजने दृष्टिम् भित्तीस् त्यक्त्वा विनिक्षिपेत्

तल्लयं तत्क्षणाद् गत्वा तल्लयात् तन्मयो भवेत्

ghaṭādibhājane dṛṣṭim bhittīs tyaktvā vinikṣipet
tallayaṃ tatkṣaṇād gatvā tallayāt tanmayo bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Cast the gaze into a jar or any vessel, the walls dropped;that instant one goes into absorption in it; from that absorption one becomes of its nature

Translation · colloquial

Cast the gaze into a jar, or any vessel, and drop its walls.That instant you go into it, and by going into it you become of its own kind.

Practice

Take a jar, a bowl, any vessel with an inside. Cast your gaze into it and drop the walls: do not look at the clay, look into the contained space. The verse says absorption into that space comes the same instant the walls are let go. From that absorption, you become of one nature with what you look into. Śivopādhyāya gives the criterion instead of a list: anything hollow within will serve. And the 'walls' he reads simply as the sides of the thing — you are letting go of the pot's flanks, not of a piece of architecture. What follows the letting-go he calls, as he has before, a coming to rest rather than a dissolving away; the word means settling, not annihilation. And where the verse says one becomes of that nature, he says one becomes absorbed into the supreme brahman.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya replaces the list with a criterion — anything hollow within will do — and reads 'the walls' as simply the sides of the thing rather than as anything architectural. The dissolving he glosses, as he does elsewhere in these portals, as a coming to rest.

Commentary

Any bowl in the house will do, and the whole instruction fits in a sentence: look into a jar, and drop the walls. It is one of the two or three most testable verses in the book. The vessel is not the object and neither is the clay. The vessel is a device for giving the eye a bounded piece of emptiness to rest on, at which point the boundary is discarded and what is left is the emptiness without an edge — the same move the twenty-fifth portal made with the skin, now with something you can pick up. Two words carry the claim. Tatkṣaṇāt: absorption follows the same instant the walls are let go, not after a period of concentration. And tanmaya, made of that — the working principle stated at the seventeenth portal, applied here to a space you can see across the room.

Word by word

ghaṭa+ādi+bhājane — in a vessel such as a jar (n. loc. sg.; KSTS ghaṭādi- with the retroflex, for the e-text's ghatādi-); dṛṣṭim — the gaze (f. acc. sg.); bhittīḥ — the walls (f. acc. pl.; KSTS bhittīs with long ī, the accusative plural the sense needs, for the e-text's bhittis); tyaktvā — having left aside, dropped (gerund of √tyaj); vinikṣipet — one should cast (3rd sg. opt. of vi-ni-√kṣip); tat+layam — absorption into that (m. acc. sg.); tat+kṣaṇāt — from that very instant (m. abl. sg.); gatvā — having gone (gerund of √gam); tat+layāt — from absorption into that (m. abl. sg.); tat+mayaḥ — made of that, of its nature (m. nom. sg.); bhavet — one becomes (3rd sg. opt. of √bhū)

037Bare hill, blank wall

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 60 · dhāraṇā 37 · Space · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

निर्वृक्षगिरिभित्त्यादिदेशे दृष्टिं विनिक्षिपेत्

विलीने मानसे भावे वृत्तिक्षीणः प्रजायते

nirvṛkṣagiribhittyādideśe dṛṣṭiṃ vinikṣipet
vilīne mānase bhāve vṛttikṣīṇaḥ prajāyate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Cast your gaze on a treeless place, a hill, a wall, or the like;the mental state dissolved, one is born whose turnings of thought have dwindled

Translation · colloquial

Cast the gaze on a treeless place, a hill, a wall, anything of that sort;the state of mind dissolves, and you come out of it with the turning worn away.

Practice

Find a place that gives the eye nothing to work on: a treeless stretch, a bare crag, a blank wall. Set your gaze there and leave it. Do not elaborate; there is nothing to elaborate. Whatever the mind was holding thins and dissolves on its own, and what remains is a mind whose turnings — its vṛttis, its habitual movements — have dwindled. Śivopādhyāya reads the three examples as one thing: an empty quarter, a direction with nothing in it. And he names why it works — the absence of any support. That is worth carrying, because nine portals back the practice was to apprehend that there is no support and not flinch; here the landscape simply takes the support away, and nothing is required of you but to keep looking. He heads this group 'the contemplation that is the binding of the gaze'. On the ending he goes further than the verse: where the mūla stops at a person whose turnings have dwindled, he says such a one comes to be of the form of the great light.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya collapses the three examples into one criterion — an empty quarter, a direction with nothing in it — and names the mechanism: the absence of any support for the eye. He also carries the verse past where it stops. The mūla promises only a person whose turnings of thought have dwindled; he says such a one comes to be of the form of the great light. He heads this group of portals 'the contemplation that is the binding of the gaze'.

Commentary

The wall returns. The tenth portal offered it as one example among many; here it gets a verse of its own, in company with a bare hill and any treeless stretch of ground. All three defeat the eye by giving it nothing to work on. The mind is not being asked to stop. It is being placed somewhere its usual occupation is unavailable, and left to run down. What deserves attention is how modest the promise is. No Śiva, no dawning, no supreme state: what arises is a person whose turnings of thought have dwindled. That is the same word the twenty-eighth portal used, and it is the plainest claim in the collection. A book of a hundred and twelve doors that is willing to say of one of them only that it makes you quieter is a book worth trusting slightly more elsewhere.

Word by word

nir+vṛkṣa+giri+bhitti+ādi+deśe — on a spot such as a treeless [tract], a hill, a wall, and so on (m. loc. sg.); dṛṣṭim — the gaze (f. acc. sg.); vinikṣipet — one should cast (3rd sg. opt. of vi-ni-√kṣip); vilīne — dissolved (ppp of vi-√lī, loc. sg., locative absolute); mānase — mental (m. loc. sg., with bhāve); bhāve — state, [mental] content (m. loc. sg., locative absolute with vilīne); vṛtti+kṣīṇaḥ — one whose turnings [of mind] have dwindled (bahuvrīhi, m. nom. sg.; KSTS -kṣīṇaḥ, glossed kṣīṇasamastavṛttiḥ, for the e-text's -kṣiṇaḥ); prajāyate — comes into being, arises (3rd sg. pres. mid. of pra-√jan)

Open portal 037 in the reader →

038The middle between two things

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 61 · dhāraṇā 38 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

उभयोर् भावयोर् ज्ञाने ध्यात्वा मध्यं समाश्रयेत्

युगपच् च द्वयं त्यक्त्वा मध्ये तत्त्वम् प्रकाशते

ubhayor bhāvayor jñāne dhyātvā madhyaṃ samāśrayet
yugapac ca dvayaṃ tyaktvā madhye tattvam prakāśate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Meditate on the knowing of two things; take hold of the middle between them;drop both at once, and in the middle the reality shines forth

Translation · colloquial

Meditate on the knowing of two things, and take hold of the middle between them;let both go at once, and in the middle what is real shines out.

Practice

Hold two things in awareness at once — any two objects or states will do. Meditate on the knowing in which both of them stand, and settle into the middle between them. Then the move that matters: drop both together, at one stroke, not one after the other. In that middle the reality shines out. You do not construct it. Śivopādhyāya says what the middle is, and it is not a neutral space lying between the two. It is the emptiness by which the two are two — the gap that makes them separate things at all. Which is why any pair will serve: whichever pair you take, the gap is the same gap. And he answers the hard part. Dropping two things simultaneously is difficult to will, and doing it in sequence leaves you holding the second. His gloss on 'dropped at once' is: dismissed by not looking at them. You do not drop them; you cease to regard them, and one act of not-looking covers both because it is aimed at neither. He also reads the settling as a third step after the finding — meditate the middle, actually catch it, and only then resort to it with one-pointedness. He heads this portal 'the contemplation that is without support'.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya defines the middle: not a neutral zone between the two things but the void by which the two are delimited — the emptiness that makes them separate at all, which is why any pair whatever will serve. And he supplies the method for the hard word. 'Dropped both at once' he glosses as dismissed by not looking at them: you do not let go of two things in one act of will, you simply cease to regard them, and a single not-looking covers both because it is aimed at neither.

Commentary

This is the rule of which the opening breath verses were a special case. There it was the two ends of a breath; here it is any two things whatever, and the madhya — the middle — is stated in its general form for the first time. Three moves, and the order matters. Hold two. Find the knowing in which both of them stand, which is already a shift from the objects to what is aware of them. Then drop both, and the word is yugapat, together, at one stroke. Dropping them in sequence does not work, and that is the ordinary failure: let go of the first and you are simply left holding the second, which is not a middle but a new object. What the verse claims for that gap is that it is not a gap. Something shines there — prakāśate, the verb this text keeps for what is not produced by the practitioner.

Word by word

ubhayoḥ — of both (gen. du.); bhāvayoḥ — of the two objects [or states] (m. gen. du.); jñāne — in the knowing, the cognition (n. loc. sg.); dhyātvā — having meditated (gerund of √dhyai); madhyam — the middle (n. acc. sg.); samāśrayet — one should take hold of, resort to (3rd sg. opt. of sam-ā-√śri); yugapat — simultaneously, at once (adv.); ca — and; dvayam — the pair (n. acc. sg.); tyaktvā — having dropped (gerund of √tyaj); madhye — in the middle (n. loc. sg.); tattvam — the reality (n. nom. sg.); prakāśate — shines forth (3rd sg. pres. mid. of pra-√kāś)

Open portal 038 in the reader →

039Not moving to the next

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 62 · dhāraṇā 39 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

भावे न्यक्ते निरुद्धा चिन् नैव भावान्तरं व्रजेत्

तदा तन्मध्यभावेन विकसत्यति भावना

bhāve nyakte niruddhā cin naiva bhāvāntaraṃ vrajet
tadā tanmadhyabhāvena vikasatyati bhāvanā

Translation · literal Sanskrit

When the object has faded from sight, awareness — held back — must go to no other at all;then, through that state of the middle, contemplation unfolds exceedingly

Translation · colloquial

When the thing you were looking at has faded, hold the awareness back and let it go to nothing else at all;then, standing in that middle, the holding opens out beyond measure.

Practice

A form you have been holding in the mind's eye fades. The verse means a deliberate visualisation — Śivopādhyāya names the three-eyed, four-armed one — and the moment it is about is the moment it goes. Do not rebuild it, and do not let attention slide on to whatever offers itself next: not to a body, not to a pot, not to anything seen. Hold it back, exactly where the form was. That is the whole instruction. He is candid that the holding-back takes acquired skill — checked and set in place, he says, by skill got from practice. And he quotes the yoga tradition to explain why an object-meditation is set up only to be dropped: even where meditation has a support, one gives up the meditator and the meditation, because what is really to be reached is rest in a middle that has no object to rest on. The mind then is like a lamp in a windless place. His reading of the last line is the one to carry. What unfolds is not an experience in the sitting. Bhāvanā, he says, is the deposit left by resting in the middle — the trace laid down each time you have stood in a gap and not filled it — and 'unfolds' means that deposit wakes up. What you are doing accumulates.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya names the fading object as the three-eyed, four-armed deity held in visualisation, and is candid that holding awareness back takes skill acquired by practice. He quotes the yoga tradition — the mind like a lamp in a windless place — to explain why a meditation with an object is set up only to be let go: what is to be reached is rest in a middle with nothing to rest on. And he reads the last line unexpectedly. Contemplation, for him, is the deposit left by resting in the middle, and 'unfolds' means that deposit is awakened — not a flowering within the sitting but something accumulating across them.

Commentary

The mirror of the portal before, and much the more available of the two. There you had to arrange two objects and let go of both together; here you arrange nothing. Objects fall away by themselves all day long — a thought finishes, a sight passes, a task ends — and each time there is a fractional interval in which awareness stands unassigned before it takes up the next thing. The instruction is only to decline the next thing. This is the same seam that the celebrated ninety-fifth portal finds in a sneeze and a fright, approached deliberately instead of by ambush. The verse does not say a vision opens, or a state: bhāvanā itself, contemplation, blossoms — the practice turns out to have been a bud, and refusing the next object is what lets it open.

Word by word

bhāve — the object (m. loc. sg., locative absolute; the vivṛti identifies it as the dhyeya-vastu, the thing meditated on, 'in the form of the three-eyed, four-armed one'); nyakte — concealed, gone out of sight (ppp, loc. sg., locative absolute with bhāve; KSTS, glossed antarhite. The Vedabhāṣyakāra gives nyaktam = truṭitam, tirohitam, and Śivopādhyāya marks it a chāndasa usage; the e-text's tyakte 'let go' has no witness here); niruddhā — held back, restrained (ppp of ni-√rudh, f. nom. sg., with cit); cit — awareness (f. nom. sg.); na eva — not at all; bhāva+antaram — another object (n. acc. sg.); vrajet — should go [to] (3rd sg. opt. of √vraj); tadā — then (adv.); tat+madhya+bhāvena — through the state of [being in] the middle of that (m. inst. sg.); vikasati — blossoms, unfolds (3rd sg. pres. of vi-√kas); ati — exceedingly (adv., with vikasati); bhāvanā — contemplation, cultivating awareness (f. nom. sg.)

Open portal 039 in the reader →

040Body and world as awareness

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 63 · dhāraṇā 40 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · gross → causal

सर्वं देहं चिन्मयं हि जगद् वा परिभावयेत्

युगपन् निर्विकल्पेन मनसा परमोदयः

sarvaṃ dehaṃ cinmayaṃ hi jagad vā paribhāvayet
yugapan nirvikalpena manasā paramodayaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Contemplate this whole body, or indeed the world, as made of awareness,all at once, with a mind free of constructing thought — the highest dawning

Translation · colloquial

Hold this whole body, or the world just as well, as made of nothing but awareness,all at once, with a mind that makes nothing, and the highest dawn comes.

Practice

Contemplate this whole body as made of awareness — or the world, if that comes easier. What carries the weight is yugapat, all at once: not limb by limb, not thing by thing, and with a mind free of constructing thought while it does so. Held whole and unconstructed like that, the contemplation is the highest dawning the verse names. Śivopādhyāya gives the body its extent — from the feet to the ends of the hair — and then guards it: in every part, and without sequence. Every part, no order. 'Free of constructing thought' he reads as a positive condition rather than an absence: pervaded by the flashing wonder of consciousness, all around, within and without. And he ends with the shortest instruction in his whole commentary. Behind it he puts a maxim — what shines is not separate from the light, or it could never be something known at all — and then, instead of leaving you with an argument, he turns it into the practice itself: contemplate that the light is shining. Not that the body is awareness, which is a proposition and can only be agreed with. That the shining is shining.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya gives the body its extent — feet to the ends of the hair — and then guards the instruction: in every part, and without sequence. Every part, no order. 'Free of constructing thought' he takes positively, as being pervaded by the flashing wonder of consciousness. And he ends with the shortest instruction in his commentary. Behind it lies a maxim — what shines is not separate from the light, or it could never be known at all — but he does not leave you with the argument. He turns it into the practice: contemplate that the light is shining.

Commentary

This portal and the forty-second are twins, and reading them together is worth more than reading either alone. Both offer the same choice of scale — this body, or the whole world, whichever you can actually hold. Both insist on yugapat, all at once, no touring from part to part. And each names one of the two great predicates this school gives to reality: here it is made of awareness, there it is filled with joy. Cit and ānanda, the two things Śiva is said to be, handed over one at a time as contemplations a person can attempt. The difficulty is not the idea, which is easy to assent to and worth nothing assented to. It is holding a whole body as awareness without the mind starting to explain how that might be true — which is the moment the practice ends and thinking about the practice begins.

Word by word

sarvam — whole, entire (m. acc. sg.); deham — the body (m. acc. sg.); cit+mayam — made of awareness (m. acc. sg.); hi — indeed (emphatic particle); jagat — the world (n. acc. sg.); vā — or; paribhāvayet — one should contemplate all round, thoroughly (3rd sg. opt. caus. of pari-√bhū); yugapat — all at once, simultaneously (adv.); nirvikalpena — free of constructing thought (n. inst. sg., with manasā); manasā — with the mind (n. inst. sg.); parama+udayaḥ — the highest dawning, supreme arising (m. nom. sg.)

041Where the two breaths meet

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 64 · dhāraṇā 41 · Breath · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

वायुद्वयस्य संघट्टाद् अन्तर् वा बहिर् अन्ततः

योगी समत्वविज्ञानसमुद्गमनभाजनम्

vāyudvayasya saṃghaṭṭād antar vā bahir antataḥ
yogī samatvavijñānasamudgamanabhājanam

Translation · literal Sanskrit

From the striking together of the two breaths — within or without, at their end —the yogi is a vessel where the knowledge of sameness rises up

Translation · colloquial

Where the two breaths strike together, inside or outside, at the end of either,the one practising becomes a vessel in which the knowing that all is one comes up.

Practice

Attend to your two breaths — prāṇa moving out, apāna moving in — at the point where they strike together, and at the far end of each: within, where the in-breath dies away, or outside, where the out-breath does. You do not make this meeting happen; it happens at every turning of breath. One who keeps returning there becomes a vessel in which the knowledge of sameness rises. Śivopādhyāya fixes both ends: within is the heart, without is the twelve-finger point — the two stations this whole book runs between, and the same interval he found in the heart's space at the twenty-sixth portal. What he says about the meeting itself is worth having exactly. Difference has not been scored into it, as a stylus has not yet marked the surface; it is bereft of both the going out and the coming in; it is very nearly a void. And what rises is, in his word, evenness of seeing — not a piece of knowledge about sameness but a level regard.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya fixes both ends of 'within or without': within is the heart, without the twelve-finger point. On the meeting itself he is precise and it is all subtraction — difference not yet scored into it, bereft of both the going out and the coming in, very nearly a void. And what rises he calls evenness of seeing, a way of looking rather than a thing known.

Commentary

The breath returns after forty portals, with a new word for the same place. Not a pause now and not a turning: saṃghaṭṭa, a striking-together, two currents meeting hard. Kashmir Śaiva writers make this a technical term for the union of opposites wherever it occurs, and choosing it here recasts what the opening verses treated as an interval into a collision — something happening, not something absent. Nothing has to be arranged. The meeting occurs at every turn of every breath, within and without, whether or not anyone is present for it. What is promised is unusually specific and unusually sober: not bliss, not Śiva, but the knowledge of sameness — an even-ness in which the difference between the two breaths, and then between rather more than that, stops being load-bearing.

Word by word

vāyu+dvayasya — of the pair of breaths (n. gen. sg.); saṃghaṭṭāt — from the striking together, the meeting (m. abl. sg.); antaḥ — within (adv.); vā — or; bahiḥ — outside (adv.); antataḥ — at the end [of their course] (adv.); yogī — the yogi (m. nom. sg.); samatva+vijñāna+samudgamana+bhājanam — a vessel for the rising up of the knowledge of sameness (n. nom. sg.)

Open portal 041 in the reader →

042Filled with your own joy

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 65 · dhāraṇā 42 · Feeling · śāktopāya · a means of mind · gross → causal

सर्वं जगत् स्वदेहं वा स्वानन्दभरितं स्मरेत्

युगपत् स्वामृतेनैव परानन्दमयो भवेत्

sarvaṃ jagat svadehaṃ vā svānandabharitaṃ smaret
yugapat svāmṛtenaiva parānandamayo bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Remember the whole world, or your own body, as filled with your own joy;all at once — by that very nectar of your own — one is made of the highest joy

Translation · colloquial

Remember the whole world, or your own body, as brimming with your own joy;all at once, by that sweetness which is your own, you are made of the highest joy.

Practice

Remember — the verse's word — the whole world, or this body, as filled to the brim with your own joy. Remember, as if recalling something already true, and all at once, not by degrees. The means is your own amṛta, your own nectar; nothing is imported from elsewhere. Filled in that way, you become made of a joy higher still. Śivopādhyāya makes 'your own' exact: it is the joy of the self with objects let go — yours in the precise sense that nothing is being enjoyed. And where the verse asks you to hold a world filled with joy without that costing anything, he answers with the oldest verse in the tradition: that is full, this is full, from the full the full comes forth, and taking the full from the full the full alone remains. His last line gives this portal its own character. Two verses back the world shone as light. Here, he says, the world shines as the congealed sap of consciousness — joy that has thickened and set into shapes. That is what makes remembering the right verb: you are not filling anything, you are recognising what the thing already is.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reads 'your own joy' as the joy of the self with objects let go — yours in the precise sense of having no object. He answers the verse's arithmetic with the oldest verse in the tradition: from the full the full comes forth, and taking the full from the full, the full alone remains. And he sets this portal explicitly against the one two back, quoting its opening words: there the world shone as light; here it shines as the congealed sap of consciousness — joy that has thickened and set into shapes.

Commentary

Smaret — remember. The verb is the verse. Not imagine, not construct, not generate: recall, in the way one recalls something that was true all along and had slipped the mind. That single choice separates this from a mood-manufacturing exercise, and it also sets the standard for doing it honestly, because a remembering that has to be worked up is not a remembering. Twice over the text says the joy is your own — your own joy, your own nectar. Nothing is being drawn down from anywhere, and there is no one to petition. This is the twin of the fortieth portal, awareness there and joy here, and between them they hand over the two things this tradition says reality is. The instruction is not to believe either. It is to remember one of them for as long as you can hold it.

Word by word

sarvam — whole, entire (n. acc. sg.); jagat — the world (n. acc. sg.); sva+deham — one's own body (m. acc. sg.); vā — or; sva+ānanda+bharitam — filled with one's own joy (acc. sg.); smaret — one should remember, bring to mind (3rd sg. opt. of √smṛ); yugapat — all at once (adv.); sva+amṛtena — by one's own nectar (n. inst. sg.); eva — precisely, that very (particle); para+ānanda+mayaḥ — made of the highest joy (m. nom. sg.); bhavet — one becomes (3rd sg. opt. of √bhū)

Open portal 042 in the reader →

043A trick of wonder

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 66 · dhāraṇā 43 · Feeling · śāmbhavopāya · no means · gross → causal

कुहनेन प्रयोगेण सद्य एव मृगेक्षणे

समुदेति महानन्दो येन तत्त्वं प्रकाशते

kuhanena prayogeṇa sadya eva mṛgekṣaṇe
samudeti mahānando yena tattvaṃ prakāśate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

By a conjuror's trick, doe-eyed one — at oncea great joy rises up, and by it reality shines forth

Translation · colloquial

Watch a conjuror do the impossible, doe-eyed one, and at once —a great gladness rises, and by that gladness what is real shows itself.

Practice

Conjure something and enter it, such as a garden that isn't there, and yourself strolling in it — and then, since it is yours to arrange, your body falling away, limb by limb, and then being reformed, and you unharmed throughout. The joy the verse promises is the wonder of it, and what the wonder shows is that you were never the thing that dissolved. Lose your body, and be made whole. Śivopādhyāya is precise about which faculty is being used. His word for the effect is astonishment, and his example runs to a full compound: a magical garden fashioned by a juggler, the flowering trees standing in it, your own self roaming about inside it, your own limbs severed and joined back together. On seeing that, he says, the thought-free state arises that very instant. Wonder does the work, and it does it before anything can be arranged. Kuhana also means tickling, and most modern renderings take it that way — which would make this the one portal needing another person in the room. Our only commentator saw the ambiguity and closed it: not tickling, he says, and then describes the garden. We follow him, and note the road not taken.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya does not read the verse's word as tickling. He reads kuhana as stage magic — the employment of wondrous illusion — and describes it: a garden conjured by a juggler, the flowering trees standing in it, one's own self roaming about within, one's limbs severed and joined again. On seeing that, he says, the thought-free state arises that very instant. What suspends thought here is astonishment.

Commentary

A scripture that tells you to go and watch a conjuring trick. The theatre is real and so is the argument under it: astonishment is one of the few conditions that reliably suspends the mind's running commentary on itself, and it does so instantly and without any credit to the practitioner. Śivopādhyāya's example is worth reading twice, because what he describes is not a card trick — a garden that is not there, and you walking about inside it, and your own limbs coming apart and joining again while you stand there unharmed. What is staged is your own dissolution, and the wonder arrives before the mind can get as far as deciding whether to be frightened. That makes it engineered spontaneity, a contradiction the text is untroubled by; the ninety-fifth portal will collect a whole list of the same kind. The instruction that matters is the one about where to stay: in the wonder, not in the trick. The trick is over in a second. What it opened is not. His reading also removes what would otherwise be this portal's oddity. Read as tickling — the sense the practice note sets out and declines — this would be the only one of the hundred and twelve needing another person in the room. Read as he reads it, it needs no one.

Word by word

kuhanena — by tickling, by a trick or jest (n. instr. sg.); prayogeṇa — by the application, the putting to use (m. instr. sg.; with kuhanena: 'by the use of tickling'); sadyas — at once, on the spot (indecl.; sadya eva in sandhi); eva — emphatic particle, right then; mṛga+īkṣaṇe — O doe-eyed one, whose eyes are a deer's (f. voc. sg., addressing the Goddess); samudeti — rises up, surges (sam-ud-√i, 3rd sg. pres. act.); mahā+ānandaḥ — great joy (m. nom. sg.); yena — by which (m. instr. sg., relative); tattvam — reality, the real (n. nom. sg.); prakāśate — shines forth, becomes manifest (pra-√kāś, 3rd sg. pres. mid.)

Open portal 043 in the reader →

044Tingling like ants

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 67 · dhāraṇā 44 · Body · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

सर्वस्रोतोनिबन्धेन प्राणशक्त्योर्ध्वया शनैः

पिपीलस्पर्शवेलायाम् प्रथते परमं सुखम्

sarvasrotonibandhena prāṇaśaktyordhvayā śanaiḥ
pipīlasparśavelāyām prathate paramaṃ sukham

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Dam all the currents; let the śakti of prāṇa rise, slowly —at the moment of the crawling-ant touch — supreme happiness spreads wide

Translation · colloquial

Dam every current, and let the power in the breath rise, slowly;at the moment of the ant-tingle, the greatest happiness spreads wide.

Practice

Sit and seal the body's openings — ears, eyes, nostrils, mouth, and the two below. Let the śakti of prāṇa rise on its own, slowly, without forcing, from the base upward. At some moment a touch comes, like an ant creeping under the skin. That touch is the appointed moment. Do not scratch it away and do not chase it; let supreme happiness spread out from it. Śivopādhyāya explains what that touch is for, and it is not what a modern reader expects. Yogins use it to take a measurement. At that moment, he writes, they establish: having risen from such a station, today prāṇa has reached that one. The outward reach of the breath is tested in a windless room by the drift of a cotton-wisp; the inward reach is tested by the ant-touch. It is the inner counterpart of the wisp — the instrument by which you learn where you have got to. He closes with the reason any of this belongs in a book of contemplations. Prāṇa made subtle calms the mind; the mind's calm calms prāṇa; and by prāṇa's calm the thought-constructs waste away.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reads 'dam all the currents' as the sealing of six openings — ear, eye, nose, mouth, and the two below — and the natural course of the breath as running from the heart out to twelve fingers beyond the nose and back again, the same two stations he named three portals ago. The ant-touch he treats as an instrument: at that moment, he says, yogins take a measurement of how far prāṇa has risen, as the drift of a cotton-wisp in a windless room measures the breath outside. His closing account of why any of this belongs here: prāṇa made subtle calms the mind, the mind's calm calms prāṇa, and by prāṇa's calm the thought-constructs waste away.

Commentary

The most physically specific report in the book. The crawling-ant sensation is not a metaphor invented here — it is named across yoga literature as a recognisable thing that happens to people who sit still and quiet the senses for long enough, and its familiarity is why the verse can use it as a landmark without describing it further. What the verse does with it is the interesting part. The sensation is not a side effect to be tolerated or a milestone to be logged. It is the door, and the moment of its arrival is the appointed moment. Three cautions belong here. Chasing the tingle guarantees you will manufacture one, and a manufactured one leads nowhere; simply perceive what is there, if anything. Sealing the nostrils and the mouth together is breath retention under another name, whatever else it is called, so the caution at the fourth portal holds here too and the seal comes off before it is too uncomfortable, not after. And śanaiḥ — slowly — is the only word in this book's energetic practices that reads like a safety instruction. Take it as one.

Word by word

sarva — all (in compound); srotas — currents, streams (the flows of the body and senses; in compound); nibandhena — by binding, by damming up (m. instr. sg.); prāṇa+śaktyā — by the śakti of prāṇa, the power of the vital breath (f. instr. sg.); ūrdhvayā — going upward (f. instr. sg., agreeing with śaktyā); śanaiḥ — slowly, gently (indecl.); pipīla+sparśa+velāyām — at the moment of the ant-touch, the sensation as of ants crawling (f. loc. sg.); prathate — spreads, widens out (√prath, 3rd sg. pres. mid.); paramam — supreme, highest (n. nom. sg.); sukham — happiness (n. nom. sg.)

Open portal 044 in the reader →

045Between fire and poison

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 68 · dhāraṇā 45 · Feeling · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle → causal

वह्नेर् विषस्य मध्ये तु चित्तं सुखमयं क्षिपेत्

केवलं वायुपूर्णं वा स्मरानन्देन युज्यते

vahner viṣasya madhye tu cittaṃ sukhamayaṃ kṣipet
kevalaṃ vāyupūrṇaṃ vā smarānandena yujyate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Between the fire and the poison cast the mind made of pleasurealone, or filled with breath — one is joined to the joy of love

Translation · colloquial

Between the fire and the poison, cast the mind that is made of pleasure,cast it alone, or filled with breath, and you are joined to the joy of love.

Practice

When desire is awake and the mind is made of pleasure, cast that mind into the middle — between what the verse names only as the fire and the poison. Cast it bare, or carried on a filled breath, and hold to the middle between the two. There one is joined to the joy of love itself, not to its occasion. Śivopādhyāya heads the verse with the pair the whole Trika turns on: contraction of śakti and expansion of śakti, and their middle. Fire is the resting-place of contraction, at the upper kuṇḍalinī above the brow. Poison is the place of expansion at the lower — and the word is not poison but the root that means to pervade, which is why it names the pole that spreads. Between them is the middle channel, and the mind made of bliss is cast there. He then offers a second reading with measurements, out of the Kaula scriptures: fire is the wind-station named Agni, four fingers above the genitals and below the navel; poison is the station named Viṣa in the middle of the genitals; the pleasure-filled mind is cast between them. As to the manner of doing it, he says only that it is to be done as it was heard — such is the tradition of the yogins. He also gives the reason this run of portals takes desire for its field. The joy of desire, he writes, is higher than the other joys because it causes the forgetting of every object. Not because it is strong. Because nothing else so reliably empties the field.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Our note called this the most locked verse in the book. Śivopādhyāya unlocks it twice. First: fire and poison are the contraction and the expansion of śakti — the upper kuṇḍalinī above the brow and the lower at the base — with the middle channel between them. Poison here is not poison but the root meaning to pervade, which is why it names the pole that spreads. Second, 'or else': fire is the wind-station named Agni, four fingers above the genitals and below the navel; poison the station named Viṣa in the middle of the genitals; and he quotes the Kulāmnāya for the list. The manner of doing it, he says, is done as it was heard — such is the tradition of the yogins. And his reason for the whole erotic run: the joy of desire is higher than other joys because it causes the forgetting of every object.

Commentary

Fire and poison, named and not explained. We had this down as the most locked verse in the book, and the readings proposed since — two centres of the body, two phases of arousal, two named channels — are all reconstructions. Śivopādhyāya unlocks it twice and declines to choose. Fire and poison are the contraction and the expansion of śakti: the upper kuṇḍalinī above the brow and the lower at the base, with the middle channel between them. Poison here is not poison at all but the root meaning to pervade, which is why it names the pole that spreads. Or else — his word — fire is the wind-station Agni, four fingers above the genitals and below the navel, and poison the station Viṣa between them. As for how it is done, he says only that it is done as it was heard. What you can read without any key is the structure, and by now it is familiar: a middle, entered between two things, with the mind cast there rather than led. What is new is the material. This begins a run of portals that take erotic arousal as the field of practice, frankly and without apology, and the last line draws the distinction the whole run depends on: one is joined to the joy of love, not to its occasion. It is the same distinction the text makes about music, and it is harder to keep here.

Word by word

vahneḥ — of fire (m. gen. sg.); viṣasya — of poison (n. gen. sg.); madhye — in the middle, between (n. loc. sg.); tu — and, but (particle); cittam — the mind (n. acc. sg.); sukha+mayam — made of pleasure (adj. n. acc. sg., with cittam); kṣipet — one should cast, throw (√kṣip, 3rd sg. opt.); kevalam — alone, by itself (n. acc. sg. with cittam, or adverbial); vāyu+pūrṇam — filled with breath (adj. n. acc. sg., with cittam); vā — or (particle); smara+ānandena — with the joy of love, of desire (smara: erotic love, also the god of love; m. instr. sg.); yujyate — one is joined, yoked (√yuj, 3rd sg. pres. passive)

046The end of union

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 69 · dhāraṇā 46 · Feeling · śāmbhavopāya · no means · causal

शक्तिसंगमसंक्षुब्धशक्त्यावेशावसानिकम्

यत् सुखम् ब्रह्मतत्त्वस्य तत् सुखं स्वाक्यम् उच्यते

śaktisaṃgamasaṃkṣubdhaśaktyāveśāvasānikam
yat sukham brahmatattvasya tat sukhaṃ svākyam ucyate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

The pleasure that comes at the close of absorption in the śakti churned up by union with the śaktiis the pleasure of brahman's reality — that pleasure is called one's own

Translation · colloquial

The pleasure that comes at the close of being taken into her, churned up by the union itself,is the pleasure of what is ultimately real; and that pleasure is called your own.

Practice

This verse describes what happens, not a technique. In union with a partner — the śakti, the embodiment of energy — arousal deepens into absorption, and at the very close of that absorption a pleasure arrives, as a bell goes on ringing once it has been struck. When it comes, recognise it: this is the pleasure of the reality of brahman, and it is your own. It was never manufactured by the other body. Śivopādhyāya is exact about the standing of the other person. Union, he says, is merely the cause of the bliss being made manifest — the way a lamp is the cause of a pot being seen — since the bliss is one's own already. He quotes for it: creation is not born from the liṅga, nor from the bhaga, nor from the seed; śakti, leaping up as bliss, creates herself by herself. And at the close, he says, the consideration of the duality of woman and man has been let go, and there is resting in one's own self alone. He also quotes the warning, which is the tradition's own and not a modern scruple: embraced by his wife he knows nothing outer and nothing inner — the śruti speaks this as an illustration; a fool takes it for an injunction.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya gives the image the verse withholds: the pleasure at the close has the form of the after-ringing of a bell — still the same event, no longer being struck. He calls union merely the cause of the bliss being made manifest, not of its being produced, and quotes for it: creation is born neither from the liṅga nor the bhaga nor the seed; śakti, leaping up as bliss, creates herself by herself. And against the obvious misuse of the doctrine he quotes a second verse — embraced by his wife he knows nothing outer or inner; the śruti speaks this as an illustration, a fool takes it for an injunction.

Commentary

Not an instruction — a description, and then a recognition. Everything in the verse is happening anyway; the practice is knowing what it is when it comes. The word the whole thing turns on is the last: svākhya, one's own. The claim is that the pleasure arriving at the end of that absorption was never produced by the other body, which was its occasion and not its cause. Two things follow, and they pull opposite ways. It is why the text can call this the pleasure of the reality of brahman without any hedging at all, which shocked later readers and is one reason this literature stayed hidden. And it is a doctrine that can be turned into a licence for using a person as an apparatus. Nothing in this book, which is throughout a conversation between two who love each other, gives any warrant for that.

Word by word

śakti — the śakti, here the partner in union (first member, in compound); saṃgama — union, coming together (in compound); saṃkṣubdha — churned up, aroused (ppp. sam-√kṣubh, in compound); śakti — the energy so aroused (second occurrence, in compound); āveśa — absorption, being taken over (in compound); avasānikam — belonging to the end, occurring at the close (adj. n. nom. sg., qualifying sukham; the whole compound: 'occurring at the end of absorption in the energy churned up by union with the śakti'); yat — which (n. nom. sg., relative); sukham — pleasure (n. nom. sg.); brahma+tattvasya — of the reality of brahman (n. gen. sg.); tat — that (n. nom. sg.); sukham — pleasure (n. nom. sg.); svākyam — one's own (adj. n. nom. sg.; KSTS prints svākyam and Śivopādhyāya derives it — svakam eva svākyam, svaka + ṣyañ in svārtha, vṛddhi and -ya exactly as cāturvarṇya from caturvarṇa. The form is regular, and an earlier conjecture svākhyam was withdrawn on the evidence of the print); ucyate — is called, is said to be (√vac, 3rd sg. pres. passive)

047Pleasure remembered

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 70 · dhāraṇā 47 · Feeling · śāktopāya · a means of mind · gross → subtle

लेहनामन्थनाकोटैः स्त्रीसुखस्य भरात् स्मृतेः

शक्त्यभावे ऽपि देवेशि भवेद् आनन्दसम्प्लवः

lehanāmanthanākoṭaiḥ strīsukhasya bharāt smṛteḥ
śaktyabhāve 'pi deveśi bhaved ānandasamplavaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

From the sheer fullness of the memory of pleasure with a woman — the licking, the churning, the pounding —even with the śakti absent, mistress of the gods, a flood of joy would rise

Translation · colloquial

Out of the sheer weight of remembering pleasure with a woman, the licking, the churning, the pounding,even with no one there at all, mistress of the gods, a flood of joy would rise.

Practice

No partner is needed, and the verse says so. Sit and remember — concretely, without haste — the full intensity of pleasure with a woman: the licking, the churning, the pressing. Śivopādhyāya glosses all three without a flicker of embarrassment: the tasting of the wine of the mouth, which is to say kissing all over; the stirring of the principal limb, or embracing; pressing again and again, or the marks of nails. Let memory do what the body once did. If the recollection is whole, a flood of joy rises even with the partner absent. His account of why it works he attributes to Abhinavagupta. The remembered touch is reflected in the field of that touch — the channel of the middle, the supreme śakti, uncontrived — and being reflected there it stirs the same power, even though no touch has been presented from outside. The surface within registers it either way. And his word for what you do is exact: turning to face it. That bliss, he says, is your own, and it arrives by your turning towards it; the woman is not the cause of it there.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya glosses the three acts without euphemism — the tasting of the wine of the mouth, the stirring of the principal limb, pressing again and again or the marks of nails — and then explains the mechanism on Abhinavagupta's authority, naming him: the touch remembered in its fullness is reflected in the field of that touch, the channel of the middle, and so stirs the same power though nothing has been presented from outside. His word for the practice is turning to face it.

Commentary

The previous verse made a claim; this one runs the experiment. If the joy is genuinely one's own, then it should be reachable with the other person absent — and the verse says it is, provided the remembering is whole. That is the argument, and it is why the Sanskrit is as explicit as it is: three physical acts named without euphemism, because a decorous memory would not have the fullness the verse requires and the demonstration would fail. We have not softened them for the same reason. Two notes on reading it now. The gendered phrasing is the text's own — a male practitioner is assumed, as almost everywhere in this literature, though the questioner throughout is the Goddess — and readers may transpose without loss. And the instruction is memory, not fantasy: what is asked for is recollection of something that happened, in its own weight.

Word by word

lehana — licking (n., in compound); manthana — churning (n., in compound); ākoṭaiḥ — by the pressings, poundings (m. instr. pl.; the three name the acts of love-play, instrumental to the remembering); strī+sukhasya — of the pleasure with a woman (n. gen. sg., dependent on smṛteḥ); bharāt — from the fullness, the sheer intensity (m. abl. sg., ablative of cause: from the fullness of the memory of pleasure with a woman); smṛteḥ — of the memory, the recollection (f. gen. sg., dependent on bharāt); śakti+abhāve — in the absence of the śakti, the partner (m. loc. sg.); api — even (particle); deveśi — O mistress of the gods (f. voc. sg., addressing the Goddess); bhavet — there would arise (√bhū, 3rd sg. opt.); ānanda+samplavaḥ — a flood, an inundation of joy (m. nom. sg.)

048Joy at a reunion

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 71 · dhāraṇā 48 · Feeling · śāktopāya · a means of mind · gross → subtle

आनन्दे महति प्राप्ते दृष्टे वा बान्धवे चिरात्

आनन्दम् उद्गतं ध्यात्वा तल्लयस् तन्मना भवेत्

ānande mahati prāpte dṛṣṭe vā bāndhave cirāt
ānandam udgataṃ dhyātvā tallayas tanmanā bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

When a great joy arrives, or a kinsman is seen after a long time,meditate on the joy welled up — dissolve into it, the mind made of it

Translation · colloquial

When a great joy arrives, or a loved one is seen after a long time,meditate on the joy that has welled up; melt into it, and let the mind be made of it.

Practice

This begins with something that befalls you: a great joy arrives, or you see one of your own people again after long absence. Śivopādhyāya makes both concrete — for a man worn down by poverty, a fortune arriving with no strings on it; or a son or a friend home at last from a long time abroad. At that moment turn the mind to the joy itself as it wells up, not to the money or the person. His instruction is one clause and it is the sharpest statement of the method these portals share: seize only the welling-up itself. Not what the joy is about, not who brought it — only the fact of its rising. Held there, inwardly minded, you come to rest in the joy alone.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya makes both occasions concrete: for a man worn down by poverty, an inexhaustible fortune arriving with no thorn in it; or a son or friend home from a long time abroad. His instruction is one clause — seize only the welling-up itself — and where our text says dissolve, he says come to rest.

Commentary

Seeing one of your own people again after a long absence is not an ascetic's occasion. This is the gentlest of the joy verses, and the most revealing about who the text was written for. There is no monastic requirement anywhere in these hundred and twelve — no renunciation, no withdrawal, no cell — and a book that puts family reunion in the same list as absorption in union and the tasting of food is making an argument about ordinary life by its choice of examples alone. The technique, shared with the four portals around it, is a single redirection: the occasion supplies the energy, and attention turns from the occasion to the state it produced. From the kinsman to the gladness at seeing them. The verse asks you to keep going until there is nothing but the gladness, which is a harder thing to do to a person than to a jar.

Word by word

ānande — joy (m. loc. sg., locative absolute); mahati — great (m. loc. sg., agreeing); prāpte — attained, arrived at (ppp. m. loc. sg., locative absolute); dṛṣṭe — seen (ppp. m. loc. sg., locative absolute); vā — or (particle); bāndhave — a kinsman, one of one's own people (m. loc. sg.); cirāt — after a long time (abl. used adverbially); ānandam — the joy (m. acc. sg.); udgatam — welled up, arisen (ppp. ud-√gam, m. acc. sg.); dhyātvā — having meditated on (gerund, √dhyai); tat+layaḥ — whose dissolution is in that, dissolved into it (bahuvrīhi, m. nom. sg.); tat+manāḥ — whose mind is that (bahuvrīhi, m. nom. sg.); bhavet — one should become (√bhū, 3rd sg. opt.)

049Savoring food and drink

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 72 · dhāraṇā 49 · Feeling · śāktopāya · a means of mind · gross → subtle

जग्धिपानकृतोल्लासरसानन्दविजृम्भणात्

भावयेद् भरितावस्थां महानन्दस् ततो भवेत्

jagdhipānakṛtollāsarasānandavijṛmbhaṇāt
bhāvayed bharitāvasthāṃ mahānandas tato bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

From the blossoming of savor and joy — the delight that eating and drinking bring —dwell on the state filled full — from that, great joy arises

Translation · colloquial

From the opening out of savour and delight that eating and drinking bring,dwell in that state of being filled full, and out of it comes a great joy.

Practice

When you eat or drink, do not hurry past what follows. Śivopādhyāya makes the meal itself plain: whatever bare morsel stops the excess of hunger, water or milk enough to stop thirst. What the verse attends to comes after. In the absence of food, he says, the channels of the body go dry; when food is there, the savour spreads through them, and joy follows on that. The savour he means is not taste but the body's own moistness returning — and the delight is felt as the limbs are felt, the hands and the feet, shining out. Then dwell on what he calls the state of the fullness of your own body. Held and cultivated so, that small satisfaction becomes great joy — for, as the śruti he quotes has it, he verily is savour, and having obtained savour one becomes possessed of bliss. Some, he notes, read the verse as being about wine and the ritual substances. He does not condemn them. He asks a question instead — whether in a state of extreme intoxication there is any contemplation of reality at all — and then says that if there is, such people are blessed and to be saluted, and one should not censure them but bow.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reads the verse's savour as the body's moisture, not its taste: without food the channels go dry, with it the savour spreads through them, and joy follows on that. The state of being filled he makes concrete — the fullness of one's own body — and the food itself minimal, whatever bare morsel stops hunger. Of those who read the verse as being about wine and the ritual substances he asks only whether in a state of extreme intoxication there is any contemplation of reality at all; and adds that if there is, such people are to be saluted and not censured.

Commentary

Taste is the way in, not the practice. The verse names the savour and then moves straight past it to bharitāvasthā, the state of being filled, and that is what you are asked to dwell on. Fullness rather than flavour; the after rather than the during. It is not mindful eating as that is usually taught. Notice what it does not licence. Nothing here recommends seeking out better food. The fullness after plain bread is the same fullness the verse means, and a practice that becomes a reason to eat well has quietly changed its object back to the savour. Read plainly it is the most domestic instruction in the book: a minute at the end of a meal, sitting still, attending to a satisfaction the body produces on its own several times a day and that almost nobody stays for.

Word by word

jagdhi — eating (f., in compound); pāna — drinking (n., in compound); kṛta — produced, brought about (ppp., in compound); ullāsa — delight, upsurge (m., in compound); rasa — savor, taste (m., in compound); ānanda — joy (m., in compound); vijṛmbhaṇāt — from the blossoming, the expanding open (n. abl. sg.; whole compound: 'from the blossoming of the savor and joy whose upsurge is produced by eating and drinking'); bhāvayet — one should contemplate, dwell upon (√bhū caus., 3rd sg. opt.); bharita+avasthām — the state of being filled, fullness (f. acc. sg.); mahā+ānandaḥ — great joy (m. nom. sg.); tataḥ — from that, thence (indecl.); bhavet — arises, comes to be (√bhū, 3rd sg. opt.)

050Absorbed in song

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 73 · dhāraṇā 50 · Sound · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle

गीतादिविषयास्वादासमसौख्यैकतात्मनः

योगिनस् तन्मयत्वेन मनोरूढेस् तदात्मता

gītādiviṣayāsvādāsamasaukhyaikatātmanaḥ
yoginas tanmayatvena manorūḍhes tadātmatā

Translation · literal Sanskrit

For the yogin whose self is one with the matchless happiness of tasting song and the other objects of sense,the mind having climbed into it, made of it — they have that for a self

Translation · colloquial

For one who has gone one with the matchless happiness of tasting song, and everything else the senses taste,the mind having climbed in and been made of it, that is what they have for a self.

Practice

Listen to song, or take up whatever savouring of the senses gives you a happiness nothing else equals. Give yourself to it whole, until self and happiness are one thing. The verse's condition is total: the mind must climb into the song and be made of it. Śivopādhyāya's word for what does the work is not pleasure but wonder — the wonder of the song-sound taken in by the ear, the wonder of seeing an exceedingly beautiful form, the savouring of sweet tastes. What follows from that wonder is a happiness matchless and beyond comparison, and reachable, he says, only in your own experience; no one can tell you from outside whether you have had it. Being made of it he calls absorption into the touch of śakti — the same phrase he uses of what a remembered touch stirs — and from that, identity: the becoming of the nature of supreme brahman.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya's word here is wonder, three times over: the wonder of the song-sound taken in by the ear, the wonder of seeing an exceedingly beautiful form, the savouring of sweet tastes. The happiness that follows he calls matchless and reachable only in one's own experience. Being made of it he names absorption into the touch of śakti — the same phrase he uses of the remembered touch three portals back — and he calls the whole portal śāmbhava, where we have called it śākta.

Commentary

One of a small group of verses that take an ordinary pleasure and treat it as a legitimate door — and the reason they can is stated here in technical terms. The mind does not enjoy the song; the mind climbs into it and becomes it, and identity with the object is what the practice is made of. Sufi sama' is the developed institution of the same argument, and it defends itself the same way. Two cautions the text does not give. The condition is total, and a divided attention that is half enjoying and half watching itself enjoy satisfies none of it. Do not do this while driving, or during anything else that needs your attention for safety. And the door is genuinely a door: the happiness is the occasion, not the destination, and a practice that stops at the occasion is just a good evening out.

Word by word

gīta+ādi — song and the rest (input prints gitādi with short i; in compound); viṣaya — sense objects (in compound); āsvāda — savoring, tasting (in compound); asama — unequalled (in compound); saukhya — happiness (in compound); ekatā — oneness (in compound); ātmanaḥ — whose self (m. gen. sg., closing the bahuvrīhi, agreeing with yoginaḥ: 'whose self is one with the unequalled happiness of savoring song and other objects'); yoginaḥ — of the yogin (m. gen. sg.); tat+mayatvena — through consisting of that, being made of it (n. instr. sg.); manas+rūḍheḥ — from the mind's having climbed into it (manorūḍhi, f. abl. sg.); tad+ātmatā — the state of having that as one's self, identity with that (f. nom. sg.)
