051Where the mind is content

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 74 · dhāraṇā 51 · Feeling · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle

यत्र यत्र मनस् तुष्टिर् मनस् तत्रैव धारयेत्

तत्र तत्र परानन्दस्वरूपं सम्प्रवर्तते

yatra yatra manas tuṣṭir manas tatraiva dhārayet
tatra tatra parānandasvarūpaṃ sampravartate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Wherever the mind finds contentment, hold the mind right therethere, in each such place, the essential form of supreme joy comes forward

Translation · colloquial

Wherever the mind is content, hold the mind right there;and there, in each such place, the highest joy comes forward in its own shape.

Practice

Wherever the mind comes to rest content — the verse sets no limit on the object — hold the mind exactly there. Contentment is the sign that a door stands open, and in each such place, held rather than passed through, the essential form of supreme joy comes forward of itself. Śivopādhyāya is careful about which word is doing the work. Contentment, he says, means gladness of mind, not the agitation of mind the text will speak of later. A thing that stirs you up is not what the verse is talking about; and holding the mind there, he insists, includes restraining it — one must not, failing to restrain it, be thrown by the agitations of thought out of the steadiness one had. His own example is the face of a desirable woman, and he is exact about the condition. What is to be held, so far as one is able, is the recognition: I am Śiva, whose nature is consciousness and bliss, and this is my own display. Held that way, the verse means what it says. Pursued instead in simple bondage to desire, he writes — and here he sets down the whole catalogue of love-making without a trace of squeamishness — one casts oneself into a pit. His standard for anyone not yet free is modest and worth keeping: even on seeing surpassing beauty, become, according to your own capacity, purified and unattached — not defiled.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya does not read this as the permissive verse. Contentment, he says, means gladness of mind and not the agitation of mind spoken of later; and holding the mind there includes restraining it — 'one should restrain the mind' is also fitting, he writes, or else the Vijñāna-bhairava would contradict the Gītā, which he quotes on desire, anger and greed as the threefold gate of hell. His own example is the face of a desirable woman, and his condition is a recognition held while looking: I am Śiva, and this is my own display. Pursued instead in bondage to desire — and he catalogues the acts of love without squeamishness — one casts oneself into a pit. For anyone not yet free his standard is modest: even on seeing surpassing beauty, become, according to your own capacity, purified and unattached, not defiled.

Commentary

After eight instances — a tickle, a union, a memory, a reunion, a meal, a song — comes the rule. Wherever the mind finds contentment, that is a door; hold it there. This is the most permissive sentence in the book and it is also the one most easily turned into an excuse, so the safeguard it does contain deserves weight. The word is tuṣṭi, contentment: the settled state, not excitement and not craving. A thing that leaves you reaching for more of it has not produced tuṣṭi and is not what the verse is talking about, and that single distinction does most of the work of keeping this from being a licence for indolence. The other instruction is the harder one. Do not trade the pleasing thing for what you imagine to be a holier one. The impulse to leave a genuinely contenting object for something more “spiritual” is precisely the impulse this verse forbids.

Word by word

yatra yatra — wherever, in whatever place (indecl., distributive); manas+tuṣṭiḥ — contentment of the mind (f. nom. sg.); manaḥ — the mind (n. acc. sg., second occurrence); tatra eva — exactly there (indecl.); dhārayet — one should hold, keep fixed (√dhṛ caus., 3rd sg. opt.); tatra tatra — there, in each such place (indecl., distributive); para+ānanda+sva+rūpam — the essential form, the own-nature, of supreme joy (n. nom. sg.; svārūpa with metrically long ā for svarūpa); sampravartate — comes forward, sets itself in motion (sam-pra-√vṛt, 3rd sg. pres. mid.)

Open portal 051 in the reader →

052The edge of sleep

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 75 · dhāraṇā 52 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

अनागतायां निद्रायाम् प्रणष्टे बाह्यगोचरे

सावस्था मनसा गम्या परा देवी प्रकाशते

anāgatāyāṃ nidrāyām praṇaṣṭe bāhyagocare
sāvasthā manasā gamyā parā devī prakāśate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

When sleep has not yet come and the outer field of sense has vanished,enter that state with the mind — the supreme Goddess shines forth

Translation · colloquial

Sleep has not yet come, and the outside world has already gone;reach that state with the mind, and the highest Goddess shines out.

Practice

Lie down for sleep and stay attentive. There is a threshold where the outer field has already gone — sounds and sights extinguished — but sleep itself has not yet arrived. The verse says that state is to be reached with the mind: enter it deliberately and hold to neither side. There the supreme Goddess shines forth. Śivopādhyāya describes it as a gap between two coverings. Darkness is coming down over the mind's activity; the five outer objects have already vanished from view; and in the middle stands the one state with nothing covering it. That is why so ordinary a moment can carry so large a claim, and why it does not last — both coverings are moving. His word for how you stay there is worth more than any amount of advice about not falling asleep. The state is to be taken up, he says, by the bracing of your own awareness: not attention aimed at something, but awareness taking hold of itself. And he quotes the Vāsiṣṭha for it — the state that comes into being at the beginning of sleep, at the end of waking; one who dwells upon that state directly enjoys imperishable bliss.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya sets the threshold between two coverings: darkness coming down over the mind's activity, the five outer objects already gone from view, and in the middle the one state with nothing over it. What holds you there he calls the bracing of your own awareness — awareness taking hold of itself rather than of anything else. He quotes the Yogavāsiṣṭha for the same threshold, and calls the portal śāmbhava where we have called it śākta.

Commentary

Everyone crosses this threshold twice a day and almost nobody is present for it: the band where the senses have already shut down and sleep has not yet taken over. The verse asks you to enter it deliberately and stay. The practical difficulty is exact, and worth saying plainly because it will be your entire experience of this practice at first. Attending keeps you awake, and letting go enough to arrive drops you over the edge into sleep. The band is narrow and most attempts end in sleeping, which is the ordinary outcome and not a failure. Later traditions built whole disciplines in this territory, the Tibetan dream and sleep yogas most systematically, and there is no need to suppose any borrowing; the doorway is standard issue. Note the disproportion the verse is content with: the highest claim in its vocabulary attached to the most banal moment of the day.

Word by word

anāgatāyām — not yet come (ppp. an-ā-√gam, f. loc. sg., locative absolute); nidrāyām — sleep (f. loc. sg., locative absolute); praṇaṣṭe — perished, vanished (ppp. pra-√naś, m. loc. sg., locative absolute); bāhya+gocare — the outer field, the range of the senses (m. loc. sg.); sā — that (f. nom. sg.); avasthā — state, condition (f. nom. sg.); manasā — by the mind (n. instr. sg.); gamyā — to be reached, to be entered (gerundive of √gam, f. nom. sg.); parā — supreme (f. nom. sg.); devī — the Goddess (f. nom. sg.); prakāśate — shines forth (pra-√kāś, 3rd sg. pres. mid.)

053Dappled light in space

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 76 · dhāraṇā 53 · Light · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → subtle

तेजसा सूर्यदीपादेर् आकाशे शवलीकृते

दृष्टिर् निवेश्या तत्रैव स्वात्मरूपम् प्रकाशते

tejasā sūryadīpāder ākāśe śavalīkṛte
dṛṣṭir niveśyā tatraiva svātmarūpam prakāśate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

When space is dappled by the light of the sun, a lamp, or the like,settle the gaze right there — the form of one's own self shines forth

Translation · colloquial

When the air is dappled by the light of the sun, or a lamp, or anything like them,settle the gaze right there, and the shape of your own self shines out.

Practice

Find a place where light breaks space into patches — brightness scattered through shadow, dappled air. Śivopādhyāya offers three: by day, the open space above and below mottled by sunlight; at night, the great space dappled by moonlight, both within the moon's orb and outside it; or indoors, one part of a room filled by the halo of a lamp and variegated by its flame. Settle your gaze right there, on the mottled space itself and not on the source of the light. Keep the eyes unblinking and let looking become the whole of what you do. In that dappled space the form of your own self shines out — and what one sees, he says, is the whole world-born mass in the shape of a solid mass of consciousness. He knows this practice has a reputation. Patañjali promises that saṃyama on the sun yields knowledge of the worlds, and on the moon knowledge of the array of the stars, and he quotes both. Then he sets them aside: even during that concentration, the shining forth of your own form is simply your own nature becoming one with the space of consciousness, and that is the principal fruit, being the most worth having. Knowledge of the worlds is a by-fruit.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya gives three settings — daylight mottling the open air, a lamp's halo filling part of a room, and, best of all, moonlight dappling the great space within the moon's orb and outside it. He quotes Patañjali's promise that saṃyama on the sun yields knowledge of the worlds and on the moon knowledge of the stars, and then sets it aside: becoming one with the space of consciousness is the principal fruit, and knowledge of the worlds only a by-fruit of the concentration.

Commentary

Dappled light is the visual equivalent of the not-quite-object: patterned enough to hold the eye, too meaningless to interpret. That is what makes it work where a picture would not. The instruction is careful about where to look — at the mottled space, not at the sun or the lamp doing the mottling — and the difference is the difference between a practice and staring at a light, which will only hurt you. Leaf-shadow moving on a wall will do it, and costs nothing. What shines forth is named more intimately here than almost anywhere else in the book: not Bhairava, not the Goddess, not reality, but the form of your own self — the same becoming-what-you-attend-to that the text has been building on since the seventeenth portal, turned now to something you can find on an ordinary afternoon.

Word by word

tejasā — by the light, the brilliance (n. instr. sg.); sūrya+dīpa+ādeḥ — of the sun, a lamp, and the like (m. gen. sg.); ākāśe — space (n. loc. sg., locative absolute); śavalīkṛte — made dappled, mottled, variegated (ppp. of śavalī-√kṛ, n. loc. sg., locative absolute; KSTS prints śavala-, the older form of śabala, and the vivṛti uses it in its own prose — candrajyotsnayā śavale); dṛṣṭiḥ — the gaze (f. nom. sg.); niveśyā — to be settled, made to enter (gerundive of ni-√viś caus., f. nom. sg.); tatra eva — right there (indecl.); sva+ātma+rūpam — the form of one's own self (n. nom. sg.); prakāśate — shines forth (pra-√kāś, 3rd sg. pres. mid.)

054Mudrā and the gaze

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 77 · dhāraṇā 54 · Body · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

करङ्किण्या क्रोधनया भैरव्या लेलिहानया

खेचर्या दृष्टिकाले च परावाप्तिः प्रकाशते

karaṅkiṇyā krodhanayā bhairavyā lelihānayā
khecaryā dṛṣṭikāle ca parāvāptiḥ prakāśate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

With karaṅkiṇī; with krodhanā; with bhairavī; with lelihānā;with khecarī — at the moment of the gaze, the supreme attainment shines forth

Translation · colloquial

With the seal of the skeleton, with the wrathful one, with Bhairavi, with the one who licks,with the walker in the sky: at the moment of the gaze, the highest arrival shines out.

Practice

This verse presupposes five mudrās — karaṅkiṇī, krodhanā, bhairavī, lelihānā, khecarī. A mudrā here is a seal: a whole-body attitude, gaze included, that stamps consciousness with its shape. Śivopādhyāya glosses all five, and read together they describe five colourings of a single act of looking. Karaṅkiṇī sees the universe as a bare skeleton, motionless, like a corpse. Krodhanā is intent with wrath. Bhairavī is the naked power of seeing. Lelihānā is one-pointed to devour, tasting everything again and again in fullness. Khecarī is the gaze stretched far out for the sake of beholding space, moving always in the open. He derives the names again from what each one does: from experiencing the body as a skeleton, karaṅkiṇī; from dissolving the traces one is made of, lelihānā; from standing uncovered in the sky of awareness, khecarī. Then the verse's own contribution, which is exact: the supreme attainment shines at the moment of the gaze — not during the holding, at the moment of looking. He is careful about where this comes from. What he has written, he says at the end, is the instruction of the tradition of the Siddhas and the Yoginīs. These are seals received by being shown. If one of them is yours, the verse tells you when it opens. If none is, you now know what they are, which is not the same as holding one.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya spends four plates here — the longest comment in the book — and glosses all five mudrās three separate times: plainly, then by assigning each to a class of adepts, then by deriving each name from what it does. He closes by naming his source as the tradition of the Siddhas and Yoginīs, and sends anyone who wants more to Abhinavagupta's Vivaraṇa.

Commentary

Five seals named and none of them explained. That is what the verse does, and it is no longer what this edition can say about the tradition. Śivopādhyāya glosses all five three times over: plainly, then by assigning each to a class of adepts, then by deriving each name from what it does. Read together the glosses converge on something usable. These are not five postures but five colourings of a single act of looking: the universe seen as a bare skeleton, motionless as a corpse; intentness with wrath; the naked power of seeing; a one-pointedness that devours, tasting everything again and again; and the gaze stretched out into open space and moving there. That makes the verse's own contribution exact, and it is a matter of timing — the supreme attainment shines at the moment of the gaze, not during the holding. What he does not claim is that any of it can be had off a page. Such is the instruction of the tradition of the Siddhas and the Yoginīs, he writes, and he sends anyone wanting more to Abhinavagupta. A mudrā of this kind is a whole-body attitude transmitted by being shown, and a description read alone yields a posture rather than a seal. So the descriptions are here, on his warrant. Holding one is still a separate matter.

Word by word

karaṅkiṇyā — by karaṅkiṇī, 'she of the skull' (f. instr. sg.; name of a mudrā); krodhanayā — by krodhanā, 'the wrathful' (f. instr. sg.; a mudrā); bhairavyā — by bhairavī, the consort-form of Bhairava (f. instr. sg.; a mudrā); lelihānayā — by lelihānā, 'the ever-licking' (intensive part. of √lih used as a name; f. instr. sg.; a mudrā); khecaryā — by khecarī, 'she who moves in the sky' (f. instr. sg.; a mudrā); dṛṣṭi+kāle — at the time of the gaze, the moment of seeing (m. loc. sg.); ca — and (particle); parā+avāptiḥ — the supreme attainment (f. nom. sg.); prakāśate — shines forth (pra-√kāś, 3rd sg. pres. mid.)

055Sitting on one buttock

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 78 · dhāraṇā 55 · Body · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → subtle

मृद्वासने स्फिजैकेन हस्तपादौ निराश्रयम्

निधाय तत्प्रसङ्गेन परा पूर्णा मतिर् भवेत्

mṛdvāsane sphijaikena hastapādau nirāśrayam
nidhāya tatprasaṅgena parā pūrṇā matir bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

On a soft seat, on one buttock, hands and feetset down on no support — by dwelling on that, awareness becomes supreme and full

Translation · colloquial

On a soft seat, resting on one buttock, with the hands and the feetset down on nothing at all: keep at that, and what you know of yourself goes highest and full.

Practice

Take a soft seat — cloth or something like it, and genuinely soft. Settle your weight onto one hip alone, and set your hands and feet down so that nothing supports them; let them rest on air. The body is now lightly off balance and unpropped, and must keep making small corrections it cannot hand over to habit. Śivopādhyāya adds what to do there, and it is not nothing. From that heightened condition, let awareness quicken through all the limbs, portion by portion, until it covers the whole body. As it goes, he says, the mind's darkness and restlessness melt off and what remains is sattva — clear, light, unagitated. That is why the seat is soft. A hard one would recruit exactly the discomfort the practice is designed to melt away. Through dwelling in it, awareness becomes full and supreme of itself.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya supplies the object the verse withholds: from the heightened condition of that unpropped posture, awareness is quickened through all the limbs, portion by portion, until darkness and restlessness melt off and the mind is left sattvic. He also treats the single hip as characterising the posture rather than defining it.

Commentary

The verse names no object at all: no breath, no sound, no image, no emptiness to contemplate — only an arrangement of the body and the instruction to stay in it. Śivopādhyāya supplies what it withholds, and his answer is worth having: out of the heightened condition of that unpropped posture, awareness quickens through the limbs portion by portion until dullness and restlessness melt off. Ours is smaller and does not contradict his. What the arrangement does is remove settling — weight on one side, hands and feet on nothing, so the body is very slightly off balance and must keep making corrections it cannot delegate to habit. You can neither forget the body nor get comfortable in it, and between those two failures is a narrow band of continuous unforced attention. Note the first two words. A soft seat: the text specifies comfort in the same breath that it removes support, and that is characteristic of the whole collection. Nothing in these hundred and twelve is austerity. Difficulty appears only where difficulty is doing a job.

Word by word

mṛdu+āsane — on a soft seat (n. loc. sg.); sphijā — with the buttock (sphij, f. instr. sg.); ekena — one (instr. sg., with sphijā); hasta+pādau — hands and feet (dvandva, acc. dual); nirāśrayam — without support, resting on nothing (adverbial acc.); nidhāya — having set down, placed (gerund, ni-√dhā); tat+prasaṅgena — through that engagement, by dwelling on that (m. instr. sg.); parā — supreme (f. nom. sg., predicative); pūrṇā — full (f. nom. sg., predicative); matiḥ — awareness, the mind (f. nom. sg.); bhavet — becomes, arises (√bhū, 3rd sg. opt.)

056The hollow of the armpit

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 79 · dhāraṇā 56 · Space · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

उपविश्यासने सम्यग् बाहू कृत्वार्धकुञ्चितौ

कक्षव्योम्नि मनः कुर्वन् शमम् आयाति तल्लयात्

upaviśyāsane samyag bāhū kṛtvārdhakuñcitau
kakṣavyomni manaḥ kurvan śamam āyāti tallayāt

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Sit down well on a seat, the two arms held half-bent,place the mind in the sky of the armpit — through its dissolving there, one comes to stillness

Translation · colloquial

Sit down properly on a seat, both arms held half bent,and put the mind in the open space of the armpit; it dissolves there, and you come to stillness.

Practice

Sit down well on your seat. Hold both arms half-bent, curved away from the body so that a hollow of empty space opens beneath each armpit. Śivopādhyāya reads the sky of the armpit as the space of both armpits together — not one pocket but a single emptiness spanning the body, held open by the two arms. Place the mind there — attend to the empty space itself, not to the arms that frame it. As the mind dissolves into that space, stillness comes. The peace arrives through the dissolving, not through effort.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya gives this portal two words. The one that matters: the sky of the armpit is the space of both armpits — a single field, not a pocket under one arm.

Commentary

The sky of the armpit. This book keeps finding sky in absurdly small places — inside a jar, inside the skull, inside the heart, and now in the hollow under a bent arm — and the joke is also the argument. Space does not get grander by being large, and a practice on the emptiness beneath your own arm is not a lesser version of a practice on the emptiness above the world. The arms are the frame, not the object. Half-bent is enough to open the hollow, and no more is asked of them. What the verse promises is modest and mechanical, in the register of the thirty-seventh portal: not a dawning but śama, stillness, arriving through the dissolving rather than through any effort of yours. And nobody watching would notice you doing it.

Word by word

upaviśya — having sat down (gerund, upa-√viś); āsane — on a seat (n. loc. sg.); samyak — properly, well (indecl., with upaviśya); bāhū — the two arms (m. acc. dual); kṛtvā — having made, holding (gerund, √kṛ); ardha+kuñcitau — half-bent (adj. m. acc. dual, with bāhū); kakṣa+vyomni — in the sky of the armpit, the hollow space beneath the arm (n. loc. sg.); manaḥ — the mind (n. acc. sg.); kurvan — making, placing (pres. act. part. of √kṛ, m. nom. sg.); śamam — peace, stillness (m. acc. sg.); āyāti — one comes to, attains (ā-√yā, 3rd sg. pres. act.); tat+layāt — through dissolution into that (m. abl. sg.)

057A gaze held motionless

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 80 · dhāraṇā 57 · Body · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → subtle

स्थूलरूपस्य भावस्य स्तब्धां दृष्टिं निपात्य च

अचिरेण निराधारं मनः कृत्वा शिवं व्रजेत्

sthūlarūpasya bhāvasya stabdhāṃ dṛṣṭiṃ nipātya ca
acireṇa nirādhāraṃ manaḥ kṛtvā śivaṃ vrajet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Let the gaze fall, frozen, on some thing of gross form;before long — the mind made supportless — one goes to Śiva

Translation · colloquial

Let the gaze fall and go still on some solid thing;before long, with the mind left standing on nothing, you go to Shiva.

Practice

Choose one solid thing — a pot, a stone, a wall; Śivopādhyāya's second example is a body. Let your gaze fall on it and freeze there: he glosses 'frozen' as neither opening nor closing the eyes. No blinking, no roving, no naming what you see. You are not studying the object; you are letting the gaze go rigid. He reads one more instruction out of the verse's little word 'and': while the eyes rest on the gross object, the seeing is also to be aimed inward. Outward and inward at once — which is why he heads this portal with the name of the mudrā rather than with a stage of practice. The mind, given nothing to do with what the eyes hold, loses its footing. Supportless, he says, means vikalpa done away with: the naming, comparing, judging traffic simply stops. Before long the mind stands without support, and that standing is the going to Śiva.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya heads this the bhairavī ground — a fourth register alongside his śāmbhavī, śāktī and āṇavī — and reads the verse's stray 'and' as adding that the gaze, while frozen on the gross object, is also aimed inward. Supportless he glosses as vikalpa done away with.

Commentary

The frozen gaze is old and widely practised — the later manuals call it trāṭaka — and this verse is precise about what ruins it. Not blinking is the easy part. What matters is that you are not studying the object: the moment the mind begins naming what the eyes hold, or noticing its texture, or admiring it, the practice has been replaced by looking at something. One caution, because we named this portal among the risky ones and owe it the note: never hold the gaze on the sun, on a close flame, or on any bright source, and let the eyes water and blink when they insist — a held gaze is not a contest with the eyelid, and forcing it past the point of strain buys nothing the practice wants. A candle at a safe distance is the usual object, with the ordinary care any open flame asks for. The eyes stay busy while the faculty that makes sense of what they see is given nothing to do, and it is that faculty losing its footing which the verse calls making the mind supportless. Nirādhāra is the word, and it returns two portals later. So the shape of it: a gross, solid, entirely ordinary object used as the means to a mind resting on nothing. The support is what gets you to supportlessness.

Word by word

sthūla+rūpasya — of gross form (gen. sg., qualifying bhāvasya); bhāvasya — of a thing, an existent object (m. gen. sg.); stabdhām — frozen, motionless (f. acc. sg.); dṛṣṭim — gaze (f. acc. sg.); nipātya — having made [it] fall upon (gerund, caus. of ni-√pat); ca — and; acireṇa — before long (instr. sg. as adverb); nirādhāram — without support (n. acc. sg.); manaḥ — the mind (n. acc. sg.); kṛtvā — having made (gerund of √kṛ); śivam — Śiva (m. acc. sg.); vrajet — one goes to (opt. 3rd sg. of √vraj)

Open portal 057 in the reader →

058Ha in the open mouth

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 81 · dhāraṇā 58 · Sound · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle → causal

मध्यजिह्वे स्फारितास्ये मध्ये निक्षिप्य चेतनाम्

होच्चारं मनसा कुर्वंस् ततः शान्ते प्रलीयते

madhyajihve sphāritāsye madhye nikṣipya cetanām
hoccāraṃ manasā kurvaṃs tataḥ śānte pralīyate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Tongue at the middle, the mouth thrown wide — cast awareness into the centresounding ha with the mind — then, into the quiet, one dissolves

Translation · colloquial

Tongue held at the middle, the mouth thrown wide, cast your awareness into the centre;sound ha in the mind, and then you dissolve into the quiet.

Practice

Open the mouth wide. Let the tongue rest in the middle, free of palate and teeth. Drop your attention into that open hollow. There, sound ha — with the mind only, no voice, no pushed breath. Śivopādhyāya is exact about which sound: the bare letter ha with no vowel attached to it. Not 'ha' said quietly, but the aspirate alone, with nothing to carry it. Try it and you will find it is a different act from saying a syllable inwardly. He also explains why this letter. The breath already carries a mantra of its own — haṃ going out, saḥ coming in; by sa it goes out, by ha it enters again. In this posture the outward sa has nowhere to go, and what remains to be sounded is ha alone. (There is a second reason worth knowing: ha is the last sound of the Sanskrit alphabet, which in this school runs from a to ha as reality runs from Śiva to Śakti. To sound ha is to sound the far end of manifestation.) Keep the mouth open, the tongue free, the silent vowelless ha sounding. When even that settles, dissolve into the quiet it leaves behind. As in the sixteenth portal, the practice is an ending — except that here the sound was never audible in the first place.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya specifies the sound exactly: the bare letter ha with no vowel attached. He explains the choice from the breath's own mantra — haṃ out, saḥ in — noting that in this posture the outward sa cannot go, so ha alone is left to sound. He reads the whole portal as khecarī mudrā, with the tongue turned back into the hollow of the skull and the gaze fixed between the brows, quoting the Vivekamārtaṇḍa for the definition; we have kept the plainer reading the verse's own words give.

Commentary

Mouth wide, tongue hanging free of palate and teeth: the arrangement makes cheating impossible. There is nowhere to swallow the sound and nothing to shape it with, so the ha has to be made in the mind alone while the body holds an open hollow that would let a real one out. The letter is not arbitrary. Ha is the last sound of the Sanskrit alphabet and the sound the breath makes leaving the body, and in this school's phonetic theology the alphabet runs from a to ha as reality runs from Śiva to Śakti — so to sound ha is to sound the far end of manifestation, the outbreath of the world. Then it stops, and what it leaves behind is the object. As in the sixteenth portal, the practice is an ending. Here the sound was never audible in the first place.

Word by word

madhya+jihve — [the mouth] with the tongue in the middle (loc. sg., loc. absolute); sphārita+āsye — with the mouth spread wide open (loc. sg., loc. absolute); madhye — into the centre (loc. sg.); nikṣipya — having cast (gerund of ni-√kṣip); cetanām — awareness (f. acc. sg.); ha+uccāram — the utterance of ha (m. acc. sg.); manasā — with the mind (n. instr. sg.); kurvan — making (pres. part. m. nom. sg. of √kṛ); tataḥ — then; śānte — in the stilled, the quiet (loc. sg.); pralīyate — one dissolves (pres. 3rd sg. ātmanepada of pra-√lī)

Open portal 058 in the reader →

059The body without support

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 82 · dhāraṇā 59 · Body · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

आसने शयने स्थित्वा निराधारं विभावयन्

स्वदेहं मनसि क्षीणे क्षणात् क्षीणाशयो भवेत्

āsane śayane sthitvā nirādhāraṃ vibhāvayan
svadehaṃ manasi kṣīṇe kṣaṇāt kṣīṇāśayo bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Settled on a seat or a bed, contemplate as supportlessyour own body — as the mind wears thin, in an instant the stored leanings wear out

Translation · colloquial

Settled on a seat, or lying on a bed, hold your own body as resting on nothing;and as the mind wears thin, in an instant what you have been storing up wears out.

Practice

Sit on a seat, or lie down on a bed. Śivopādhyāya lingers on the surface — hide, silk, cotton, velvet, cool smooth cloth — because for him the softness is not scenery. At the very moment of contact, he says, a condition made of bliss wells up and the mind wears thin. Let that happen. Then contemplate your own body as having nothing beneath it — no bed, no floor: hanging in nothing. He marks the step that makes this work: even the felt sense of your own body melts away, the touch-awareness by which you know you have one, and it is after that melting that the form you consider is genuinely free of any support. The mind, finding no ground under the body, fades. Let it fade. In that instant, the verse says, the mind's old stored leanings are worn away with it — and he glosses it flatly: at once, free of latent traces. That last claim is the largest in the book. Set against experience it is hard to credit: what a strong sitting reliably does is suspend the store, not empty it, as anyone who has come out of a deep retreat and met their family can report. A state opened is not a structure changed, and reading a verse like this as a promise of permanent alteration has wrecked more practitioners than any of the difficult techniques have. We record that Śivopādhyāya does not share this caution — he takes the verse at its word. The contemplation itself is excellent either way.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya makes the soft surface causal rather than incidental: at the very moment of contact a bliss-condition wells up and the mind wears thin. He names the step our note leaves out — even the felt sense of the body melts — and then glosses the verse's largest claim without softening it at all: at once, free of latent traces.

Commentary

The claim at the end of this verse is the largest in the book. Āśaya is the store of latent dispositions — the sediment of a life, the tendencies that reassert themselves the moment attention lapses — and the verse says it is worn out kṣaṇāt, in an instant. Śivopādhyāya does not soften it: free of latent traces. Set against a life of practice the claim needs one distinction, between what a sitting does and what a lifetime does. A strong sitting reliably suspends the store. It does not empty it, as anyone who has come out of a long retreat and met their family can report. Over years the suspension comes easier and becomes something close to second nature, and the store does thin — but it is still there, and this is what gets called, harshly, killing the ego. So take free of latent traces as the end state, the small self finished and the Self standing permanently revealed, and not as the report of an afternoon. What the verse describes exactly is the instant in which the store is not operating. That is real and it is available now. Until the rest arrives, Bhairava is in the background, more or less present in the foreground, and that alternation is its own instruction.

Word by word

āsane — on a seat (n. loc. sg.); śayane — on a bed (n. loc. sg.); sthitvā — having settled (gerund of √sthā); nirādhāram — supportless (m. acc. sg., with svadeham); vibhāvayan — contemplating, making vividly present (pres. part. caus. m. nom. sg. of vi-√bhū); sva+deham — one's own body (m. acc. sg.); manasi — the mind (n. loc. sg., loc. absolute); kṣīṇe — faded, wasted away (loc. sg., loc. absolute; text reads kṣiṇe); kṣaṇāt — in an instant (m. abl. sg.); kṣīṇa+āśayaḥ — one whose store of latent dispositions is worn out (m. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi); bhavet — one becomes (opt. 3rd sg. of √bhū)

060The slowly rocking body

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 83 · dhāraṇā 60 · Body · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → subtle

चलासने स्थितस्याथ शनैर् वा देहचालनात्

प्रशान्ते मानसे भावे देवि दिव्यौघम् आप्नुयात्

calāsane sthitasyātha śanair vā dehacālanāt
praśānte mānase bhāve devi divyaugham āpnuyāt

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Now settled on a moving seat, or through the slow sway of the bodywhen the mind's state is wholly stilled, Goddess, one attains the divine flood

Translation · colloquial

Now settled on something that moves, or simply swaying the body slowly,when the state of mind has gone wholly quiet, Goddess, a flood comes that is not of this world.

Practice

Ride something that sways. Śivopādhyāya's examples are not gentle ones — a cart, an elephant, a horse — and he calls the seat very unsteady: motion you are given rather than motion you supply. Or, seated still, rock the body yourself, slowly, and keep it slow. Do nothing else; let the motion be the whole of it. Thought is lulled by the swaying and the state of the mind settles toward calm. The flood is not something you fetch — when the calm is complete, it comes. What comes, he says, is the flowing stream of the bliss of consciousness belonging to the supreme space: the same open space the sky portals work with, arriving here by way of a moving seat.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya wants a genuinely lurching seat — his examples are a cart, an elephant, a horse — and glosses the divine flood as the flowing stream of the bliss of consciousness belonging to the supreme space, which puts this portal next to the sky practices rather than on its own.

Commentary

Every infant is rocked and every long cart ride does this to its passengers. The verse simply asks you to stay awake for it. Slow, repetitive, unwilled motion gives the mind a rhythm it cannot argue with and nothing to do, and thought subsides without being suppressed — which is why this is one of the few portals here that will work on somebody who has never meditated and does not intend to. It permits either a seat that moves or a body that moves itself, and śanaiḥ, slowly, is doing the same work it did at the forty-fourth portal: too fast and this becomes stimulation instead. The last line is careful. The flood is not attained by the rocking. It comes when the calm is complete, and the rocking only completes the calm.

Word by word

cala+āsane — on a moving seat (n. loc. sg.); sthitasya — of one settled (pres. part. gen. m. sg. of √sthā); atha — now, or again; śanaiḥ — slowly (instr. pl. as adverb); vā — or; deha+cālanāt — from a moving, a swaying, of the body (n. abl. sg.); praśānte — fully calmed (loc. sg., loc. absolute); mānase — mental (loc. sg., loc. absolute); bhāve — state, condition (m. loc. sg., loc. absolute); devi — O Goddess (f. voc. sg.); divya+ogham — the divine flood, stream (m. acc. sg.); āpnuyāt — one would attain (opt. 3rd sg. of √āp)

061Gazing into clear sky

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 84 · dhāraṇā 61 · Space · śāmbhavopāya · no means · causal

आकाशं विमलम् पश्यन् कृत्वा दृष्टिं निरन्तराम्

स्तब्धात्मा तत्क्षणाद् देवि भैरवं वपुर् आप्नुयात्

ākāśaṃ vimalam paśyan kṛtvā dṛṣṭiṃ nirantarām
stabdhātmā tatkṣaṇād devi bhairavaṃ vapur āpnuyāt

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Gazing on the stainless sky, the gaze unbrokenthe whole self stilled — and that instant, Goddess, one wears Bhairava's body

Translation · colloquial

Gazing at a spotless sky, and never letting the gaze break,the whole self gone still: and that instant, Goddess, you wear Bhairava's body.

Practice

Find open sky with nothing in it — no cloud, no bird, no branch at the edge of vision. Look into it and hold the gaze unbroken. Śivopādhyāya reads 'unbroken' quite physically: without interruption, because the eyes are neither opening nor closing. Not blinking is the instruction. Beyond that, no wandering and no refocusing on distance, since there is no distance to find. Let the body go motionless — 'the self stilled', which he glosses simply as a motionless body. In that arrest, at once, the form you wear is Bhairava's. Of everything else in the verse he says only that it explains itself by being said. It is the one portal in this book where the commentator, who elsewhere will spend four pages on five words, finds nothing to add.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya glosses two words — unbroken means unblinking, the self stilled means a motionless body — and then writes that the rest explains itself by the mere saying of it. It is the only place in his commentary where he declines to comment.

Commentary

The eyes are open on something with no feature to rest on, the body is still, and the verse claims the result in the same instant — no sequence, no stages, nothing to get better at. No support at all, which is what makes it the hardest of the easy-sounding ones. That immediacy is the mark of the third kind of means: it either happens or it does not, and effort is precisely what is in the way. Unbroken sky-gazing is a principal practice in the Tibetan Dzogchen and Bon manuals, held there with the same stillness of the eyes, and there is no need to suppose either tradition took it from the other. A cloudless sky is a rare piece of equipment, and it works on whoever looks.

Word by word

ākāśam — the sky, space (n. acc. sg.); vimalam — stainless, clear (n. acc. sg.); paśyan — beholding (pres. part. m. nom. sg. of √dṛś/paś); kṛtvā — having made (gerund of √kṛ); dṛṣṭim — the gaze (f. acc. sg.); nirantarām — unbroken, without gap (f. acc. sg.); stabdha+ātmā — whose whole self is frozen still (m. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi); tat+kṣaṇāt — from that very instant (abl. sg.); devi — O Goddess (voc.); bhairavam — of Bhairava, Bhairava's (n. acc. sg. adj.); vapuḥ — body, form (n. acc. sg.); āpnuyāt — one would attain (opt. 3rd sg. of √āp)

062Sky dissolved into the crown

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 85 · dhāraṇā 62 · Space · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

लीनं मूर्ध्नि वियत् सर्वम् भैरवत्वेन भावयेत्

तत् सर्वम् भैरवाकारतेजस्तत्त्वं समाविशेत्

līnaṃ mūrdhni viyat sarvam bhairavatvena bhāvayet
tat sarvam bhairavākāratejastattvaṃ samāviśet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Contemplate the whole sky dissolved into the crown of the head — as Bhairavaand all of that enters the reality of light that bears Bhairava's shape

Translation · colloquial

Hold the whole sky dissolved into the crown of the head, and hold it as Bhairava;and all of it goes into the reality of light that has Bhairava's shape.

Practice

Sitting, take the whole sky into mind — all of space at once — and contemplate it as gathered and dissolved inside the crown of your head, and that indrawn sky as Bhairava. Śivopādhyāya locates it a little differently from the way the word 'crown' sounds: the principal place, he says, is the one called heart-and-brahmarandhra, and the verse he quotes has space dissolving in the heart and in the brahmarandhra both. Take it as an axis rather than a point. He also says what the indrawn sky is like. It is space, or it is darkness — black, because it has swallowed the portion of light that makes things different from one another. It is filled to the brim with everything there is, now inward. And because it withdraws all, it has the form of Time. Hold everything in that one place. When the contemplation ripens, all that is held there passes into light bearing Bhairava's shape — the space made of consciousness. The wondrous fruit, he says, of contemplating everything as that black form is becoming the supreme space oneself.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya glosses the crown as the place called heart-and-brahmarandhra, and reads the indrawn sky as darkness as well as space — black because it has swallowed the light that makes difference — and as Time, because it withdraws all. He quotes the Mālinīvārtika for it, and this is where his three darkness portals begin.

Commentary

The whole of space is gathered and dissolved into the crown of your head: the largest available object put into one of the smallest available places. The earlier space practices expanded you into the sky; this one runs the film backwards. It is the same wager the armpit verse made from the other end, and the collection keeps returning to it — if space is genuinely space, its size is not one of its properties, and a practice can move it around. What is asked of you is only the holding. The last line uses the school's own technical word for what follows: samāveśa, entry, being taken into. It is not something a practitioner performs. In this text the practitioner arranges a condition and the entry happens, and the verse is careful to put that in a verb you are not the subject of.

Word by word

līnam — dissolved, merged (n. acc. sg.); mūrdhni — in the head, the crown (m. loc. sg.); viyat — the sky, open space (n. acc. sg.); sarvam — entire (n. acc. sg.); bhairavatvena — as Bhairava-ness, as being Bhairava (n. instr. sg., manner); bhāvayet — one should contemplate (opt. 3rd sg. caus. of √bhū); tat — that (n. nom. sg.); sarvam — all (n. nom. sg.); bhairava+ākāra+tejas+tattvam — the reality of light whose shape is Bhairava (n. acc. sg. compound); samāviśet — would enter into (opt. 3rd sg. of sam-ā-√viś)

063The all as Bhairava's form

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 86 · dhāraṇā 63 · Awareness · śāmbhavopāya · no means · causal

किञ्चिज् ज्ञातं द्वैतदायि बाह्यालोकस् तमः पुनः

विश्वादि भैरवं रूपं ज्ञात्वानन्तप्रकाशभृत्

kiñcij jñātaṃ dvaitadāyi bāhyālokas tamaḥ punaḥ
viśvādi bhairavaṃ rūpaṃ jñātvānantaprakāśabhṛt

Translation · literal Sanskrit

A something known gives duality; the outer light in its turn is darknessknowing Bhairava's form as Viśva and the rest, one bears unending light

Translation · colloquial

Know any one thing and already you are two. The light out there shows you nothing but things, so as seeing goes it is a kind of dark.Know that the all, and everything that follows from it, is Bhairava's own form — and the light you carry has no end.

Practice

Śivopādhyāya reads the three terms of this verse as the three states. A something known — a pot, a wall — is waking, and it is where difference begins. The light outside, which shows only such things, is dream, lit by nothing but the residue of waking. The darkness is deep sleep, which he calls the enjoyment of not-knowing. Consciousness taking itself to be each in turn is Viśva, then Taijasa, then Prājña. So: pass through the three, and stop in none of them. What is Bhairava here is the Fourth — not a state standing beside the others but what does not change across them, and what has nothing outside it to stand against. The light such knowing carries has no end because there is no longer anything outside it to be dark.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reads the verse’s three terms as the three states — waking, dream, dreamless sleep — so viśva is not ‘the universe’ but Viśva, the waking self, with taijasa and prājña taken in by ‘and the rest’; Bhairava is तुरीयस्वरूपं, the Fourth (plates 0082–0083). He announces the frame himself — अत्र इयं वेदान्तशास्त्रप्रक्रिया, ‘here this is the procedure of the Vedānta śāstra’ — and closes on ‘all this is verily brahman’. The frame is his, and Vedāntic; the verse is Trika.

Commentary

One of the very few verses here that argues rather than instructs, and the argument is compressed almost past legibility. Knowing a something produces a split — a knower here, a known there — so every act of ordinary cognition manufactures the duality it then reports. From which the startling second clause: the outer light, the light by which we see things, is a kind of darkness, because all it can show is separated objects. This is the recognition philosophy of Kashmir in one line, and the remedy is not to stop knowing. It is to know the whole of what appears, at once, as a single form, so that nothing is left standing opposite you to be known. Whether that can be done on purpose is exactly what divides the third kind of means from the other two. The verse offers no method because there is none to offer.

Word by word

kiñcit — something, a mere something (n. nom. sg.); jñātam — known (past part. n. nom. sg. of √jñā); dvaita+dāyi — duality-giving (n. nom. sg.); bāhya+ālokaḥ — the outer light (m. nom. sg.); tamaḥ — darkness (n. nom. sg.); punaḥ — again, in turn; viśva+ādi — the all and its origin, beginning with the universe (n. acc. sg., adj. to rūpam; the received line reads 'Viśva and the rest', taking viśva as the name of the waking self and the series as the three states — waking, dream, dreamless sleep. That is Śivopādhyāya's frame and he announces it himself as the procedure of the Vedānta śāstra. The compound alone will not give it); bhairavam — of Bhairava (n. acc. sg. adj.); rūpam — form (n. acc. sg.); jñātvā — having known (gerund of √jñā); ananta+prakāśa+bhṛt — bearer of unending light (m. nom. sg.)

Open portal 063 in the reader →

064Darkness of a moonless night

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 87 · dhāraṇā 64 · Space · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → causal

एवम् एव दुर्निशायां कृष्णपक्षागमे चिरम्

तैमिरम् भावयन् रूपम् भैरवं रूपम् एष्यति

evam eva durniśāyāṃ kṛṣṇapakṣāgame ciram
taimiram bhāvayan rūpam bhairavaṃ rūpam eṣyati

Translation · literal Sanskrit

In just this way, on a black night as the dark fortnight comes on — a long whilecontemplating the form made of darkness, one will come to the form of Bhairava

Translation · colloquial

Just the same on a black night, as the dark half of the month comes on, and for a long while:hold the shape that darkness makes, and you will come to the shape of Bhairava.

Practice

Wait for the dark half of the month, and a night gone fully black — Śivopādhyāya glosses the verse's 'bad night' as a night covered over by cloud, so new moon and overcast together. Go where no lamp reaches. Stay a long while — the verse says long — with the eyes open on the blackness, contemplating that darkness itself as a form before you. He names this one, in closing, the contemplation of darkness with the eyes open, and pairs it with the portal that follows, which is the same contemplation with the eyes closed. He also treats it as continuous with the two verses before it: the same dark, met first inwardly and then out here. Do not people it with images. Held so, the dark form you are watching and the form of Bhairava arrive as one. One honest warning, which is ours and not his. Sustained looking into total darkness produces visual noise in nearly everyone — drifting shapes, false light, faces — because a visual system given no signal starts inventing one. The instruction not to people the dark with images is the whole difficulty of this practice, and it is not a small one.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya glosses the bad night as one covered over by cloud, and names this the contemplation of darkness with the eyes open — pairing it with the next portal, which is the same contemplation with them closed. He reads it as continuous with the two verses before it rather than standing alone.

Commentary

The night twin of the sky-gazing portal: there a featureless bright, here a featureless dark, and the same demand that the eye be given nothing it can work with. It is also one of only two or three practices in the book with a logistical requirement — the dark fortnight, a moonless night, no lamp — which in most modern places is harder to arrange than the text could have imagined. Ciram, a long while, is worth marking: this is the only portal besides the twenty-eighth to say anything about duration, and it says the opposite. One honest warning. Sustained looking into total darkness produces visual noise in nearly everyone — drifting shapes, false light, faces — because a visual system given no signal starts inventing one. The instruction not to people the dark with images is the whole difficulty of this practice, and it is not a small one.

Word by word

evam eva — in just this way; durniśāyām — on a black, forbidding night (f. loc. sg.); kṛṣṇa+pakṣa+āgame — at the coming of the dark fortnight (m. loc. sg.); ciram — a long while (adverbial acc.); taimiram — made of darkness (n. acc. sg., with rūpam); bhāvayan — contemplating (pres. part. caus. m. nom. sg. of √bhū); rūpam — form (n. acc. sg.); bhairavam — of Bhairava (n. acc. sg. adj.); rūpam — form (n. acc. sg.); eṣyati — one will come to, will reach (fut. 3rd sg. of √i)

065Darkness behind closed eyes

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 88 · dhāraṇā 65 · Space · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle

एवम् एव निमील्यादौ नेत्रे कृष्णाभम् अग्रतः

प्रसार्य भैरवं रूपम् भावयंस् तन्मयो भवेत्

evam eva nimīlyādau netre kṛṣṇābham agrataḥ
prasārya bhairavaṃ rūpam bhāvayaṃs tanmayo bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

In just this way — both eyes first closed on the blackness in frontthen thrown wide — contemplating that as Bhairava's form, one becomes made of it

Translation · colloquial

Just the same again: both eyes closed first on the blackness in front of you,then thrown open; hold that as Bhairava's shape, and you are made of it.

Practice

This needs no night — Śivopādhyāya says so directly: it works even in the absence of the dark fortnight and a clouded night. First close both eyes. Attend to the blackness that stands in front of them — seen, not imagined — and contemplate it as the form of Bhairava; black-appearing, he says, and made of darkness. When that holds steady, open the eyes wide and find the same dark form spread before you, unbroken by what appears in it. Stay with it. You become made of what you contemplate — and what you become made of, he says, is light. One plain observation. What you actually see with the eyes shut is not black. It is a mottled, drifting grey, and the Sanskrit says as much — a black appearance, a black glow, rather than an absence. Attending to it as something rather than as nothing is the shift the verse wants.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya confirms that this one needs no night at all, keeps the two steps distinct — first the eyes closed, afterwards thrown wide on the same black — and finishes the arc that began two portals back: contemplating the darkness, one becomes made of light.

Commentary

The domestic version of the verse before, requiring neither a moonless night nor anywhere to go — and it repays doing because of its second half. First establish the dark as a form with the eyes closed; then open them, and find the same form still there, unbroken by what is now appearing in it. That two-step is the pattern the whole tradition depends on: a condition found in the shelter of a practice, then deliberately carried across the boundary into a room with things in it. Almost everything that fails in contemplative life fails at that boundary. One plain observation. What you actually see with the eyes shut is not black. It is a mottled, drifting grey, and the Sanskrit says as much — a black appearance, a black glow, rather than an absence. Attending to it as something rather than as nothing is the shift the verse wants.

Word by word

evam eva — in just this way; nimīlya — having closed (gerund of ni-√mīl); ādau — at first (loc. sg. as adverb); netre — the two eyes (n. acc. dual); kṛṣṇa+ābham — the black appearance, black glow (n. acc. sg.); agrataḥ — in front (adverb); prasārya — having opened wide, spread (gerund, caus. of pra-√sṛ; object taken as netre); bhairavam — of Bhairava (n. acc. sg. adj.); rūpam — form (n. acc. sg.); bhāvayan — contemplating (pres. part. caus. m. nom. sg. of √bhū); tat+mayaḥ — made of that (m. nom. sg.); bhavet — one becomes (opt. 3rd sg. of √bhū)

066A sense stopped

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 89 · dhāraṇā 66 · Body · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle → causal

यस्य कस्येन्द्रियस्यापि व्याघाताच् च निरोधतः

प्रविष्टस्याद्वये शून्ये तत्रैवात्मा प्रकाशते

yasya kasyendriyasyāpi vyāghātāc ca nirodhataḥ
praviṣṭasyādvaye śūnye tatraivātmā prakāśate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

When any sense whatever, through a blocking blow and through restrainthas entered the emptiness without a second — right there the ātman shines forth

Translation · colloquial

When any sense at all, through a blocking blow and through being held back,has gone into the emptiness that has no second, right there the self shines out.

Practice

Take any one sense and bring it to a stop — palms sealed over the ears, eyes pressed shut. Śivopādhyāya reads the verse's two methods as three. There is the blocking blow, made by something outside that overpowers the sense. And there is restraint, which he divides: the stop that arises of its own accord, and the stop you bring about by effort. The first of those three asks nothing of you but that you notice. Then follow the stopped sense back in. You do not attend to the blocking; you go in along the sense to the place its object used to occupy. Where its object was, there is now an emptiness with no second thing in it. Enter there and become firmly established in it — the inward-facing state, he calls it. Immediately thereupon, he says — not gradually — exactly there, the ātman shines out: the self that was doing the sensing, found where the sensing stopped.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya makes three doors of the verse's two: the blow from outside, the restraint that arises of its own accord, and the restraint brought about by effort. Entering the emptiness he glosses as becoming firmly established in the inward-facing state, and he adds that the shining follows immediately rather than by degrees.

Commentary

The seventeenth portal's formula returns — any whatever, take your pick — and the two methods offered here are startlingly unequal in refinement and offered as equals. A blocking blow: palms clamped over the ears, eyes pressed shut. Or restraint: the same stop made from inside, with nothing touched. Crude and subtle, listed with no preference, which is characteristic of a book whose ninety-fifth portal will treat a sneeze as seriously as a samādhi. What separates this from merely plugging your ears is the follow-back. You do not attend to the blocking; you go in along the stopped sense to the place its object used to occupy, and stay in what is there instead. And what shines there is named unusually: not Bhairava and not the Goddess but the ātman — the one who was doing the sensing, found where the sensing stopped.

Word by word

yasya kasya — of whichever whatsoever (gen. sg.); indriyasya — of the sense-faculty (n. gen. sg.); api — even; vyāghātāt — through obstruction, a blocking blow (m. abl. sg.); ca — and; nirodhataḥ — through restraint, suppression (abl. in -taḥ); praviṣṭasya — of [that faculty] having entered (past part. gen. sg. of pra-√viś, continuing indriyasya); advaye — non-dual, without a second (n. loc. sg.); śūnye — in the emptiness (n. loc. sg.); tatra eva — right there; ātmā — the self, ātman (m. nom. sg.); prakāśate — shines forth (pres. 3rd sg. ātmanepada of pra-√kāś)

067Reciting the bare a

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 90 · dhāraṇā 67 · Sound · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle → causal

अबिन्दुम् अविसर्गं च अकारं जपतो महान्

उदेति देवि सहसा ज्ञानौघः परमेश्वरः

abindum avisargaṃ ca akāraṃ japato mahān
udeti devi sahasā jñānaughaḥ parameśvaraḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

For one reciting the letter a — no bindu, no visarga — a greatflood of knowing rises all at once, Goddess — the highest lord

Translation · colloquial

Recite the letter a with nothing added to it, no point above, no breath after, and a greatflood of knowing rises all at once, Goddess: the highest lord.

Practice

Recite the open vowel a — the bare sound, with nothing to close it: no bindu, the nasal hum written as a dot, and no visarga, the trailing breath written ḥ. Some, Śivopādhyāya notes, do this while the breath is held. He is clear that the two marks being stripped are not merely punctuation. Bindu is the awareness of undividedness, the knowledge of non-duality; visarga is the act that emits the whole spread of difference — every other letter, from ka onward, being its work. What is left when both are gone stands before either: not the knowledge that all is one, and not the outpouring that makes things many. He also hears the letter's own name as an instruction. A-kāra: that whose means of making is not present. It is the sound a mouth makes by opening, requiring no articulation at all, and what you are practising is the sound that is not made. He calls it the Unsurpassed, and calls the contemplation of it the thinking of nothing whatever. Let it repeat, plain and unfinished. What follows is not something you do: suddenly, the verse says, a great flood of knowing rises — the highest lord. Keep to the endingless a, and be available to what rises. Set it beside the sixteenth portal, which attended to the ending of OM: there the door was the finish, here it is the refusal of one.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reads the two stripped marks as more than phonetics: bindu is the knowledge of non-duality, visarga the act that emits the whole spread of difference. What is left stands before both. He hears a-kāra as 'that whose means of making is not present', calls the contemplation the thinking of nothing whatever, and notes that some recite it while the breath is held. He quotes the Dvayasaṃpatti of Vāmananātha, which cites this very verse as scripture — the same work, under a second name and a second spelling, that he leans on again at the taking up of the full I-ness. Vāmana and Hrasva both mean short, and the verse he draws from it is the same verse in both places.

Commentary

A is the sound a mouth makes by opening — the first letter, requiring no articulation at all — and in this school's phonetic theology it is Śiva's own letter, the counterpart of the ha that the fifty-eighth portal put in an open mouth. Between them the two verses hold the whole alphabet, which is to say the whole of manifestation, at its two ends. What is stripped away here are precisely the two marks that let a syllable finish: the bindu that closes it into a hum, the visarga that lets it breathe out. Without them the sound has no way to end, and reciting something that cannot conclude is the practice. Set it beside the sixteenth portal, which attended to the ending of OM: there the door was the finish, here it is the refusal of one. Sahasā — suddenly — is how the verse says the rest arrives.

Word by word

a+bindum — without bindu, the nasal point ṃ (m. acc. sg.); a+visargam — without visarga, the final breath ḥ (m. acc. sg.); ca — and; a+kāram — the letter a (m. acc. sg.); japataḥ — of one reciting (pres. part. gen. m. sg. of √jap); mahān — great (m. nom. sg.); udeti — rises up (pres. 3rd sg. of ud-√i); devi — O Goddess (voc.); sahasā — suddenly, all at once (instr. as adverb); jñāna+oghaḥ — flood of knowing (m. nom. sg.); parama+īśvaraḥ — the highest lord (m. nom. sg.)

068The end of the visarga

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 91 · dhāraṇā 68 · Sound · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle → causal

वर्णस्य सविसर्गस्य विसर्गान्तं चितिं कुरु

निराधारेण चित्तेन स्पृशेद् ब्रह्म सनातनम्

varṇasya savisargasya visargāntaṃ citiṃ kuru
nirādhāreṇa cittena spṛśed brahma sanātanam

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Of a letter joined with its visarga, set awareness on the visarga's endwith the mind supportless, one touches brahman, the everlasting

Translation · colloquial

Take a letter that carries its breath after it, and set awareness on the very end of that breath;with the mind resting on nothing, you touch what is everlasting.

Practice

Sound a letter that closes with visarga — aḥ. The visarga is the light out-breath at the end, an h that thins away into nothing. Put your awareness on the very end of that breath — the point where the sound is over and nothing has yet replaced it. The mind waiting there has nothing to hold. With that unsupported mind, the verse says, you touch brahman, which does not end. Śivopādhyāya adds that the ending is not to be engineered. The visarga comes to its end of itself, he says, out of an excess of touching your own inward-facing nature: attend inward hard enough and the outward emission simply runs out. He also reads the same word a second way — not at the end of the visarga but in the very nature of the visarga — which makes the emission itself the place you rest rather than the thing you wait out. Either is the practice.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya calls the visarga the instrument of the bound condition — the outward emission by which one is bound at all — so its ending is the withdrawal of that apparatus, brought about by an excess of inward attention rather than by managing the breath. He offers a second reading of the same word as a locative: make awareness in the very nature of the visarga. And where our note reads the verb as a brief touch, he writes that once knowing has nothing to rest on, the touch of the supreme brahman is constant.

Commentary

This closes a set of four portals working one seam in sound: the silence after OM, both edges of any letter whatever, a syllable with no way to end, and now the ending itself taken as the object. Visarga is the light breath at the close of a syllable, written as two dots — and in this school it is also a technical term for emission, the outpouring by which Śiva becomes a world. So attending to where a visarga finishes is attending, in miniature, to where emission stops. Whether that resonance is doing any work in the practice or only in the theology is a fair question, and the practice stands without it. Note the verb, which is the most modest result-word in the whole book. Not becomes, not attains, not enters. One touches brahman — a hand laid on something briefly, and taken away.

Word by word

varṇasya — of the letter (m. gen. sg.); sa+visargasya — accompanied by visarga (m. gen. sg.); visarga+antam — ending at the visarga, [set] on the visarga's end (f. acc. sg., adj. to citim); citim — awareness (f. acc. sg.); kuru — make (imper. 2nd sg. of √kṛ); nirādhāreṇa — supportless (n. instr. sg.); cittena — with the mind (n. instr. sg.); spṛśet — one would touch (opt. 3rd sg. of √spṛś); brahma — brahman (n. acc. sg.); sanātanam — everlasting (n. acc. sg.)

069The self shaped like sky

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 92 · dhāraṇā 69 · Space · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

व्योमाकारं स्वम् आत्मानं ध्यायेद् दिग्भिर् अनावृतम्

निराश्रया चितिः शक्तिः स्वरूपं दर्शयेत् तदा

vyomākāraṃ svam ātmānaṃ dhyāyed digbhir anāvṛtam
nirāśrayā citiḥ śaktiḥ svarūpaṃ darśayet tadā

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Meditate on your own self shaped like the sky, unbounded in any directionthen citi, the śakti with nothing to rest on, shows her own nature

Translation · colloquial

Meditate on your own self shaped like the sky, fenced in by no direction at all;then the power that is awareness, having nothing to rest on, shows you what she really is.

Practice

Sit and meditate on your own self as space — the shape of sky, with no covering in any direction: nothing above, nothing below, nothing at any side that bounds you. Śivopādhyāya is careful about what that means: sky-shaped means formless, and formless does not mean a nothing. Unenclosed by the directions, he says, is simply all-pervading. Be that unwalled extent, steadily. When it holds, awareness herself — citi, the śakti, left with no base to stand on — shows you her own nature. The showing is hers; the unwalled self is yours to keep.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya glosses 'unenclosed by the directions' as all-pervading, and then guards the other phrase: sky-shaped means formless — but not, he says, a mere nothing.

Commentary

The exact inversion of the sixty-second portal. There the sky was drawn into the crown of the head; here the self is opened out until it has the shape of sky and no direction closes it. Read together they say that which way round you do it does not matter, which is itself the teaching. What is worth noticing is the word describing awareness when the walls are gone: nirāśrayā, with nothing to rest on. That word and its cousin nirādhāra have run through half a dozen portals now — a frozen gaze, a body imagined unsupported, the end of a visarga — and they are all producing the same condition by different routes. And the last line disowns the result in the usual way. She shows her own nature. You do not find it, and you are not the one doing the showing.

Word by word

vyoma+ākāram — having the form of the sky (m. acc. sg.); svam — one's own (m. acc. sg.); ātmānam — self (m. acc. sg.); dhyāyet — one should meditate on (opt. 3rd sg. of √dhyai); digbhiḥ — by the directions (f. instr. pl.); anāvṛtam — uncovered, unbounded (m. acc. sg.); nirāśrayā — baseless, without support (f. nom. sg.); citiḥ — awareness (f. nom. sg.); śaktiḥ — power, śakti (f. nom. sg.); sva+rūpam — [her] own form, own nature (n. acc. sg.); darśayet — would show (opt. 3rd sg. caus. of √dṛś); tadā — then

070Awareness at a needle's point

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 93 · dhāraṇā 70 · Feeling · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → causal

किंचिद् अङ्गं विभिद्यादौ तीक्ष्णसूच्यादिना ततः

तत्रैव चेतनां युक्त्वा भैरवे निर्मला गतिः

kiṃcid aṅgaṃ vibhidyādau tīkṣṇasūcyādinā tataḥ
tatraiva cetanāṃ yuktvā bhairave nirmalā gatiḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Pierce some limb first with a sharp needle or the likethen yoke awareness to that very place — a stainless way into Bhairava

Translation · colloquial

First pierce some part of the body with a sharp needle or something like it,then yoke your awareness to that one place: a clean road into Bhairava.

Practice

The verse is blunt, and Śivopādhyāya does not soften it: prick some part of the body with a sharp needle. He restricts it about as far as it will go — a finger or a thumb, with a needle or a thorn — and our own caution about this portal stands unchanged. What he changes is where the attention goes. Not to the pain: to the awareness that is undergoing it. Yoke your knowing to that one spot and be collected in the power of awareness that is the experiencer of the sting — not the flinch, not the story, and not the sensation either, but the one feeling it. Attended that way, he says, stainless knowledge whose object is Bhairava does come about.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya restricts the limb to a finger or a thumb and the instrument to a sharpened needle or a thorn, and his verb is 'having cut'. But he puts the attention not on the pain: one is to be collected, he says, in the power of awareness that is the experiencer of the needle's pain.

Commentary

Here the book asks something we would not pass on without comment. A needle in the skin is the most commanding object a person can produce on demand — a small bright pain admits no wandering at all, which is exactly why it works as a concentration device and exactly why it is not safe. Nothing in this text screens who is reading it, and a verse that sets a needle beside a good meal and a piece of music has not weighed the difference in risk. For anyone with any history of hurting themselves, this door should stay shut, and we would say the same to anyone who finds the idea attractive rather than merely interesting. What the practice actually consists of — attention pinned to a single point of sensation, without the flinch and without the story — is available from a stubbed toe, from the crawling-ant tingle of the forty-fourth portal, and from any pain that arrives on its own. The ninety-fifth portal makes precisely that case.

Word by word

kiṃcit — some (n. acc. sg.); aṅgam — limb, part of the body (n. acc. sg.); vibhidya — having pierced (gerund of vi-√bhid); ādau — first (loc. sg. as adverb); tīkṣṇa+sūcī+ādinā — with a sharp needle or the like (instr. sg.); tataḥ — then; tatra eva — right there, at that very place; cetanām — awareness (f. acc. sg.); yuktvā — having yoked, fixed (gerund of √yuj); bhairave — in, into Bhairava (m. loc. sg.); nirmalā — stainless, clear (f. nom. sg.); gatiḥ — going, way, access (f. nom. sg.)

071No inner machinery

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 94 · dhāraṇā 71 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

चित्ताद्यन्तःकृतिर् नास्ति ममान्तर् भावयेद् इति

विकल्पानाम् अभावेन विकल्पैर् उज्झितो भवेत्

cittādyantaḥkṛtir nāsti mamāntar bhāvayed iti
vikalpānām abhāvena vikalpair ujjhito bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

'The inner apparatus, mind and the rest, is not within me' — contemplate thisthrough the absence of thought-constructs, one is abandoned by thought-constructs

Translation · colloquial

'The inner machinery, mind and all the rest of it, is not inside me' — hold that;and with nothing being made of anything, you are the one the makings have walked out on.

Practice

Sit quietly and put this to yourself, in the concrete form Śivopādhyāya gives it: the inner machinery — mind, judgment, the sense of an I — is not inside this body. Look for the organ where it is supposed to be and find no such thing there. Then let the denial widen: not in the body, not inside anywhere, not yours, not the furniture of a self. Hold it as a felt conviction, not an argument. Thought-constructs need that machinery to stand on; as the conviction deepens they lose their footing. When they go, do not build anything in their place. Remain in what is left when they have left you.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reads 'within me' as within my body: the inner organ does not exist inside this body. He supplies the sentence's syntax and stops there, saying nothing about the second line.

Commentary

Hold, as a felt conviction rather than a proposition, that the inner apparatus is not in you. Mind, discriminating intellect, the I-maker: standard equipment in Indian psychology, the machinery every school agrees a person runs on, and the verse asks you to find it not yours, not inside, not the furniture of a self. Not that it is unreal, and not that you should suppress it. The mechanism is structural. Thought-constructs need that apparatus to stand on, and when the conviction settles they lose their floor. Then comes the sentence the whole book could be summarised by, and it is worth reading twice for its grammar. Not that one abandons thought-constructs, which would be another act of the very machinery in question. One is abandoned by them. They leave. You are what is left when they have gone.

Word by word

citta+ādi+antaḥkṛtiḥ — the inner apparatus, mind and the rest (f. nom. sg.; antaḥkṛti ≈ antaḥkaraṇa, the inner instrument); na — not; asti — is, exists (3rd sg. pres.); mama — of me, my (gen. sg.); antar — within (indecl.); bhāvayet — let one contemplate, cultivate the conviction (3rd sg. opt. caus.); iti — thus, closing the quoted thought (indecl.); vikalpānām — of thought-constructs (m. gen. pl.); abhāvena — through the absence (m. inst. sg.); vikalpaiḥ — by thought-constructs (m. inst. pl.); ujjhitaḥ — abandoned, left behind (past part., m. nom. sg.); bhavet — one becomes (3rd sg. opt.)

072The veils doing their work

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 95 · dhāraṇā 72 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

माया विमोहिनी नाम कलायाः कलनं स्थितम्

इत्यादिधर्मं तत्त्वानां कलयन् न पृथग् भवेत्

māyā vimohinī nāma kalāyāḥ kalanaṃ sthitam
ityādidharmaṃ tattvānāṃ kalayan na pṛthag bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Māyā is by name the bewilderer; parceling out stands as kalā's workreckoning such characters of the tattvas, one does not become separate

Translation · colloquial

The great illusion is called the bewilderer by name, and parcelling out is the parceller's own work;reckon such marks in the things the world is made of, and you do not come apart from them.

Practice

Śivopādhyāya names this one: a dhāraṇā of standing aside. He prefaces it with the old line that the strands move among the strands — and then asks, in his own words, what has come to me? Take the tattvas, the principles that build your experience, one at a time. Māyā bewilders by spreading difference out — and bewildering is her property, not mine. Kalā's property is a little doing; the vidyā-tattva's is a little knowing. Reckon each veil as a function doing its named job, and each time, notice that the property belongs to the veil and not to you. Reckoning them so, the verse says, you do not become separate. Śivopādhyāya puts it positively: what you come into is the recognition of non-difference — and discriminating is how you get there, not what you arrive at.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya heads this a dhāraṇā of standing aside, prefaced by the Gītā's 'the strands move among the strands' and his own 'what has come to me?'. Bewildering, he says, is māyā's property, not mine; kalā's property is a little doing, and the vidyā-tattva's a little knowing. Not becoming separate he reads positively, as coming to recognise non-difference — and then quotes the Sāṃkhya formula that liberation comes of discriminating prakṛti from puruṣa, having just made discrimination the means to non-difference rather than the end.

Commentary

A scholastic practice like the thirty-third portal, and it needs a word of apparatus. In this system a person is not a fragment of the divine but the whole of it wearing a set of restrictions — māyā and the sheaths under her, of which kalā is the one that cuts unlimited power down to the size of a particular doer. The verse asks you to take each of these in turn and reckon it as a function doing its named job: this is bewilderment, bewildering; this is limitation, limiting. The Sanskrit enacts what it asks, using one root three times over — kalā's kalana, reckoned by one who is kalayan. What the practice rests on is a simple and testable claim: a limitation you have watched operating as a limitation stops being the shape of you. Naming the veil is not removing it. It is stopping mistaking it for a face.

Word by word

māyā — Māyā, the power of bewilderment (f. nom. sg.); vimohinī — deluding, the bewilderer (f. nom. sg.); nāma — by name (indecl.); kalāyāḥ — of kalā, [the principle of] limited agency (f. gen. sg.); kalanam — parceling out, the act of limiting (n. nom. sg.); sthitam — stands as, is settled (past part., n. nom. sg.); iti+ādi+dharmam — character such as this (m. acc. sg.; 'the dharma beginning thus'); tattvānām — of the tattvas, the constituent principles (n. gen. pl.); kalayan — reckoning, considering (pres. part., m. nom. sg.); na — not; pṛthak — separate, apart (indecl.); bhavet — one becomes (3rd sg. opt.)

073A desire caught arising

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 96 · dhāraṇā 73 · Feeling · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle

झगितीच्छां समुत्पन्नाम् अवलोक्य शमं नयेत्

यत एव समुद्भूता ततस् तत्रैव लीयते

jhagitīcchāṃ samutpannām avalokya śamaṃ nayet
yata eva samudbhūtā tatas tatraiva līyate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

A desire springs up in a flash — look at it, and lead it to quietthe very place it sprang from — then, in that very place, it dissolves

Translation · colloquial

A wanting springs up in a flash: look straight at it, and lead it to quiet.The very place it sprang from is the place where, then and there, it dissolves.

Practice

There is nothing to set up. Wait for what happens many times an hour: a desire flashing up — let this be mine. Catch one at the instant of arising and look straight at it, inwardly, before it turns into plans. Then bring it quickly to quiet — not by argument, but by holding it in the looking. Śivopādhyāya adds the reason it goes: the wanting was never in you, only shown midway by not-knowing, and not-knowing is of the nature of space. It dissolves where it rose. Stay at that place. If you cannot catch it in time — and Śivopādhyāya raises this objection himself, because one wanting is only followed by the next — there is a second way in, and it asks nothing of your speed. Let two wantings come. Attend to the place where the first has finished and the second has not yet formed: the opening that is felt spreading through everything at the end of the two thoughts. Hold both thoughts in that single act of noticing. That opening is where each of them came from, and holding it wears both of them away.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya glosses the place a desire dissolves into as the blessed Spanda in the form of unmeṣa, and quotes Vasugupta for it. He also raises the obvious objection — that one cannot always bring a desire to quiet, since another follows it — and answers with a second practice: meditate on the unmeṣa felt pervasively at the end of the two thoughts, holding both in one act of connecting, and both are worn away. He notes that pure consciousness, being formless, contains no desire at all; the wanting is shown midway by ignorance, and ignorance is of the nature of space.

Commentary

Everything depends on the first word. Jhagiti — in a flash, at the instant. You are to catch a desire at its arising, before it has become an intention and long before it has become a plan, and by then it is no longer the object this verse is talking about. That is the whole difficulty, and it is a matter of speed rather than of virtue. What follows is not suppression and not argument: you look at it, and the looking itself leads it back. The verse's claim about where it goes is precise and can be checked — a desire watched at its source dissolves into the source, and what interests the text is not that the wanting stopped but that you are now standing at the place wanting comes from. Read this one together with the portal two doors along, which gives the opposite instruction on the same material.

Word by word

jhagiti — in a flash, all at once (indecl.); icchām — a desire (f. acc. sg.); samutpannām — arisen, sprung up (past part., f. acc. sg.); avalokya — having looked at it (gerund); śamam — quiet, stilling (m. acc. sg.); nayet — let one lead [it] (3rd sg. opt.); yataḥ — from which place (indecl. abl.); eva — the very (emphatic); samudbhūtā — it sprang up (past part., f. nom. sg.); tataḥ — then, thereupon (indecl.); tatra — there, in that place (indecl.); eva — precisely (emphatic); līyate — it dissolves, melts away (3rd sg. pres. mid.)
