074Who am I before desire

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 97 · dhāraṇā 74 · Feeling · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

यदा ममेच्छा नोत्पन्ना ज्ञानं वा कस् तदास्मि वै

तत्त्वतो ऽहं तथाभूतस् तल्लीनस् तन्मना भवेत्

yadā mamecchā notpannā jñānaṃ vā kas tadāsmi vai
tattvato 'haṃ tathābhūtas tallīnas tanmanā bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

When no desire of mine has arisen, no knowing — who then am I?in truth I am that very condition — let one become dissolved in it, mind on it

Translation · colloquial

When no wanting of mine has come up, and no knowing either, who am I then?In truth I am exactly that condition; melt into it, with the mind on nothing else.

Practice

Watch for the gap in which nothing is wanted, nothing is being known and nothing is being done — it occurs, briefly, on its own. In that gap ask: who am I then? Do not answer in words. The verse answers for you: in truth you are that very condition. So let the mind sink into it and rest there, dissolved in it, the mind given over to it. Śivopādhyāya adds a correction worth having. What you meet there is not confined to the gap. Consciousness and beatitude, wanting, knowing and doing are the Self's own powers in every state, he says, and not only at that moment. The gap is the occasion, not the location — it is simply where nothing is covering what is always the case.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya takes 'knowing' to stand for action too, so the verse's pair is the familiar triad of will, knowledge and action. His answer to the question is flat: in that condition there is no 'I' at all, only consciousness and bliss. And he adds that these powers belong to the Self in every state, not merely in that moment.

Commentary

The question is not rhetorical and it is not to be answered. It is an instrument for staying in a condition that has already opened — the small gap, which does occur, in which nothing is wanted and nothing is being known. Ramana Maharshi's self-enquiry is this question and this method, eleven hundred years later, and there is no reason to think he had it from here; the enquiry is available to anyone who notices the gap and declines to fill it. What makes this verse Saiva rather than Vedantic is the second line: the condition is not a stripped-back witness but the whole thing, and one is asked to dissolve into it rather than stand behind it observing.

Word by word

yadā — when (indecl.); mama — my (gen. sg.); icchā — desire (f. nom. sg.); na — not; utpannā — arisen (past part., f. nom. sg.); jñānam — knowing, cognition (n. nom. sg.); vā — or (indecl.); kaḥ — who (m. nom. sg.); tadā — then (indecl.); asmi — I am (1st sg. pres.); vai — indeed (indecl.); tattvataḥ — in truth (indecl.); aham — I (nom. sg.); tathā+bhūtaḥ — become thus, of that very condition (past part., m. nom. sg.); tat+līnaḥ — dissolved into that (m. nom. sg.); tat+manāḥ — with mind on that (m. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi); bhavet — let one become (3rd sg. opt.)

Open portal 074 in the reader →

075Entering the stirring itself

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 98 · dhāraṇā 75 · Feeling · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

इच्छायाम् अथवा ज्ञाने जाते चित्तं निवेशयेत्

आत्मबुद्ध्यानन्यचेतास् ततस् तत्त्वार्थदर्शनम्

icchāyām athavā jñāne jāte cittaṃ niveśayet
ātmabuddhyānanyacetās tatas tattvārthadarśanam

Translation · literal Sanskrit

When a desire has arisen, or a knowing — settle the mind into itsure it is the self, mind on nothing else — then, sight of what is

Translation · colloquial

When a wanting has come up, or a knowing, settle the mind right into it,certain that it is yourself, with the mind on nothing else; and then you see what is.

Practice

When a desire arises, or a sudden knowing, do not follow it out toward its object. Śivopādhyāya puts the cut exactly: abandon the mental construct of the object, and keep the arising. Set the mind inside the stirring itself, holding it with the conviction that this is nothing but ātman — your self — and let nothing else into attention. By that alone, he says, comes knowledge of what is finally real; and he quotes the old line, this Self is brahman. Held so, the stirring opens and the real nature of things shows itself. Notice this differs from stilling the desire: here you enter it.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya makes the cut precise: abandon the construct of the object, not the desire. He quotes 'this Self is brahman' as scripture for it, and classifies the portal — this is contemplation with form, otherwise called contemplation with a support.

Commentary

Two portals ago a rising desire was led back to quiet. Here one is entered and ridden. The text sets opposite instructions on the same material almost side by side and ranks neither, which is worth pausing on: a collection built to be authoritative would have chosen. This one is choosing to say that both are doors, and that which one is yours today is not a question a book can answer. What makes this the more radical of the pair is the instrument named — ātmabuddhi, the conviction that the stirring is the self. Not a disturbance to be pacified, not an intruder, not evidence of a fault: a movement of what you are, entered as such. That is the sentence separating this tradition from every renunciate reading of the same phenomenon, and it is stated here without argument, as though obvious.

Word by word

icchāyām — in a desire (f. loc. sg.); athavā — or else (indecl.); jñāne — in a knowing (n. loc. sg.); jāte — when it has arisen (past part., loc. sg., locative absolute); cittam — the mind (n. acc. sg.); niveśayet — let one set [it], make it enter (3rd sg. opt. caus.); ātma+buddhyā — with the conviction [that it is] the self (f. inst. sg.); an+anya+cetāḥ — with mind on nothing else (m. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi); tataḥ — then (indecl.); tattva+artha+darśanam — the seeing of the real matter, sight of what is (n. nom. sg.)

076Knowing with no owner

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 99 · dhāraṇā 76 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

निर्निमित्तम् भवेज् ज्ञानं निराधारम् भ्रमात्मकम्

तत्त्वतः कस्यचिन् नैतद् एवम्भावी शिवः प्रिये

nirnimittam bhavej jñānaṃ nirādhāram bhramātmakam
tattvataḥ kasyacin naitad evambhāvī śivaḥ priye

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Knowing is without cause — resting on nothing — made of wandering errorin reality it is no one's — contemplating so, beloved, one is Śiva

Translation · colloquial

Knowing is causeless, groundless, and made of wandering error;in truth it belongs to no one at all. Hold it so, beloved, and you are Shiva.

Practice

Take any moment of ordinary knowing — seeing the cup, remembering a name — and examine it. It has no cause you can find behind it, no ground it rests on, and it wanders and errs. Above all, look for its owner: whose is it? In reality, no one's. When 'mine' falls away from knowing, what remains contemplating is Śiva. Let the search for an owner fail, and rest in the failing. Śivopādhyāya offers one image for the middle of this. To think 'this is that same cup' is, he says, like saying 'this is that same current of the Vitastā' — the river of his own valley. Same channel, same name, and not for one moment the same water.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya heads this a śākta practice, and says the verse casts a sidelong glance at the Buddhist view: knowing alone, momentary, a series, with no knower and no substrate belonging to it. He then takes that analysis further than this edition asks the reader to go — no stable self, no pot, not even the eye and the light — and illustrates it with the river of his own valley: to think 'this is that same pot' is like saying 'this is that same current of the Vitastā'. He caps it with what the Buddhists have not: one devoted to contemplating so is Śiva himself.

Commentary

Kin to the seventy-fourth portal's question, turned from wanting onto knowing. Take any ordinary cognition — the cup seen, the name recalled — and look for what it belongs to. The verse asserts three things about it that a reader is free not to grant: that it has no cause, no support, and is made of wandering error. The practice does not require granting them. It requires the fourth move, which is the search for an owner, and that search can be conducted by anyone honestly and will produce the same result. There is no one there. What is strange and precise in the instruction is where it asks you to stop: not at the conclusion that knowing is unowned, which is a thought like any other, but in the failing of the search — the moment the looking finds nothing and has not yet reported it.

Word by word

nir+nimittam — without cause (n. nom. sg.); bhavet — is, turns out to be (3rd sg. opt.); jñānam — knowing, cognition (n. nom. sg.); nir+ādhāram — without support, resting on nothing (n. nom. sg.); bhrama+ātmakam — delusive in nature, made of wandering error (n. nom. sg.); tattvataḥ — in reality (indecl.); kasyacit — of anyone, anyone's (gen. sg.); na — not; etat — this (n. nom. sg.); evam+bhāvī — one who contemplates thus, whose being is thus (m. nom. sg.); śivaḥ — Śiva (m. nom. sg.); priye — beloved (f. voc. sg.)

Open portal 076 in the reader →

077The same in every body

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 100 · dhāraṇā 77 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

चिद्धर्मा सर्वदेहेषु विशेषो नास्ति कुत्रचित्

अतश् च तन्मयं सर्वम् भावयन् भवजिज् जनः

ciddharmā sarvadeheṣu viśeṣo nāsti kutracit
ataś ca tanmayaṃ sarvam bhāvayan bhavajij janaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

What is by nature consciousness is in every body — no difference anywhereso, contemplating all this as made of that, a person conquers becoming

Translation · colloquial

What is awareness by its nature is in every body, and nowhere is there any difference;so hold that all of it is made of that, and a person is the conqueror of becoming.

Practice

As you move among people and animals, hold this: what looks out of each body has consciousness for its nature, and in that there is no difference anywhere — no more of it here, no less there. Śivopādhyāya puts it at full strength: from a worm up to Sadāśiva, no difference at all; and he quotes a verse saying that the very awareness in the sun that ornaments the sky is the same one in the belly of a worm in a hole in the ground. Then widen it: everything whatever is made of that. The verse asks for sustained contemplation, not a passing thought. A person who keeps it up outgrows the round of becoming.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya takes the claim further than the verse does — from a worm up to Sadāśiva, he says, there is no difference at all — and quotes a verse for it: the same awareness that is in the sun ornamenting the sky is the one in the belly of a worm in a hollow of the earth. But he then restricts what may be drawn from it. 'Contemplating', he insists, means holding the knowledge and nothing else, and not the act of sitting down to eat with a dog or a dog-cooker; otherwise, he asks with a quoted verse, what would separate a seer of reality from a dog eating filth? This edition does not follow him there. The restriction is his, not the verse's, and the reader should have both.

Commentary

In a book almost wholly concerned with what happens inside one person, this verse turns and faces outward, and it is the nearest thing here to an ethic. What looks out of every body has consciousness for its nature, and there is no difference anywhere — not less of it in one, not more in another. The word is deha, body, not manuṣya, human, and the text does not qualify it. A person actually holding this cannot treat a body as an instrument, which is a live question in a collection that elsewhere takes union with a partner and a needle in the skin as doorways. Read it against the forty-sixth and the seventieth: it does not cancel them, it sets the terms on which they could be practised without harm. Śivopādhyāya draws the claim wider than we do — from a worm up to Sadāśiva, no difference at all — and then narrows what may be done with it: contemplating means holding the knowledge, he says, and not sitting down to eat with a dog-cooker. Even he sets social bounds on conduct, and as a practical matter that may be sound: holding the knowledge that there is no difference anywhere does not by itself tell you how to behave. Yet the verse says no difference anywhere and qualifies nothing; the restriction is an import, and the particular sensibility it protects is his century's rather than ours.

Word by word

cit+dharmā — having consciousness as its nature (m. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi; the unstated subject, the self); sarva+deheṣu — in all bodies (m. loc. pl.); viśeṣaḥ — distinction, difference (m. nom. sg.); na — not; asti — is (3rd sg. pres.); kutracit — anywhere (indecl.); ataḥ — hence (indecl.); ca — and (indecl.); tat+mayam — made of that (n. acc. sg.); sarvam — everything (n. acc. sg.); bhāvayan — contemplating (pres. part., m. nom. sg.); bhava+jit — conqueror of becoming (m. nom. sg.); janaḥ — a person (m. nom. sg.)

Open portal 077 in the reader →

078Motionless amid the passions

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 101 · dhāraṇā 78 · Feeling · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

कामक्रोधलोभमोहमदमात्सर्यगोचरे

बुद्धिं निस्तिमितां कृत्वा तत् तत्त्वम् अवशिष्यते

kāmakrodhalobhamohamadamātsaryagocare
buddhiṃ nistimitāṃ kṛtvā tat tattvam avaśiṣyate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

In the field of desire; anger; greed; delusion; drunken pride; envystill the understanding utterly — that reality is what remains

Translation · colloquial

In the field of wanting, of anger, of greed, of confusion, of drunken pride, of envy,hold the understanding utterly still, and that reality is what is left standing.

Practice

You cannot schedule this one. It becomes available when desire, anger, greed, delusion, drunken pride, or envy is actually up. Śivopādhyāya explains why the book chooses these six: a passion at full strength has, of its own accord, already destroyed every other movement of the mind. The field is cleared for you. What remains is to use it. Do not flee the passion and do not feed it. In its very field, bring the understanding to a dead stop — no verdict, no story, no next move — and make it one-pointed. Śivopādhyāya splits the technique in two according to which passion it is. For anger and for doubt: the tortoise drawing in its limbs, a contraction that calms. For elation and for the urge to rush: the great expansion, spreading the movement wide until it pervades. Either way the passion spends itself, and what is left standing when it has passed is the reality.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya says why these six: a heightened passion of its own accord consists in the destruction of every other mental movement, so the field is already cleared. He gives two contrary techniques — the tortoise's drawing in of its limbs, for anger and doubt; the great expansion and pervasion, for elation and rushing. He calls this the śāmbhava ground rather than the śākta. And he adds a warning this edition does not repeat: non-yogins, he says, are simply deluded here.

Commentary

The six named here are the standard inventory of what wrecks a person, and what this verse does with them is neither of the two things traditions usually do. It does not ask you to leave the field, and it does not ask you to convert the energy into something better. It asks for the understanding to go dead still while the passion is at full strength, and to leave it standing. The difficulty is specific and worth naming: anger in particular does not arrive as raw force but with a case to make, a wholly convincing account of who was wrong. Nistimitā means not making the case — not refuting it either, since a refutation is another verdict. What is claimed is that the passion spends itself against that stillness and something remains standing when it has gone. Nobody can do this on demand at the beginning. It is worth attempting anyway, badly.

Word by word

kāma — desire (compound member); krodha — anger (compound member); lobha — greed (compound member); moha — delusion (compound member); mada — drunken pride (compound member); mātsarya — envy (compound member); gocare — in the field, the range [of these] (m. loc. sg., closing the compound); buddhim — the understanding (f. acc. sg.); nistimitām — utterly still, motionless (f. acc. sg.); kṛtvā — having made (gerund); tat — that (n. nom. sg.); tattvam — reality (n. nom. sg.); avaśiṣyate — remains, is left over (3rd sg. pres. pass.)

079The world as conjury

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 102 · dhāraṇā 79 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

इन्द्रजालमयं विश्वं व्यस्तं वा चित्रकर्मवत्

भ्रमद् वा ध्यायतः सर्वम् पश्यतश् च सुखोद्गमः

indrajālamayaṃ viśvaṃ vyastaṃ vā citrakarmavat
bhramad vā dhyāyataḥ sarvam paśyataś ca sukhodgamaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

The world is made of conjury — or spread out like a paintingor whirling — for one who contemplates and sees it all so, happiness rises

Translation · colloquial

The world is a conjuring trick, or else laid out flat like a painting,or else spinning; and for the one who holds it so and sees it so, happiness rises.

Practice

The verse offers three ways of seeing the world: as a conjuror's illusion, as a painting, or as whirling. Take whichever holds. Śivopādhyāya says what the whirling is — birth and everything after it — and gives the image for it: trees and mountains streaming past someone sitting in a boat. Nothing out there is going anywhere, and you are not moving either. What he calls the universe here is not just the objects but the whole arrangement of grasper and grasped. Meditate that way and see it all; happiness rises.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya glosses 'the universe' as the world consisting of the grasped and the grasper, and 'whirling' as birth and what follows it — like the trees and mountains that stream past someone riding in a boat.

Commentary

Indrajāla is not illusion in the philosophical sense — it is stage magic, the conjuror's show, and the difference matters. The verse is not saying the world is not there; it is saying the world is produced the way a trick is produced, with the appearance arriving whole and nothing solid behind it. It offers two alternatives with equal weight: a painting, spread out and perfectly still, or a whirling, all movement and no substance. Take whichever your eye can hold. That the text is indifferent between three incompatible pictures tells you it does not care about your metaphysics, only that the seeing be live — both participles in the last line, contemplating and seeing, the second stopping this from being a thought you have about the day afterwards. What rises is only sukha, happiness. The claim is small and it is exactly what people report.

Word by word

indrajāla+mayam — made of conjury, of a magician's show (n. nom. sg.; indrajāla, lit. 'Indra's net,' the stock word for stage-magic); viśvam — the world, the whole (n. nom. sg.); vyastam — spread out, displayed (past part., n. nom. sg.); vā — or (indecl.); citrakarma+vat — like a painting, like picture-work (indecl.); bhramat — whirling, wandering (pres. part., n. nom. sg.); vā — or (indecl.); dhyāyataḥ — of one contemplating (pres. part., m. gen. sg.); sarvam — all of it (n. acc. sg.); paśyataḥ — of one seeing (pres. part., m. gen. sg.); ca — and (indecl.); sukha+udgamaḥ — the rising of happiness (m. nom. sg.)

080Between pain and pleasure

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 103 · dhāraṇā 80 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

न चित्तं निक्षिपेद् दुःखे न सुखे वा परिक्षिपेत्

भैरवि ज्ञायतां मध्ये किं तत्त्वम् अवशिष्यते

na cittaṃ nikṣiped duḥkhe na sukhe vā parikṣipet
bhairavi jñāyatāṃ madhye kiṃ tattvam avaśiṣyate

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Do not set the mind down in pain; do not fling it about in pleasureBhairavī, let it be known — in madhya, what reality remains?

Translation · colloquial

Do not set the mind down in pain, and do not fling it about in pleasure;Bhairavi, let this be known: in the middle, what is real, and what is left?

Practice

Pain will come, and pleasure; the practice is the placement of the mind when they do. When it hurts, do not set the mind down into the hurt. When it delights, do not fling the mind out into the delight. Śivopādhyāya names what both amount to: laying the properties of the inner machinery over your own clear self, in the two forms I am happy and I am wretched. Stand in madhya — the centre between the two — and from there let the question be asked: what reality is left? Ask it live, each time; do not answer it from memory. Śivopādhyāya keeps the imperative pointed at you — investigate for yourself — and then says what the enquiry finds: the one consciousness that is both the witness of pleasure and pain and the thread connecting them. Take that as his report and not as your answer.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reads both halves of the instruction as one fault — superimposing the inner organ's properties on the pure self, as 'I am happy' and 'I am wretched'. He keeps the verse's question addressed to the reader, glossing it 'investigate for yourself', and then gives its outcome: what remains in the middle is the one principle of consciousness that both witnesses the two and connects them.

Commentary

Two different verbs, and the difference is a precise piece of psychology. Pain is what the mind is set down into, deposited, laid to rest in as though it were a place to live. Pleasure is what the mind is flung about after, scattered outward, chasing. Those are genuinely the two failures, and they are not the same failure with the sign reversed. Between them is madhya, the middle, the term this book has been building since its opening breath and which has now been applied to two objects, two breaths, two moments, and here to the whole affective life. What is unusual is the ending. Almost alone with the seventy-fourth portal, this one closes on a question rather than a claim, and the question is to be asked in the moment, with the pain or the pleasure still present. Answered from memory it is worth nothing.

Word by word

na — not; cittam — the mind (n. acc. sg.); nikṣipet — let one set [it] down, deposit (3rd sg. opt.); duḥkhe — in pain (n. loc. sg.); na — nor; sukhe — in pleasure (n. loc. sg.); vā — or (indecl.); parikṣipet — let one fling [it] about, scatter (3rd sg. opt.); bhairavi — O Bhairavī (f. voc. sg.); jñāyatām — let it be known (3rd sg. impv. pass.); madhye — in madhya, in the middle (loc. sg.); kim — what (n. nom. sg.); tattvam — reality (n. nom. sg.); avaśiṣyate — remains (3rd sg. pres. pass.)

Open portal 080 in the reader →

081I am everywhere

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 104 · dhāraṇā 81 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

विहाय निजदेहास्थां सर्वत्रास्मीति भावयन्

दृढेन मनसा दृष्ट्या नान्येक्षिण्या सुखी भवेत्

vihāya nijadehāsthāṃ sarvatrāsmīti bhāvayan
dṛḍhena manasā dṛṣṭyā nānyekṣiṇyā sukhī bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Leaving behind attachment to one's own body, contemplating 'I am everywhere'with mind firm, with a gaze that looks on nothing else, one becomes happy

Translation · colloquial

Let go of the hold your own body has on you, and hold instead that you are everywhere;with the mind firm, with a gaze that looks at nothing else, you become happy.

Practice

Sit with the mind made firm. Drop the standing assumption that you live inside this one body — Śivopādhyāya is exact about what goes: not the body, and not even attachment to it, but being the one who knows from inside it. Hold instead: I am everywhere. Not a picture of travelling about; an immediate placement, renewed each time it slackens, and that renewal is what the firm mind means. Keep the gaze single, resting on nothing else. Śivopādhyāya adds the one distinction that keeps this from being a phrase anyone can say all afternoon. What is being taken up is the full I — the one that has everything inside it, as Sadāśiva has. What is being put down is the limited I, and it is only that one, he says, that is to be shunned. He closes with a line from Tapasvirāja: this ghoul called 'I', whose fangs are mine-ness and who drinks the blood of people's courage, by the mere remembrance of you becomes a servant. It is not killed. It is put to work. The happiness follows the conviction; it is not held separately in view.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya heads this the taking up of the full I-ness and the abandoning of the I-ness invested in the body — and glosses the verse's 'attachment' as being the knowing subject in one's own body. 'I am everything' is to be held as Sadāśiva and Īśvara hold it, he says, and not as the Śiva who is pure consciousness alone. He then reports a real dispute: the author of the Tattvamañjarī denied even the universal I-feeling, siding with the Buddhists, who hold that where there is a self there is an other, and from that division come attachment and aversion. Against them he sets the Vimarśadīpikā, his own former teacher, and Śrī Hrasvanātha in the Advayasaṃpatti — Vāmananātha's Dvayasaṃpatti under its other name — and settles it in a phrase: it is the limited I-feeling that is to be shunned. He ends with Tapasvirāja: this ghoul called 'I', with its fangs of mine-ness, thirsty for the blood of people's courage — by the mere remembrance of you it becomes a handmaid.

Commentary

The trouble with this verse is that it is very easy to say and can be said all afternoon without anything happening. I am everywhere costs nothing as a sentence. What rescues it is the instrument the verse specifies — a firm mind — and firmness here does not mean strain but return: the contemplation slackens within seconds, and the practice is putting it back, and putting it back again, until the placement holds for longer than it takes to notice it. Note what is dropped first. Not the body, but the assumption of being lodged in it, which is a different and more precise target. And note the size of the promise, which is the same size as several of its neighbours: one becomes happy. This text is grandiose about its metaphysics and remarkably restrained about what it says will happen to you.

Word by word

vihāya — leaving behind (gerund); nija+deha+āsthām — attachment to one's own body (f. acc. sg.; KSTS nijadehāsthāṃ, which the vivṛti splits nije dehe āsthāṃ and glosses svadehe mamatāṃ, my-ness in one's own body — the proper object of vihāya. The e-text had nijadehasthaṃ); sarvatra — everywhere (indecl.); asmi — I am (1st sg. pres.); iti — thus, the content of the contemplation (indecl.); bhāvayan — contemplating (pres. part., m. nom. sg.); dṛḍhena — firm (n. inst. sg.); manasā — with mind (n. inst. sg.); dṛṣṭyā — with a gaze, a seeing (f. inst. sg.); na+anya+īkṣiṇyā — looking at nothing else (f. inst. sg.); sukhī — happy (m. nom. sg.); bhavet — one becomes (3rd sg. opt.)

Open portal 081 in the reader →

082No birthplace for knowing

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 105 · dhāraṇā 82 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

घटादौ यच् च विज्ञानम् इच्छाद्यं वा ममान्तरे

नैव सर्वगतं जातम् भावयन् इति सर्वगः

ghaṭādau yac ca vijñānam icchādyaṃ vā mamāntare
naiva sarvagataṃ jātam bhāvayan iti sarvagaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

The knowing of a jar and the like, or the desire and the rest within mewas never born at all — it has gone everywhere; contemplating so, one goes everywhere

Translation · colloquial

The knowing of a jar or anything like it, or the wanting and the rest of it inside me,was never born at all; it has gone everywhere. Hold it so, and you go everywhere.

Practice

Notice a knowing as it lights on some object — a jar, says the verse, anything at hand. Notice a desire stirring in you. Now contemplate: this knowing was not born in the jar, and this desire was not born in me; neither has a birthplace, because each is already everywhere. Hold that through the day's perceptions and wants. One who contemplates so goes everywhere with them. Śivopādhyāya draws out what 'already everywhere' costs. If the knowing is not mine, then the jar knows too, by way of my knowing — and everything from a worm up to Sadāśiva knows and wants by way of me, and the worm knows all of it back. He quotes Somānanda: the jar knows the knowable by way of me, and I by way of the jar; knowing his own self through the many existents, Śiva abides. That is what the contemplation is holding, and it is not a figure of speech.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reports two readings of this verse and rejects the first. Others, he says, take it that none of it ever came to be at all, contemplated as emptiness. His own is that the knowing is not within me alone — the particle 'at all' having slipped out of place — but has arisen all-pervading. What follows he states without softening: jars too know, by way of our knowing; everything from Sadāśiva down to a worm knows and desires by way of me, and the worm likewise knows all of them. He quotes Somānanda's Śivadṛṣṭi for it: the jar knows the knowable by way of me, and I by way of the jar; knowing his own self through the many existents, Śiva himself abides.

Commentary

This belongs to a short run — from the seventy-sixth portal to the eighty-third — that takes apart the ordinary account of perception one joint at a time. There, a knowing with no owner. Here, a knowing with no birthplace. Next, attention moved off both ends of the transaction and onto the join. What is elegant about this one is its symmetry: the usual account has knowing starting either out there in the jar or in here in me, and the verse denies both in the same breath, leaving the cognition with nowhere it began. The reason given is not that it is unreal but that it is already everywhere, which is a stronger and stranger claim. And it is to be held while perceiving and wanting, through an ordinary day of jars and wishes — not settled once and filed.

Word by word

ghaṭa+ādau — in a jar and the like (m. loc. sg.); yat — which (n. nom. sg.); ca — and (indecl.); vijñānam — the knowing, cognition (n. nom. sg.); icchā+ādyam — desire and the rest (n. nom. sg.); vā — or (indecl.); mama — my (gen. sg.); antare — within, inside (loc. sg.); na eva — not at all (indecl.; here negating local birth); sarva+gatam — gone everywhere, omnipresent (n. nom. sg.); jātam — born, arisen (past part., n. nom. sg.); bhāvayan — contemplating (pres. part., m. nom. sg.); iti — thus (indecl.); sarva+gaḥ — going everywhere, all-pervading (m. nom. sg.)

Open portal 082 in the reader →

083Where seer meets seen

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 106 · dhāraṇā 83 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

ग्राह्यग्राहकसंवित्तिः सामान्या सर्वदेहिनाम्

योगिनां तु विशेषो ऽस्ति सम्बन्धे सावधानता

grāhyagrāhakasaṃvittiḥ sāmānyā sarvadehinām
yogināṃ tu viśeṣo 'sti sambandhe sāvadhānatā

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Awareness of grasped and grasper is common to all embodied beingsbut for yogis there is a difference: attention held in the joining

Translation · colloquial

Awareness of what is grasped and of who grasps it is common to everyone with a body;for those who practise there is one difference: the attention is held in the joining.

Practice

Take any perception — a cup seen, a bird heard. Object and knower are given together in it; that pairing is common to every embodied being, from the highest to a worm, and needs no practice. The yogi's one difference is where attention rests: in the joining of the two. Śivopādhyāya says exactly what resting there consists of, and it is worth taking from him rather than working it out. In ordinary seeing the object is there and the one seeing has vanished into it. Attentiveness is not forgetting the one seeing. Keep the cup, and keep, at the same time and without turning to look at it, the fact that there is someone here it is appearing to. Hold both and you are in the seam. That is the whole of the practice, and it is available in every perception you will have today.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya makes the yogi's attentiveness precise: it is the not-forgetting of the grasper. The pairing of knower and known is common to everything from Sadāśiva down to a worm; the difference is only that the yogi does not lose the knowing subject while the object is present. Some manuscripts read the sentence so that the attentiveness too is common to all, which would remove the verse's distinction altogether.

Commentary

Here is the book's most compact definition of a yogi, and it says nothing about what a yogi experiences. Everyone has the pairing of knower and known. It comes free with being embodied, requires no training, and is going on in you now. The single difference named is where attention rests: not on the cup, not on the one seeing the cup, but on the meeting of the two. It is the madhya again in epistemological dress, the middle located this time in the seam of every perception you will have today. The word is sāvadhānatā, attentiveness — an ordinary one, the same you would use for a careful clerk. Śivopādhyāya makes it precise as the not-forgetting of the grasper, and this is where the commentary and experience come apart. Subjectivity can fade. There is sometimes no one at home; or what remains is not a knower standing off from a known but a single person, Bhairava and Bhairavī playing off one another. His not-forgetting names the practice well and describes its far end badly. What the yogi does not lose is better put as the joining itself, which was the verse's own word before the gloss tightened it.

Word by word

grāhya — the grasped, the object (compound member); grāhaka — the grasper, the knower (compound member); saṃvittiḥ — awareness [of both] (f. nom. sg.); sāmānyā — common, shared (f. nom. sg.); sarva+dehinām — of all embodied beings (m. gen. pl.); yoginām — of yogis (m. gen. pl.); tu — but (indecl.); viśeṣaḥ — a difference (m. nom. sg.); asti — there is (3rd sg. pres.); sambandhe — in the relation, the joining (m. loc. sg.); sa+avadhānatā — attentiveness, the keeping of attention (f. nom. sg.)

Open portal 083 in the reader →

084Awareness in another's body

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 107 · dhāraṇā 84 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

स्ववद् अन्यशरीरे ऽपि संवित्तिम् अनुभावयेत्

अपेक्षां स्वशरीरस्य त्यक्त्वा व्यापी दिनैर् भवेत्

svavad anyaśarīre 'pi saṃvittim anubhāvayet
apekṣāṃ svaśarīrasya tyaktvā vyāpī dinair bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

As in oneself, so in another's body — let one feel the awareness therelet go of regard for one's own body — within days one becomes pervading

Translation · colloquial

As in yourself, so in another's body: feel out the awareness that is there,and let go of your regard for your own body; within days you spread through everything.

Practice

Choose another body — a person across the room, an animal. Feel the sentience in it as directly as you feel your own; the verse asks for felt experience, not inference or imagining. The paired move: let regard for your own body go — its comfort, its claim to be where you live. The verse gives its own timescale: kept up over days, this widens you until you pervade. Śivopādhyāya spends more space on this portal than on any other, and his help is to make the second move first. Notice that consciousness has never in fact depended on a body. It persists in dream when the gross body has dropped away, and in dreamless sleep when even the subtle one has — otherwise, he asks, if someone put the question 'who slept?' to you, what would you answer? Three bodies come and go; the one that knows them does not. Once that has been seen rather than granted, feeling the sentience in another body is not an extension of your reach but the removal of a restriction, and the verse's few days are not a training period. And in the highest sense, he says, 'another's body' means every body: the whole world is my own body, and not this wretched scrap of one.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

This portal draws Śivopādhyāya's longest comment. He argues that dependence on a body was never a fact: consciousness runs continuously through waking, dream, deep sleep and the fourth state, while the gross, subtle and causal bodies each drop away in turn — otherwise, he asks, if the question 'who slept?' were put to you, what would your answer be? He reports the practice being done on dead bodies as well as living ones, with entry into them for the sake of play, and the powers of mastery and of attaining whatever is wished as its fruit in the living; the yogin's own wish, he says, is the only measure. Then he turns it around: in the highest sense 'another's body' means the whole of the knowable, felt as one's own limbs — the entire world is my body, and not this wretched scrap of one. He quotes the Virūpākṣapañcāśikā: one who has stepped out of the universe-body into a single frame is the living dead, breath rattling in the throat. And he ends by affirming, without hedging, that in just a few days one becomes all-pervading.

Commentary

The practice form of what the seventy-seventh portal asserted. And it is worth being clear about what it is not: not loving-kindness, not wishing another well, not imagining their circumstances. The verb asks for felt experience of the sentience in that body, as directly as you have your own — a stranger and harder thing than compassion, and it does not require you to like the person. The second half is what makes it work and what makes it hard. Regard for your own body has to be let go in the same movement, because as long as one body is the place you live from, another's sentience can only be inferred. The timescale is given only here and at the twenty-eighth portal: within days. Śivopādhyāya affirms it without a hedge, and our earlier doubt was aimed at the wrong thing. Days is not implausible for the state — the felt boundary of a body can go in an afternoon, and this practice is built to take it. What does not arrive in days is the settling in which it stops coming back. He points at that himself when he turns the verse around: in the highest sense another's body means the whole of the knowable, felt as one's own limbs — the entire world is my body, and not this wretched scrap of one.

Word by word

sva+vat — as in oneself (indecl.); anya+śarīre — in another's body (n. loc. sg.); api — too, even (indecl.); saṃvittim — the awareness, sentience (f. acc. sg.); anubhāvayet — let one bring [it] into felt experience (3rd sg. opt. caus.); apekṣām — regard, concern (f. acc. sg.); sva+śarīrasya — for one's own body (n. gen. sg.); tyaktvā — having given up (gerund); vyāpī — pervading (m. nom. sg.); dinaiḥ — within days (m. inst. pl., time within which); bhavet — one becomes (3rd sg. opt.)

Open portal 084 in the reader →

085The mind without support

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 108 · dhāraṇā 85 · Awareness · śāmbhavopāya · no means · causal

निराधारं मनः कृत्वा विकल्पान् न विकल्पयेत्

तदात्मपरमात्मत्वे भैरवो मृगलोचने

nirādhāraṃ manaḥ kṛtvā vikalpān na vikalpayet
tadātmaparamātmatve bhairavo mṛgalocane

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Make the mind supportless — form no dividing thoughtsthen, the self being the highest self — Bhairava, deer-eyed one

Translation · colloquial

Make the mind stand on nothing, and make no dividing thoughts at all;then this self, being the highest self, is Bhairava, deer-eyed one.

Practice

Sit and take every support away from the mind: no image, no breath to follow, no word to lean on. Śivopādhyāya adds the half that is easy to miss — the inner supports go too, not only the outer ones. When a dividing thought starts to form, do not form it; let the construction stop unbuilt. What has to be declined is small: he calls it the sliver of construing, and says that being a separate person consists in precisely that sliver, while the constructless condition is brahman itself. The verse names what remains when the mind stands nowhere and no construct is built: the self as the highest self — Bhairava. Return there whenever a prop reappears.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya glosses 'supportless' as having abandoned both outer and inner support — the pair to the portal ten back, which he classified as contemplation with a support. What is to be declined he calls the sliver of construing: by that sliver, individuality; by the constructless condition, brahman-hood.

Commentary

Half a dozen portals have arrived at a supportless mind by some means — a frozen gaze, a body imagined hanging in nothing, the last of a breathed-out visarga, a self opened to the shape of the sky. This one asks for it directly and offers nothing to do it with. That is what puts it in the third kind of means: there is no method here, only the condition, and either it is available or the verse has nothing to give you today. The Sanskrit does something in the second half that a translation can only approximate — do not construct the constructs, the noun and the verb from one root. The instruction is placed at the moment of forming rather than after it. Not the removal of a thought that has arrived, which is another thought, but declining to build the one now assembling itself.

Word by word

nirādhāram — without support (n. acc. sg. adj.); manaḥ — the mind (n. acc. sg.); kṛtvā — having made (gerund, √kṛ); vikalpān — dividing thoughts, mental constructs (m. acc. pl.); na — not; vikalpayet — should construct (3rd sg. opt. caus., vi-√kḷp; cognate accusative with vikalpān); tadā — then; ātma+paramātmatve — in the [state of] the self being the highest self (n. loc. sg.); bhairavaḥ — Bhairava (m. nom. sg.); mṛga+locane — O deer-eyed one (f. voc. sg.)

Open portal 085 in the reader →

086I am that very one

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 109 · dhāraṇā 86 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

सर्वज्ञः सर्वकर्ता च व्यापकः परमेश्वरः

स एवाहं शैवधर्मा इति दार्ढ्याद् भवेच् छिवः

sarvajñaḥ sarvakartā ca vyāpakaḥ parameśvaraḥ
sa evāhaṃ śaivadharmā iti dārḍhyād bhavec chivaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

All-knowing, doer of all, pervading everything: the highest lord —that very one am I, of Śiva's nature — held firm, one becomes Śiva

Translation · colloquial

All-knowing, doer of everything, spread through everything: the highest lord.That very one am I, of the same nature; hold it firm, and you become Shiva.

Practice

This is a conviction held, not an image seen. Take the sentence the verse gives — the one who knows everything, does everything, fills everything, the highest lord: that very one is what I am — and hold it. Śivopādhyāya states it in both directions: he alone am I, I alone am he. And he says what holding it firmly means: holding it with no doubt in it. Not as a mood but with firmness, the way you hold a fact. Return to it whenever it slackens. The verse says the becoming follows from the firmness itself. He also says this is only half. The recognition has two parts — I am that, and this world is my own expansion — and the second is the next portal. Keep them together, and the claim being made here stops sounding like a claim about oneself.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya calls this the śākta ground, and glosses 'of Śiva's nature' as having Śiva's own properties, transparency and autonomy. He states the identity both ways round — he alone am I, I alone am he — and takes 'held firm' to mean held with no doubt in it. He also says this verse and the next are the two halves of one recognition: I am that, and this world is my own expansion.

Commentary

The boldest sentence in the book, and its predicates repay attention. All-knowing, pervading everything — those any non-dual tradition would allow. But the second is doer of all, and identity with an agent is precisely what a Vedāntin would not affirm of the absolute, which knows and pervades but does not act. This school's highest reality does act, and what you are asked to claim identity with includes the acting. That is not a small difference, and it is why this tradition's liberated person is free within the world rather than free of it. The method is one word: dārḍhyāt, from firmness. Not from insight, not from grace, not from having earned it — from the holding, kept up and returned to. The verse is content to say that a conviction held firmly enough stops being a conviction.

Word by word

sarvajñaḥ — all-knowing (m. nom. sg.); sarva+kartā — doer of all (m. nom. sg.); ca — and; vyāpakaḥ — pervading [all] (m. nom. sg.); parama+īśvaraḥ — the highest lord (m. nom. sg.); saḥ — that one (m. nom. sg.); eva — very, precisely; aham — I (nom. sg.); śaiva+dharmā — having Śiva's attributes (m. nom. sg., bahuvrīhi in -dharman); iti — thus [closing the held conviction]; dārḍhyāt — from firmness (n. abl. sg., ablative of cause); śivaḥ — Śiva (m. nom. sg.; KSTS orders the pāda dārḍhyād bhavec chivaḥ where the e-text had dārḍhyāc chivo bhavet — same sense, and both scan as pathyā); bhavet — one becomes (3rd sg. opt., √bhū)

Open portal 086 in the reader →

087Waves of one water

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 110 · dhāraṇā 87 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

जलस्येवोर्मयो वह्नेर् ज्वालाभङ्ग्यः प्रभा रवेः

ममैव भैरवस्यैता विश्वभङ्ग्यो विभेदिताः

jalasyevormayo vahner jvālābhaṅgyaḥ prabhā raveḥ
mamaiva bhairavasyaitā viśvabhaṅgyo vibheditāḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

As waves to water; flickers of flame to fire; light to the sun —so these waves of the world, spread into difference, are mine alone — Bhairava's

Translation · colloquial

As waves are to water, as flickers are to fire, as light is to the sun,so these waves of a world, spread out into differences, are mine alone, and I am Bhairava.

Practice

Watch anything that moves or shows itself — a bird, traffic, your own thoughts rising. Say inwardly what the verse says: this is a wave, and a wave is not other than its water. The verse speaks in Bhairava's own voice, and that voice says mine — meaning the I that you are. Let each thing you notice fold back into the one it belongs to. Śivopādhyāya insists this is the second half of a pair, and that the pair matters. The portal before claims: I am that. This one claims: and all of this is my own expansion. Take them together, and he draws the consequence that saves this from being a rule against thinking — even the making of dividing thoughts, he says, is my own expansion, whose mark is autonomy. So the thoughts rising are not an exception to what you are watching. They are the clearest case of it.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya treats this verse and the one before as one recognition in two parts — I am that, and this expansion is mine — and grows unusually hot with those who read the second line otherwise, calling them self-styled scholars and fools for not seeing that the Lord taught two verses for two separate practices. He quotes Abhinavagupta on what 'I am that' means: not some separate self, but that which is of the nature of light, and no other. And he draws the consequence plainly: even the making of dividing thoughts is my own expansion, whose mark is autonomy.

Commentary

Three images for one relation — waves and water, flickers and fire, light and sun — and all three are chosen so that the appearance is fully real. A wave is not a mistake about water. This is the corrective to any reading of the seventy-ninth portal that turns the conjuror's show into unreality: nothing here is being dismissed, only re-attributed. Note who is speaking. The verse is in Bhairava's own voice and says mine, and the practitioner is asked to take that sentence up in the first person — this is the recognition on which the whole Kashmiri school is named. The very last verse of the book will use the same figure once more, the sun reflected in water, to make the final and slightly different point: a real reflection of a real sun, in the wrong place.

Word by word

jalasya — of water (n. gen. sg.); iva — as, like; ūrmayaḥ — waves (f. nom. pl.); vahneḥ — of fire (m. gen. sg.); jvālā+bhaṅgyaḥ — flickerings of flame (f. nom. pl.); prabhā — light, radiance (f. nom. sg.); raveḥ — of the sun (m. gen. sg.); mama — of me (gen. sg.); eva — alone, indeed; bhairavasya — of Bhairava (m. gen. sg.); etāḥ — these (f. nom. pl.); viśva+bhaṅgyaḥ — waves of the universe (f. nom. pl.); vibheditāḥ — set apart, spread into difference (f. nom. pl., past part. caus., vi-√bhid)

Open portal 087 in the reader →

088Whirling until you fall

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 111 · dhāraṇā 88 · Body · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → causal

भ्रान्त्वा भ्रान्त्वा शरीरेण त्वरितम् भुवि पातनात्

क्षोभशक्तिविरामेण परा संजायते दशा

bhrāntvā bhrāntvā śarīreṇa tvaritam bhuvi pātanāt
kṣobhaśaktivirāmeṇa parā saṃjāyate daśā

Translation · literal Sanskrit

From whirling and whirling with the body till it falls swiftly to the groundwith the śakti of agitation come to rest, the supreme state is born

Translation · colloquial

From whirling and whirling with the body until it drops fast to the ground,when the power that was churning comes to rest, the highest state is born.

Practice

Choose soft ground with nothing to strike. Spin with the whole body — keep going past the point of control — until you drop. The fall is part of the method, not an accident to avoid. Lie where you fell. The spinning has roused a live force in the body, and that force has an end. Attend to the moment it runs out: the verse locates the supreme state exactly there. Śivopādhyāya reads the whirling less wildly, and his reading is the one to use if spinning is unwise for you. He takes it as the worshipper going round and round the shrine and its offerings, or the walker whose body is fit from roaming the roads day and night. Circle something, steadily and long enough that the body is carrying its own momentum; then let yourself down. What ceases, on his reading, is not agitation of mind but the sheer impetus of the moving body, still running on after it has stopped. Attend to that running out.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya sets the whirling in a worshipper's circumambulation of the shrine and its offerings, or in the momentum of a hard-walking traveller fit from the roads, rather than in ecstatic spinning. And the force that comes to rest he calls the force of momentum — the body's impetus, still running on after it has come to the ground, and then running out.

Commentary

The same architecture as the breath forced and released, the bindu watched out, the long vowel let go — make something, then attend to its ending — but here the thing made is a whirlwind in the whole organism, and the ending is a collapse. The verse is honest that the fall is method rather than accident. It is also the second physically dangerous portal in this book, and we would say more than it does: soft ground, nothing within reach to strike, nothing to fall from, and for anyone past middle age or unsteady this one is best admired rather than performed. The Mevlevi turning is a different discipline and no borrowing is being claimed either way — that turning is sustained, controlled and does not end on the floor. What this verse wants is specifically the moment the roused agitation runs out.

Word by word

bhrāntvā bhrāntvā — having whirled and whirled (gerund, √bhram, doubled for continued repetition); śarīreṇa — with the body (n. instr. sg.); tvaritam — swiftly (adv.); bhuvi — on the ground (f. loc. sg.); pātanāt — from the falling (n. abl. sg., ablative of cause); kṣobha+śakti+virāmeṇa — through the coming-to-rest of the force of agitation (m. instr. sg.); parā — supreme (f. nom. sg.); saṃjāyate — is born, arises (3rd sg. pres. mid., sam-√jan); daśā — state, condition (f. nom. sg.)

Open portal 088 in the reader →

089The end of the churning

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 112 · dhāraṇā 89 · Awareness · śāmbhavopāya · no means · causal

आधारेष्व् अथवा ऽशक्त्या ऽज्ञानाच् चित्तलयेन वा

जातशक्तिसमावेशक्षोभान्ते भैरवं वपुः

ādhāreṣv athavā 'śaktyā 'jñānāc cittalayena vā
jātaśaktisamāveśakṣobhānte bhairavaṃ vapuḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Or when the supports fail one, or through unknowing, the mind dissolves away —when the churning of absorption into Anāśritā, the śakti so born, is at its end — Bhairava's body

Translation · colloquial

Or the thing you were leaning on gives way, or you simply do not know — and the mind comes undone.Something has been roused that leans on nothing. When its churning quiets, what stands there is Bhairava's own body.

Practice

This verse describes what happens, not what you perform, and Śivopādhyāya makes the shape of it plain: two ways in, one result. Either the props give out — you cannot hold the thing you were using — or you simply do not know, the knowing is not there. Either way the mind dissolves. What is roused he names: Anāśritā, the power that rests on nothing, which is why nothing you had been resting on was needed. Her absorption comes with a wavering, and the wavering has an end. When it stops, do not reach for the method again. Stay in the settling: what stands there is Bhairava's form, and it is the bliss of your own experience, not a new thing arriving.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

He names the power that is roused: शक्तौ अनाश्रिताख्यायां, ‘in the śakti called Anāśritā’ — the power that rests on nothing, which is why nothing you had been resting on was needed (plate 0106). He also makes the verse two causes with one result — failure of the props, or unknowing — rather than three parallel routes.

Commentary

The most generous verse in the collection, and the one to give anyone who has decided they cannot meditate. Everything it lists is a failure: the supports gave out, you could not manage the method, you did not understand what you were doing, or the mind simply melted and none of it was yours. And the verse says a śakti was roused all the same, and that its churning has an end, and that the end is the door. Structurally this is what half the book has been teaching — attend to the finish, not the thing — applied now to the finish of an attempt rather than of a breath or a note. The instruction is only not to reach for the method again when it collapses. Sit in the settling of a practice that did not work, and what stands there, the verse says, is Bhairava's own form.

Word by word

ādhāreṣu — with regard to the supports (m. loc. pl.); athavā — or else; aśaktyā — through incapacity (f. instr. sg.; avagraha marks the privative); ajñānāt — through unknowing (n. abl. sg.; avagraha marks the privative); citta+layena — through the dissolution of the mind (m. instr. sg.); vā — or; jāta+śakti+samāveśa+kṣobha+ante — at the end (anta, loc. sg.) of the agitation (kṣobha) of the absorption (samāveśa) into the śakti that has arisen (jāta; the received line names that śakti Anāśritā. The name is Śivopādhyāya's identification of which śakti is meant, not the compound's — nothing in the Sanskrit supplies it); bhairavam — belonging to Bhairava (n. nom. sg. adj.); vapuḥ — form, body (n. nom. sg.)

090The eyes held still

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 113 · dhāraṇā 90 · Body · śāmbhavopāya · no means · causal

सम्प्रदायम् इमम् देवि शृणु सम्यग् वदाम्य् अहम्

कैवल्यं जायते सद्यो नेत्रयोः स्तब्धमात्रयोः

sampradāyam imam devi śṛṇu samyag vadāmy aham
kaivalyaṃ jāyate sadyo netrayoḥ stabdhamātrayoḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Hear this transmission, Goddess — I speak it in full:aloneness is born at once — the two eyes no sooner stilled

Translation · colloquial

Hear this handed-down teaching, Goddess; I speak it out in full:aloneness is born at once, the two eyes no sooner gone still.

Practice

Let the eyes stop. Not a stare at something — an arrest of the eyes themselves: lids open, gaze unmoving, focused nowhere. The verse calls this a handed-down teaching and gives no preparation and no stages. Hold the stillness of the two eyes and nothing else. What it promises is immediate: aloneness — awareness standing single, no longer paired with any object.

Commentary

The only verse in the hundred and twelve that announces itself — hear this transmission, I speak it in full — and having done so it offers no preparation, no stages and no support. Just stop the eyes. The distinction between this and staring is the whole practice: a stare is fixed on something, and here nothing is being looked at, the gaze arrested and focused nowhere. It is harder than it sounds, because eyes never actually stop; they make small involuntary jumps continuously, and what is being asked for is not muscular but a stopping of the looking. One word here is borrowed. Kaivalya, aloneness, is the goal of the Sāṃkhya and Yoga schools and not native to this one, where the aim is usually fullness rather than isolation. It appears here for what it names precisely: awareness standing single, no longer paired with an object.

Word by word

sampradāyam — the transmitted teaching (m. acc. sg.); imam — this (m. acc. sg.); devi — O Goddess (f. voc. sg.); śṛṇu — hear (2nd sg. impv., √śru); samyak — rightly, in full (adv., with vadāmi); vadāmi — I speak (1st sg. pres., √vad); aham — I (nom. sg.); kaivalyam — aloneness, the isolation [of awareness] (n. nom. sg.); jāyate — is born (3rd sg. pres. mid., √jan); sadyaḥ — at once (adv.); netrayoḥ — the two eyes (n. loc. du.); stabdha+mātrayoḥ — merely stilled, no sooner fixed (loc. du.; locative absolute with netrayoḥ)

Open portal 090 in the reader →

091Sound with no letters

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 114 · dhāraṇā 91 · Sound · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle → causal

संकोचं कर्णयोः कृत्वा ह्य् अधोद्वारे तथैव च

अनच्कम् अहलं ध्यायन् विशेद् ब्रह्म सनातनम्

saṃkocaṃ karṇayoḥ kṛtvā hy adhodvāre tathaiva ca
anackam ahalaṃ dhyāyan viśed brahma sanātanam

Translation · literal Sanskrit

The two ears closed, and the lower door likewise,meditating on what has no vowel, no consonant, one enters the everlasting brahman

Translation · colloquial

The two ears closed, and the lower door closed in just the same way,meditate on what has no vowel and no consonant, and you enter what is everlasting.

Practice

Stop the ears — press them closed — and draw the lower door, the opening at the base of the trunk, shut with the same firmness. Śivopādhyāya gives the way it was done: press it with your own heel, the way the seated posture sets the heel beneath you. In the sealed quiet a sound remains that no mouth could say: no vowel in it, no consonant. Meditate on that unspellable resonance. The verse says it is the way in, and that what you enter is the everlasting brahman.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya takes the lower door to be the anal aperture, pressed shut with one's own heel. He glosses 'ahala' as free of consonants, and calls the form an Aiśa reading — the Lord's own usage, the tradition's name for the scriptures' irregular Sanskrit. Of the sound itself he says only that the rest is plain.

Commentary

The body is sealed at both ends of the trunk, ears and lower door, which is the ordinary way anyone finds an inner sound. The two negations in the third line are technical jokes. Ac and hal are the grammarians' shorthand for the vowels and the consonants — between them the entire alphabet, which in this school is the entire manifest world. So a sound with neither is not merely wordless; it is what is left when everything sayable has been subtracted, and the verse names it by taking away Sanskrit itself. This closes the sound sequence that ran through the silence after OM, the letter with no ending, and the breath at the close of a syllable. Honesty requires one observation: what you hear with the ears blocked is at first your own circulation and the noise a nervous system makes with nothing to listen to. Every tradition that works with unstruck sound begins there. Whether what the verse points at is that noise attended to differently, or something else that appears behind it, we do not know and will not pretend to.

Word by word

saṃkocam — contraction, closing (m. acc. sg.); karṇayoḥ — of the two ears (m. gen. du.); kṛtvā — having made (gerund, √kṛ); hi — indeed; adhas+dvāre — at the lower door (n. loc. sg.); tathā eva ca — and in the same way; anackam — without vowel (n. acc. sg.; ac = the vowels in the grammarians' shorthand); ahalam — without consonant (n. acc. sg.; hal = the consonants); dhyāyan — meditating on (m. nom. sg. pres. part., √dhyai); viśet — one should enter (3rd sg. opt., √viś); brahma — brahman (n. acc. sg.); sanātanam — everlasting (n. acc. sg.)

Open portal 091 in the reader →

092Looking down a deep well

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 115 · dhāraṇā 92 · Space · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → causal

कूपादिके महागर्ते स्थित्वोपरि निरीक्षणात्

अविकल्पमतेः सम्यक् सद्यश् चित्तलयः स्फुटम्

kūpādike mahāgarte sthitvopari nirīkṣaṇāt
avikalpamateḥ samyak sadyaś cittalayaḥ sphuṭam

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Standing beside a well or any great chasm, gazing down from above,for one whose mind forms no dividing thought — at once, wholly, unmistakably — the mind dissolves

Translation · colloquial

Stand at the edge of a well, or any long drop, and look down.If no dividing thought forms, the mind goes — all at once, all of it, past mistaking.

Practice

Find a real drop — a well, a gorge, a mountain edge with the ground far below. Śivopādhyāya is clear that you stand beside it, not over it: the Sanskrit locative here is the one that means near, as in 'the cows lie down at the banyan'. From there, look down into the depth with an unmoving eye — without naming it, measuring it, or telling yourself anything about it. Kept free of thought and held on that depth, the mind does not thin out gradually; he says the quieting is certain, and immediate. Stay with the gone-ness rather than the vertigo.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reads the locative as one of proximity — one stands beside the chasm, not over it — and cites the grammarians' stock example, 'the cows lie down at the banyan'. 'And the like' takes in a mountain peak as well as a well. He makes the second line's condition explicit: it holds for one of whom it is true that his mind is such that no dividing thought exists in it, which he notes is a bahuvrīhi with a bahuvrīhi inside it. What follows at that instant he calls, more modestly than the verse, the quieting of the mind — and he keeps the verse's certainty entire.

Commentary

This is the only portal that recruits a reflex you cannot override. A real drop under your eyes stops the mind because a very old part of the nervous system takes the controls, and the verse is using that seizure as the method. Read it beside the portal three doors along, which takes terror and hunger and the moment before a sneeze as they arrive: this one manufactures the same suspension on purpose. Two cautions. Brace yourself against something solid, and do not do this alone at a real edge — the practice wants the depth, not the risk. And read the fourth word of the second line as a condition rather than a promise: it works for one whose mind forms no dividing thought, which is precisely the capacity a beginner does not have, and without it a chasm produces only vertigo and a good story. The verse is unusually emphatic — at once, wholly, unmistakably — and that pile of certainty is worth noticing in a book that mostly says one becomes happy.

Word by word

kūpa+ādike — at a well or the like (m. loc. sg.); mahā+garte — at a great pit, chasm (m. loc. sg.); sthitvā — having taken one's stand (gerund, √sthā); upari — above; nirīkṣaṇāt — from gazing down, fixed looking (n. abl. sg., ablative of cause); avikalpa+mateḥ — for one whose mind is without dividing thought (f. gen. sg.); samyak — completely (adv.); sadyaḥ — at once (adv.; text sadyas); citta+layaḥ — dissolution of the mind (m. nom. sg.); sphuṭam — clearly, unmistakably (adv.)

093Wherever the mind goes

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 116 · dhāraṇā 93 · Awareness · śāmbhavopāya · no means · causal

यत्र यत्र मनो याति बाह्ये वाभ्यन्तरे ऽपि वा

तत्र तत्र शिवावस्था व्यापकत्वात् क्व यास्यति

yatra yatra mano yāti bāhye vābhyantare 'pi vā
tatra tatra śivāvasthā vyāpakatvāt kva yāsyati

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Wherever the mind goes, to the outer or even to the inner,right there is the state of Śiva. Since he pervades all, where will it go?

Translation · colloquial

Wherever the mind goes, outward or even inward,right there is Shiva's own state. He is everywhere; where would it go?

Practice

Do not rein the mind in. Let it go where it goes — out to a sound or a worry, in to a memory or an ache — and each time it lands, notice that what it has landed on is already the state of Śiva. There is no wrong destination; nothing lies outside what pervades everything. Ask the verse's own question of each wandering: where could it go? Śivopādhyāya answers it as flatly as the verse does — nowhere at all — because space, time and form shine only by having light for their substance, and that light is not cut off anywhere. He also says where this portal stands, and it is worth knowing before taking it up. This is not one practice among the hundred and twelve; it is the seventh samādhi, the constant one, which depends on no effort and is reached by the ripening of six that do. And he quotes the tradition's own version of this verse, which states the conditions the verse leaves silent: when identification with the body has melted away, and the highest Self is known — then wherever the mind goes, there are samādhis. Until then the wandering is only wandering, and there is other work.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Here Śivopādhyāya keeps a promise made sixty portals earlier. This state, he says, is what the Yogaśāstra calls the constant samādhi, of the nature of direct realisation and dependent on no effort — the seventh, reached by the ripening of six that come before it. He enumerates them from the Vākyasudhā: samādhi with construction threaded with the seen and threaded with the word, and samādhi without construction, like a lamp in a windless place — each practised first inwardly in the heart, then on an outer support such as sun, moon or sky. With these six, that text says, let one pass the time unceasingly. And it carries this verse's own twin, which supplies the conditions this one leaves silent: when identification with the body has melted away and the highest Self is known, then wherever the mind goes, there are samādhis.

Commentary

The most quoted verse in the collection, and the one that appears to dissolve it. If every place the mind lands is already the state, then nothing in the preceding hundred and ninety verses was ever necessary. The argument closes not with an instruction but with a rhetorical question answered by a word of grammar — because he pervades, nowhere cut off. There is nowhere else. It is also the most easily abused verse here, and the tradition supplies its own reply, which is better than any warning of ours. Śivopādhyāya places this state seventh: the constant samādhi, dependent on no effort, reached by the ripening of six that depend on effort entirely. And he quotes a near-twin of the verse, which states the conditions this one leaves silent — when identification with the body has melted away and the highest Self is known, then wherever the mind goes, there are samādhis. So the preceding verses were not made unnecessary. They are the ladder, and this describes standing off it; the effortlessness is the fruit of effort and not its refutation. Until then the wandering is only wandering, and there is other work.

Word by word

yatra yatra — wherever (doubled for distribution); manaḥ — the mind (n. nom. sg.); yāti — goes (3rd sg. pres., √yā); bāhye — to the outer (loc. sg.); vā — or; abhyantare — to the inner (loc. sg.); api vā — or even; tatra tatra — right there, in each such place; śiva+avasthā — the state of Śiva (f. nom. sg.; KSTS śivāvasthā, glossed avasthā avasthānam and required by the pathyā cadence — the e-text's śivāvāsthā is not a word); vyāpakatvāt — because of [his] all-pervadingness (n. abl. sg., ablative of cause); kva — where? (interrogative); yāsyati — will it go (3rd sg. fut., √yā)
