094Awareness at the sense doors

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 117 · dhāraṇā 94 · Body · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

यत्र यत्राक्षमार्गेण चैतन्यं व्यज्यते विभोः

तस्य तन्मात्रधर्मित्वाच् चिल्लयाद् भरितात्मता

yatra yatrākṣamārgeṇa caitanyaṃ vyajyate vibhoḥ
tasya tanmātradharmitvāc cillayād bharitātmatā

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Wherever the consciousness of the all-pervading one shows itself along the road of a sense,for its nature is that alone — by dissolving into awareness, the self is filled full

Translation · colloquial

Wherever the awareness of the one who fills everything shows itself along the road of a sense,since that alone is its nature, melt into awareness there, and you are filled full.

Practice

Catch consciousness in the act of showing itself — at the eye as seeing, at the ear as hearing, at the skin as touch. Śivopādhyāya takes the verse's 'road of a sense' as the physical aperture: the hollow of the eye, and the rest. What appears along each such road is not private property; it is the awareness of the one who pervades all, and it is nothing but that. He gives the reason: were the blue anything in excess of consciousness, it could not be known at all — as the many-coloured reflections in a mirror are nothing but the mirror. Let the perceiving mind dissolve into that bare awareness at the very door where it flashed — into consciousness itself, which he calls the wall the world is painted on. Fullness of self is its settling. And he gives the image to hold while doing it: as the orb of the sun is nothing but light become dense, so consciousness, congealed, shines in the form of the world.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya takes 'the road of a sense' to be the physical aperture — the hollow of the eye and the rest — and settles the verse's difficult word: the blue has consciousness alone for its property, since anything in excess of consciousness could not be known at all, as the many-coloured reflections are nothing but the mirror. What one dissolves into he calls the wall the world is painted on. And he closes with the tradition's image: just as the orb of the sun is nothing but light become dense, so consciousness itself, congealed, shines in the form of the world.

Commentary

A word here misleads anyone who knows the other school. Tanmātra is the Sāṃkhya term for the subtle elements behind the senses, but it is not doing that work in this verse — here it is just that alone, the point being that what shows up at a sense door is nothing other than the pervading one's own awareness. Note the verb again: it is made manifest, passive, the same grammar of disclosure that has run through this book wherever something important arrives. Nothing is being shut, unlike the blocked senses of the sixty-sixth portal, and nothing is being attended to for its own sake. The catch is made at the door itself, in the flash of seeing before it becomes a seen thing. And the result-word is worth holding beside its neighbour: fullness of self, this school's preferred vocabulary, three verses before another portal will call the same destination a dwelling of the void. The book uses both languages and never troubles to reconcile them.

Word by word

yatra yatra — wherever (doubled); akṣa+mārgeṇa — by the pathway of a sense-organ (m. instr. sg.); caitanyam — consciousness (n. nom. sg.); vyajyate — is made manifest (3rd sg. pres. pass., vi-√añj); vibhoḥ — of the all-pervading [lord] (m. gen. sg.); tasya — of that [manifested consciousness] (gen. sg.); tat+mātra+dharmitvāt — because its nature is that alone (n. abl. sg., ablative of cause); cit+layāt — through dissolving into awareness (m. abl. sg.); bharita+ātmatā — the condition of a self filled full (f. nom. sg.)

Open portal 094 in the reader →

095Sneeze, fright, and hunger

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 118 · dhāraṇā 95 · Feeling · śāmbhavopāya · no means · causal

क्षुताद्यन्ते भये शोके गह्वरे वा रणाद् द्रुते

कुतूहलेक्षुधाद्यन्ते ब्रह्मसत्तामयी दशा

kṣutādyante bhaye śoke gahvare vā raṇād drute
kutūhalekṣudhādyante brahmasattāmayī daśā

Translation · literal Sanskrit

At the start and end of a sneeze; in terror; in grief; over a chasm; fleeing a battle;in sharp curiosity; at the onset of hunger and at its end — there, made of brahman's own being, is the state

Translation · colloquial

At the start of a sneeze and at its end; in terror; in grief; over a chasm; running from a battle;in sharp curiosity; when hunger comes on and when it goes: there, made of being itself, is the state.

Practice

Nothing here is performed; these states arrive. A sneeze gathers and lets go. Fear jolts, grief hollows, a drop yawns underfoot, you run, curiosity seizes you, hunger bites and is fed. At the raw verge of each — before the story about it forms — being itself stands open. Your work is availability: know the list, and when one of these takes you, fall toward the gap instead of past it. Śivopādhyāya's word for what arrives is occasion. On each such occasion, he says, the one who is awake enters; and he does not soften what he says about the one who is not. So the preparation is not for the sneeze. It is everything else you do.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reports a variant in which brahman's being does not compose the state but stands close by — right at hand — in each of these moments. He calls each of them an occasion, and says the well-awakened one, having considered this, enters at it, while the unawakened is here simply a fool. He names the ground: this is the ground of Śambhu.

Commentary

The most anthologised list in the book and the most misread. Nothing in it is performed: a sneeze, a fright, a grief, the pull at the edge of a drop, a rout, a curiosity that seizes you, the bite of hunger and its relief. Every one is involuntary, and the instruction is not to arrange them but to be awake when they arrive. What they share is a seam — a moment in which the ordinary machinery of wanting and knowing is knocked out of gear and has not yet re-engaged. The text's claim is that the gap is not empty. Śivopādhyāya's word for what comes is occasion, an opening that arrives rather than a state produced, so our reading is his; but he adds a condition we would not have dared, that to the unawakened this is a sneeze and he is here simply a fool. Both halves are true at once and they do not cancel. A portal does nothing for someone unprepared — and there was never a moment when the one standing at it was not already Bhairava. The preparation is not for the sneeze. It is everything else you do.

Word by word

kṣuta+ādi+ante — at the beginning (ādi) and end (anta) of a sneeze (loc. sg.); bhaye — in fear (n. loc. sg.); śoke — in grief (m. loc. sg.); gahvare — at a chasm, in a deep place (n. loc. sg.); vā — or; raṇāt — from battle (m. abl. sg.); drute — in having fled (loc. sg., past part. of √dru; with raṇāt, 'in flight from battle'); kutūhale — in keen curiosity (n. loc. sg.); kṣudh+ādi+ante — at the beginning and end of hunger (loc. sg.); brahma+sattā+mayī — made of the being of brahman (f. nom. sg.); daśā — state (f. nom. sg.)

Open portal 095 in the reader →

096Dropping a remembered scene

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 119 · dhāraṇā 96 · Space · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

वस्तुषु स्मर्यमाणेषु दृष्टे देशे मनस् त्यजेत्

स्वशरीरं निराधारं कृत्वा प्रसरति प्रभुः

vastuṣu smaryamāṇeṣu dṛṣṭe deśe manas tyajet
svaśarīraṃ nirādhāraṃ kṛtvā prasarati prabhuḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

While things are being remembered, cast the mind into the place directly seen;one's own body made supportless, the lord streams forth

Translation · colloquial

While something is being remembered, throw the mind into the place you are actually looking at;your own body left with nothing to rest on, and the lord comes pouring through.

Practice

Let something remembered arise — a place, a scene, an old encounter. Do not fight the image and do not feed it. Instead find what the memory is made of: you are remembering it only because it was once undergone, and that undergoing is still here, underneath the picture. Throw the mind into that. Not away from the memory — through it, into the direct experience that is its ground. Śivopādhyāya is explicit that the verb here is cast, not release, and gives the reason in four words: memory is preceded by experience. Let the body too lose its inner prop, as if what carried it were taken away. Then what streams forth is the one awareness that has been holding the remembering and the experiencing together all along.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya reads the verb as cast into, not let go: while things are being known by memory, the mind is to be thrown into the field of direct experience that underlies them — his reason being that memory is preceded by experience. The place seen, on his reading, is that experiential ground. What then streams forth he calls the Lord whose very form is direct experience: the single awareness that synthesizes the remembering and the experiencing, the remembered and the undergone.

Commentary

The instruction is one small displacement from what everyone else advises, and the displacement is the whole practice. Faced with an intrusive memory, most traditions say let go of the image. This verse says cast the mind — throw the holder, not the held. Whether the remembered place stays or fades is not the business. The specification matters: a place once seen, not an invented one; the practice needs the particular pull only an actual recollection has. Śivopādhyāya reads a step further. Memory is preceded by experience, so the place directly seen is not a rival object but the ground the memory was made out of, which means the mind is not being moved from one thing to another at all. There is no object of remembrance standing apart. And the same throw can be made once more, into what underlies both the remembering and the place seen — made there it is no longer a displacement of the mind but the letting go of it. Then the body loses its inner prop as well, that supportless condition this book keeps arriving at from every direction, and what fills the unpropped space is not blankness. The lord streams forth: an expansion where the logic of the practice would predict a void.

Word by word

vastuṣu — things, objects (n. loc. pl.); smaryamāṇeṣu — being remembered (pres. pass. part., loc. pl.; locative absolute with vastuṣu); dṛṣṭe — seen (past part., m. loc. sg.); deśe — place (m. loc. sg.); manaḥ — the mind (n. acc. sg.); tyajet — one should let go, abandon (3rd sg. opt., √tyaj; the received line reads 'cast … into', which is Śivopādhyāya's own gloss: he replaces tyajet with prakṣipet, cast into. The root carries 'abandon' unaided, and the throw comes from him); sva+śarīram — one's own body (n. acc. sg.); nirādhāram — supportless (n. acc. sg.); kṛtvā — having made (gerund, √kṛ); prasarati — spreads forth, streams out (3rd sg. pres., pra-√sṛ); prabhuḥ — the lord (m. nom. sg.)

Open portal 096 in the reader →

097Withdrawing the gaze slowly

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 120 · dhāraṇā 97 · Body · āṇavopāya · with a support · gross → subtle

क्वचिद् वस्तुनि विन्यस्य शनैर् दृष्टिं निवर्तयेत्

तज् ज्ञानं चित्तसहितं देवि शून्यालयो भवेत्

kvacid vastuni vinyasya śanair dṛṣṭiṃ nivartayet
taj jñānaṃ cittasahitaṃ devi śūnyālayo bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Settle the gaze on some object, then slowly draw it back —and that knowing with the thought bent on it: Goddess, one's dwelling is the void

Translation · colloquial

Let your eyes settle on something, then draw them slowly back;and that knowing, with the thought still bent on it — Goddess, the place you live is empty.

Practice

Rest the eyes on any one thing until the seeing settles. Then withdraw — slowly, so slowly the object seems to fade rather than be left. Draw back with the gaze the knowing of the thing, and the particular thought that was bent on it. What you are left holding is not blankness pursued but emptiness inhabited: the verse says you become the one whose dwelling it is. Śivopādhyāya offers a way of doing the drawing-back that is worth having. Either contemplate the void directly, or use what he calls the inward aim with the outward gaze: leave the eyes open and outward, and let the aim alone turn in. He glosses dwelling as rest. It is a place to stop, not a thing to reach.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya construes the second line as the continuation of the withdrawal: what is drawn back is the knowing of that object together with the conceptualization aimed at it — not the mind entire, only the construing bent on that thing. What becomes a dwelling of the void is then the practitioner: one whose repose, he glosses, is in the void. He offers a second method beside contemplation of the void — the inward aim held with the outward gaze — and reports a variant reading, dissolution into the void, for dwelling in it.

Commentary

The exact reverse of the frozen gaze. There the eyes were pinned open on an object until the sense-making faculty lost its footing; here they settle first and are then drawn back off it, and the withdrawal is the method. Everything hinges on the adverb — slowly, the same dosage word that governed the crawling tingle and the rocking body. Done briskly this is only looking away. Done at the right speed the object seems to recede of itself, and three things retire in step: the thing, the knowing of the thing, and the mind that knew it. This is the fourth limb of classical yoga performed on one sense in slow motion. The destination gets a name found nowhere else in the book — a dwelling of the void, emptiness as a place one lives rather than a lack one attains. Compare the fullness promised three portals back. Same door, opposite vocabulary.

Word by word

kvacit — on some, on any (loc. sg.); vastuni — object (n. loc. sg.); vinyasya — having placed, settled (gerund, vi-ni-√as); śanaiḥ — slowly (adv.); dṛṣṭim — the gaze (f. acc. sg.); nivartayet — one should turn back, withdraw (3rd sg. opt. caus., ni-√vṛt); tat — that (n. acc. sg.); jñānam — the knowing [of the object] (n. acc. sg.); citta+sahitam — together with the mind (n. acc. sg.); devi — O Goddess (f. voc. sg.); śūnya+ālayaḥ — a dwelling of the void (m. nom. sg.; KSTS śūnyālayo, glossed śūnye ālayaḥ — viśrāntiḥ and required by the pathyā cadence — the e-text's śūnyālāyo is not a word); bhavet — one becomes (3rd sg. opt., √bhū)

Open portal 097 in the reader →

098Devotion's overflow

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 121 · dhāraṇā 98 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

भक्त्युद्रेकाद् विरक्तस्य यादृशी जायते मतिः

सा शक्तिः शाङ्करी नित्यम् भावयेत् तां ततः शिवः

bhaktyudrekād viraktasya yādṛśī jāyate matiḥ
sā śaktiḥ śāṅkarī nityam bhāvayet tāṃ tataḥ śivaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

From devotion's overflow, the understanding born in one detached —that is the śakti of Śaṅkara; cultivate her always, and from that, Śiva

Translation · colloquial

The understanding that is born in one who has let go, out of devotion brimming over,is Shankara's own power; hold to her always, and out of that, Shiva.

Practice

Two conditions meet: you want nothing for yourself, and devotion rises anyway, past its usual banks. A particular quality of mind is born there — clear, warm, unowned. The verse says that mind is not your feeling but the śakti of Śaṅkara, a name of Śiva: his power, present in you. Cultivate her constantly — return to that state, dwell in it, let it deepen. From that dwelling, Śiva. Śivopādhyāya, asked what devotion is, quotes a definition that starts by saying what it is not: falling at the feet is not devotion to a Lord who is everywhere — there is nowhere to fall toward. For those made of feeling, he says, devotion is the contemplation of becoming one with him. Which tells you what cultivate her always is asking for, and what it is not.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya defines devotion by quotation, and the definition opens by excluding the obvious: falling at the feet is not devotion to the all-pervading supreme Lord. For those whose very nature is feeling, it runs, devotion is the contemplation of becoming one with him. He glosses overflow as simply abundance. The quoted verse is given without attribution.

Commentary

The only devotional portal in the hundred and twelve, and it sets two conditions that look incompatible. Devotion past its banks, in someone who wants nothing. Put that way it is a test: the overflow counts only when there is no one left asking for anything, which quietly excludes most of what passes for religious feeling. And what is said to be born is mati, a quality of mind, an understanding. Not an emotion, though an emotion is what produced it. The verse then does the move this book does everywhere with anything valuable: it takes it out of your hands. What rose in you is not your love; it is Śiva's own power, and you are the place where she showed. And uniquely here the instruction is constancy — always, not at a moment or in a flash. Every other portal describes an occasion. This one describes a residence.

Word by word

bhakti+udrekāt — from the overflow, surging excess, of devotion (m. abl. sg.); viraktasya — of one detached, free of craving (m. gen. sg.); yādṛśī — of whatever kind (f. nom. sg., relative); jāyate — is born (3rd sg. pres. mid., √jan); matiḥ — understanding, quality of mind (f. nom. sg.); sā — that (f. nom. sg., correlative); śaktiḥ — śakti, power (f. nom. sg.); śāṅkarī — belonging to Śaṅkara [Śiva] (f. nom. sg.); nityam — always (adv.); bhāvayet — one should cultivate, dwell on (3rd sg. opt. caus., √bhū; KSTS prints the causative — the e-text's bhavayet is not a form); tām — her (f. acc. sg.); tataḥ — from that, thereupon; śivaḥ — Śiva (m. nom. sg.)

099Emptiness around one thing

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 122 · dhāraṇā 99 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

वस्त्वन्तरे वेद्यमाने शनैर् वस्तुषु शून्यता

ताम् एव मनसा ध्यात्वा विदितो ऽपि प्रशाम्यति

vastvantare vedyamāne śanair vastuṣu śūnyatā
tām eva manasā dhyātvā vidito 'pi praśāmyati

Translation · literal Sanskrit

While one thing is being known — emptiness, gradually, in the [other] thingsmeditating with the mind on that very emptiness, even while knowing, one grows still

Translation · colloquial

While one thing is being known, emptiness comes, little by little, into the others;meditate with the mind on that very emptiness, and even in the midst of knowing you go quiet.

Practice

Let attention rest on one thing — a cup, a sound, a flame. While that one thing is known, every other thing has fallen empty of your attention. Turn the mind to that emptiness itself rather than to the object. Hold it there. You do not need to stop knowing the one thing; the verse allows the knowing to continue. Even while it is known, everything settles. Śivopādhyāya defines the emptiness twice over, and the second definition is the one to hold. Negatively it is simply their not-appearing just now. Positively it is the light of consciousness, which is what was there when the objects stopped showing up. So you are not attending to a lack. Attend with what he calls non-conceptual awareness rather than with the thinking mind, and what settles, he says, is that you become of the nature of quiescent brahman.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya defines the emptiness plainly: it is the other things' not-appearing at that time. He then says what it is positively — its nature is the absence of the knowable, and its form is the light of consciousness. The mind with which it is meditated on he glosses as non-conceptual awareness. He also pairs this portal with the slow withdrawal of the gaze: there a single object is seen and the thought about it withdrawn; here all the other knowables are taken as empty while the one goes on being known.

Commentary

You produce a void continuously, all day, simply by attending to anything — because whatever you are not attending to has gone empty for you. There is nothing to manufacture. The practice is to turn round and take that as the object: not the cup, but the world's absence around the cup. The permission in the last line is what makes this unusual. You need not stop knowing the one thing. Nearly every meditative model treats the surviving object as the last obstacle, and this verse says the stillness arrives with it still standing there. Attending to the ground rather than the figure is one modern way to put it, though the verse claims more than a perceptual trick. What is being attended to is not merely the unnoticed remainder but emptiness itself, which the object was obscuring by being interesting.

Word by word

vastu+antare — [when] one thing among [all] (n. loc. sg.); vedyamāne — is being known (pres. passive part., n. loc. sg.; locative absolute with vastvantare); śanaiḥ — gradually, little by little (adv.); vastuṣu — in the [other] things (n. loc. pl.; KSTS śanair vastuṣu, where the vivṛti supplies the 'all' in its own gloss sarvavedyeṣu tadanyeṣu — the e-text had sarvavastuṣu); śūnyatā — emptiness (f. nom. sg.); tām — that [emptiness] (f. acc. sg.); eva — very, precisely; manasā — with the mind (n. instr. sg.); dhyātvā — having meditated (gerund); viditaḥ — [though still] knowing (past part. of √vid, m. nom. sg., of the meditator); api — even, although; praśāmyati — he grows still (3rd sg. pres., pra+√śam)

Open portal 099 in the reader →

100Beyond pure and impure

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 123 · dhāraṇā 100 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

किंचिज्ज्ञैर् या स्मृता शुद्धिः सा ऽशुद्धिः शम्भुदर्शने

न शुचिर् ह्य् अशुचिस् तस्मान् निर्विकल्पः सुखी भवेत्

kiṃcijjñair yā smṛtā śuddhiḥ sā 'śuddhiḥ śambhudarśane
na śucir hy aśucis tasmān nirvikalpaḥ sukhī bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

The purity the little-knowing prescribe is, in the vision of Śambhu, impurity;for nothing is pure, nothing impure — therefore, free of dividing thought, become happy

Translation · colloquial

What people who know a little call purity is, in the seeing of Shambhu, impurity;nothing is pure and nothing is impure, so let the dividing stop, and be happy.

Practice

This is a conviction to carry, not a posture to hold. When the judgment arises — this is clean, that is defiled, I am fit or unfit today — see it as a rule made from partial knowledge. In the seeing that belongs to Śambhu, a name of Śiva, such purity does not stand. Drop the division wherever you catch it at work, and let the ease that follows be the fruit. Śivopādhyāya spends four pages on this verse, more than on any other, and the argument is worth carrying with the practice. The rules are not abolished; they are confined. Purification by clay is a limb of ritual action, so let it hold for whoever is doing ritual — but nowhere, he says, has anyone written that one is qualified for knowledge only when clean. His own reason for setting the ablution aside is not disdain but attention: when you wash the body you look at the body, and looking is how identification gets back in. And he quotes the condition under which the verse is safe. Nothing here is enjoined and nothing forbidden — except one thing: the mind must be made steady in the reality, with great effort; and in whatever way that becomes attainable, so let one act. Of such a person it is then said that he is like one armed with a mantra against poison, who eats poison and is not deluded by it. The freedom belongs to the mantra.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya gives this verse four pages, more than any other, and identifies its target precisely: the purity of the law-books, cleansing with clay and water, prescribed by men of small understanding. He does not abolish it — purification is a limb of ritual, he says, so let it hold for whoever is qualified for ritual; but nowhere has it been said that one is qualified for knowledge only when clean. His own reason for dropping it is that washing makes one look at the body, and even a knower of reality may come to identify with what he looks at. He then answers the Gītā's praise of purity with the Skandapurāṇa's sevenfold bath, in which the mental bath is the chief and water merely one of seven, and answers the appeal to dharma out of the Yājñavalkyasmṛti itself: the supreme dharma is the seeing of the self by yoga. Before the verse he quotes at length the passage in which nothing whatever is enjoined and nothing forbidden — no purity, no dietary rule, no vow, no pilgrimage, nothing of another's name or lineage — and then its one exception: this alone is laid down as a rule, that the yogin must with great effort make the mind steady in the reality, and conduct himself in whatever way makes that attainable. Of such a yogin, he quotes, it is said that he is like a man armed with a mantra against poison, who eats poison and is not deluded by it. He closes conciliatory: even one who has not given up washing becomes happy, once there is purity of mind.

Commentary

The most socially dangerous sentence in the collection, and the tradition knew it. The chain is fierce: what people of small knowledge prescribe as purity is, in Śiva's own seeing, not pure — it is the impurity, because a rule that sorts the world into clean and defiled is itself the defilement it claims to remove. This is the antinomian nerve of the whole tantric movement stated in half a verse. It needs one guardrail, which the verse does not supply and we will. What is being dismantled is the judging, not the conduct; read as permission it becomes a well-worn excuse, and the check on it is the portal about consciousness in every body. Śivopādhyāya, who gives this verse more space than any other, reaches the same guardrail from the other side. Nothing whatever is enjoined and nothing forbidden, he quotes — and then the single exception: that the yogin must make the mind steady in reality, and conduct himself in whatever way makes that attainable. Conduct is not released. It is re-anchored to a harder measure. The live modern form of the rule is smaller and more personal than ritual bathing: not clean enough, not calm enough, not ready today, not the sort of person this is for. That verdict is the one this verse dissolves.

Word by word

kiṃcit+jñaiḥ — by those who know [only] a little (m. instr. pl.); yā — which (f. nom. sg.); smṛtā — is taught, prescribed (past part., f. nom. sg.); śuddhiḥ — purity (f. nom. sg.); sā — that (f. nom. sg.); aśuddhiḥ — impurity (f. nom. sg.; KSTS prints sā 'śuddhiḥ with the avagraha, sā aśuddhiḥ; the e-text dropped it and inverted the half-verse, which the next pāda na śucir hy aśuciḥ rules out); śambhu+darśane — in the vision of Śambhu (n. loc. sg.); na — not; śuciḥ — pure (adj., nom. sg.); hi — indeed, for; aśuciḥ — impure (adj., nom. sg.); tasmāt — therefore (abl.); nirvikalpaḥ — free of dividing thought (m. nom. sg.); sukhī — happy (m. nom. sg.); bhavet — one should become (3rd sg. opt., √bhū)

101Bhairava in the ordinary

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 124 · dhāraṇā 101 · Awareness · śāmbhavopāya · no means · causal

सर्वत्र भैरवो भावः सामान्येष्व् अपि गोचरः

न च तद्व्यतिरेकेण परो ऽस्तीत्य् अद्वया गतिः

sarvatra bhairavo bhāvaḥ sāmānyeṣv api gocaraḥ
na ca tadvyatirekeṇa paro 'stīty advayā gatiḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Everywhere, being is Bhairava — in plain view, even in the ordinaryand apart from that no other exists — thus the way is without a second

Translation · colloquial

Everywhere, whatever is, is Bhairava, and he is in plain view even in ordinary things;and apart from him there is nothing else at all: so the way has no second.

Practice

Whatever presents itself — a wall, a chore, an ordinary face — attend to the bare fact that it is. That being, the verse says, is Bhairava, and it stands in plain view in the common thing as much as in the exalted one. When the mind proposes something higher elsewhere, return: apart from this there is no other. Walk through the day on that footing. Śivopādhyāya turns the same line toward people, and toward you. What is in plain view, he says, is the flash of I know, I do — which is going on in everyone, including everyone without a trace of discrimination, and which is one and the same flash in all of them. He quotes: whom even the dull know, whom even the women carrying water know. And then he asks the question the whole book has been circling: if it is more plain than anything, what is the use of a method? The net of means cannot illumine Śiva. Does the sun need a lamp?

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya takes the ordinary to mean ordinary people rather than ordinary things: it is graspable, he says, even among those without discrimination, because in every knower the awareness I know, I do flashes out plainly, and that awareness is one. He is careful to add what being is not — not, as with the Buddhists and their like, a form empty of the knowable, but the real, the self that knows. And he draws the conclusion the verse invites: the Lord is nothing but the form I, and he is simply manifest, so what use are the three means? The net of means cannot illumine Śiva; does the thousand-rayed sun shine by a pot? He quotes the Mahārthamañjarī — whom even the dull know, whom even the water-carrying women know — and then the Veda, where the cowherds and the water-carriers see him and all beings see him.

Commentary

Read this beside the verse a few doors back about wherever the mind goes; they are close but not the same. That one located the state in every destination. This one says the bare fact that anything is at all — bhāva, being, before any of its qualities — is Bhairava, and that this is in plain view. The word is gocara, within range, originally the ground a cow can graze: not hidden, not esoteric, available to an ordinary look. And then the flat clause that ends the search: apart from that, no other exists. Which is the point of the ordinary things named in the first line. A text that has spent two hundred verses on chasms, mudrās, needles and unstruck sound finishes by saying the plain wall will do, and that anything the mind proposes as higher and elsewhere is the one thing that certainly is not it.

Word by word

sarvatra — everywhere (adv.); bhairavaḥ — Bhairava (m. nom. sg.); bhāvaḥ — being, existence (m. nom. sg.); sāmānyeṣu — in ordinary [things, people] (loc. pl.); api — even; gocaraḥ — within range, in plain view (m. nom. sg.); na — not; ca — and; tad+vyatirekeṇa — apart from that (instr. sg.; the text prints vyatirekteṇa); paraḥ — [something] other (m. nom. sg.); asti — exists (3rd sg. pres., √as); iti — thus; advayā — without a second, nondual (f. nom. sg.); gatiḥ — the course, way (f. nom. sg.)

Open portal 101 in the reader →

102Equal to friend and enemy

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 125 · dhāraṇā 102 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

समः शत्रौ च मित्रे च समो मानावमानयोः

ब्रह्मणः परिपूर्णत्वात् इति ज्ञात्वा सुखी भवेत्

samaḥ śatrau ca mitre ca samo mānāvamānayoḥ
brahmaṇaḥ paripūrṇatvāt iti jñātvā sukhī bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

The same to enemy and friend; the same in honor and in contemptbecause brahman is utterly full — knowing this, become happy

Translation · colloquial

The same to an enemy and to a friend, the same in honour and in contempt,because what is real is full to the brim: know that, and be happy.

Practice

Recall someone who opposes you and someone who loves you; recall a moment of honor and one of humiliation. Set them side by side and look for what is equally present in each — brahman, the one reality, lacking nowhere. The verse asks knowing, not forcing: you do not manufacture warmth toward the enemy, you see that nothing is missing anywhere. Rest in the evenness that follows.

Commentary

The reasoning is what distinguishes this from ordinary counsel about equanimity. It does not ask you to feel warmly toward an enemy or coolly toward praise; it gives a reason from which the evenness is meant to follow — because brahman is complete, lacking nowhere, and therefore not more present in the friend or less in the adversary. The word is knowing, not forcing. Very similar lines appear in the Bhagavad-gītā, which tells you this vocabulary was common property of the period rather than borrowed in either direction. One honest caution, since sameness language is easily misused: what the verse addresses is the interior weather of honour and contempt, and it does not say that harm and kindness are the same thing or that you should stop protecting yourself and others. Evenness toward a person is not indifference to what they do.

Word by word

samaḥ — the same, even (m. nom. sg.); śatrau — toward an enemy (m. loc. sg.); ca ... ca — both ... and; mitre — toward a friend (n. loc. sg.); samaḥ — the same (m. nom. sg.); māna+avamānayoḥ — in honor and in contempt (m. loc. dual); brahmaṇaḥ — of brahman (n. gen. sg.); paripūrṇatvāt — because of [its] complete fullness (n. abl. sg.); iti — thus; jñātvā — having known (gerund, √jñā); sukhī — happy (m. nom. sg.); bhavet — one should become (3rd sg. opt., √bhū)

Open portal 102 in the reader →

103Neither craving nor aversion

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 126 · dhāraṇā 103 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

न द्वेषम् भावयेत् क्वापि न रागम् भावयेत् क्वचित्

रागद्वेषविनिर्मुक्तौ मध्ये ब्रह्म प्रसर्पति

na dveṣam bhāvayet kvāpi na rāgam bhāvayet kvacit
rāgadveṣavinirmuktau madhye brahma prasarpati

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Cultivate no aversion anywhere; cultivate no desire anywherein release from desire and aversion, brahman glides forth in the madhya

Translation · colloquial

Do not work up a dislike for anything anywhere, and do not work up a wanting for anything either;let go of both, and in the middle what is real glides forward.

Practice

Watch for the two movements: toward and against. When aversion starts up at anything, do not build it; when desire starts, do not build it either. You are not suppressing what arises — you are declining to cultivate it. In the release from both pulls a middle opens — the madhya, the centre between them — and into it brahman unfolds of itself. Your work is only the not-building. Śivopādhyāya's whole comment on this verse is two words: glides forth means blossoms open. Not something coming in from outside once you have made room, but something opening where it already was, the way a flower opens. The middle is not a doorway. It is the place.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya's entire comment on this verse is two words: glides forth means blossoms open. Brahman does not enter the middle from elsewhere; it unfolds there, as a flower opens. He heads the verse as teaching the ground of Śambhu.

Commentary

The verb is the joke and the instruction. Bhāvayet — cultivate, cause to become — is the word this book uses everywhere for meditation itself. So the sentence reads: do not practise aversion; do not practise desire. The text treats the two pulls as contemplations you are already performing with great diligence, rehearsing the grievance, dwelling on the wanted thing, and asks only that you stop doing the exercise. Nothing is being suppressed. What arises is left to arise and simply not built on, which is a far smaller and more achievable thing than not feeling it. And in the gap where the building would have gone, the book's oldest word returns for the last time: madhya, the middle — first found between two breaths, then between two thoughts, two objects, two moments, and now between the two motions that organise a whole life. Brahman is said to glide into it, the way something enters a room that has been emptied.

Word by word

na — not; dveṣam — aversion (m. acc. sg.); bhāvayet — one should cultivate (3rd sg. opt. causative, √bhū); kva+api — anywhere, toward anything; na — nor; rāgam — desire, attachment (m. acc. sg.); bhāvayet — one should cultivate (3rd sg. opt.); kvacit — anywhere; rāga+dveṣa+vinirmuktau — in complete release from desire and aversion (f. loc. sg. of vinirmukti); madhye — in the centre (loc. sg.); brahma — brahman (n. nom. sg.); prasarpati — glides forth, creeps in (3rd sg. pres., pra+√sṛp)

Open portal 103 in the reader →

104Contemplating the ungraspable

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 127 · dhāraṇā 104 · Space · śāmbhavopāya · no means · causal

यद् अवेद्यं यद् अग्राह्यं यच् छून्यं यद् अभावगम्

तत् सर्वम् भैरवम् भाव्यं तदन्ते बोधसम्भवः

yad avedyaṃ yad agrāhyaṃ yac chūnyaṃ yad abhāvagam
tat sarvam bhairavam bhāvyaṃ tadante bodhasambhavaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

What cannot be known; what cannot be grasped; what is empty; what goes into absencecontemplate all that as Bhairava — at the end of that, awakening arises

Translation · colloquial

What cannot be known, what cannot be got hold of, what is empty, what goes out into nothing:hold all of it as Bhairava, and at the end of that attempt, the waking comes.

Practice

Take as your object exactly what refuses to be an object: whatever cannot be known, cannot be held, what is empty, what has gone into absence — the sound that has died away, the space behind a thought. Śivopādhyāya pushes the category further than that, and the push matters: his example is the sky-flower, which did not fade but never was. Even that, he says, has the throb of being-ness, and so is not outside Bhairava. Contemplate it as Bhairava himself, not as a mere blank. Stay with it without asking it to become something. At the end of this contemplating, the verse says, awakening arises.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Vimarśadīpikā, quoted at length; Maheśvarānanda quoted by name (महेश्वरानन्दपादैः). Footnote, plate 0118 line 8: MSS ga and pu read ज्ञानदशा for ज्ञेयदशा. Footnote, plate 0119 line 21: MSS ka and pu lack the passage from सद्भाव to अनुप्राण.

Commentary

Four negations, each removing a way a thing could be an object for you — unknowable, ungraspable, empty, gone into absence — and then the sentence that makes the verse: contemplate all that as Bhairava. This is the whole difference between this tradition's emptiness and a merely privative one, and it is his sentence before it is ours: the ground of emptiness contemplated by the sons of the Sugata is one thing, he writes, and that of the worshippers in the Goddess's way another. What you are asked to sit with is not a nothing but a someone. The fourth negation is the one to watch. What goes into absence sounds like things that have ceased — a sound died away, the space behind a thought. He glosses it instead with the sky-flower, which never existed at all, and insists that even that has the throb of being-ness; Maheśvarānanda, quoted here, presses it to a question — what difference in being-ness is there between a flower and a flower in the sky? The category is not things that have gone. It is things that were never there. There is also an impossibility in the grammar: the gerundive says it is to be contemplated, and contemplation needs something to rest on, which the first line has just taken away. Which is why the awakening is placed, as so often here, at the end of the attempt rather than in it.

Word by word

yat — what (n. nom. sg., four times, distributive); avedyam — not to be known (gerundive with a-, n. nom. sg.); agrāhyam — not to be grasped (gerundive with a-, n. nom. sg.); śūnyam — empty (n. nom. sg.); abhāva+gam — going into non-being (n. nom. sg.); tat sarvam — all that (n. nom. sg.); bhairavam — as Bhairava (predicate); bhāvyam — is to be contemplated (gerundive of causative √bhū, n. nom. sg.); tat+ante — at the end of that (loc. sg.); bodha+sambhavaḥ — the arising of awakening (m. nom. sg.)

Open portal 104 in the reader →

105Mind in the open sky

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 128 · dhāraṇā 105 · Space · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle → causal

नित्ये निराश्रये शून्ये व्यापके कलनोज्झिते

बाह्याकाशे मनः कृत्वा निराकाशं समाविशेत्

nitye nirāśraye śūnye vyāpake kalanojjhite
bāhyākāśe manaḥ kṛtvā nirākāśaṃ samāviśet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Constant; without support; empty; pervading; free of reckoning —place the mind in that outer sky, and enter the spaceless

Translation · colloquial

Constant, standing on nothing, empty, spread everywhere, free of all reckoning:put the mind in that outer sky, and go into what has no sky at all.

Practice

Sit where you can face open sky. Place the mind in that outer space and let it take on what it rests in: nothing held, nothing leaned on, no edge, pervading everywhere, empty of all reckoning. Stay until mind and sky agree. Then go further — enter what has no space at all, where even the sky is left behind. Do not read that last step as a vanishing. Śivopādhyāya is emphatic at exactly this point: what is entered is an abode, and it is not empty.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Śivopādhyāya heads this ground Śākta and no other. The mind made into outer sky is knowing whose nature is vimarśa, brought to samāpatti by the qualifiers the verse supplies, beginning with constancy. Then, when supportless consciousness rises, what one enters is an abode gone forth out of space — and he says of it that it is not empty. Footnote, plate 0120 line 8: MSS ka, ga and pu read इति शाक्त्येव.

Commentary

Five adjectives are hung on the open sky — constant, unsupported, empty, pervading, free of all reckoning — and every one of them is a description this school uses for the absolute. The verse is smuggling its metaphysics in as weather. That is the operating principle behind every space practice in the collection, stated here more fully than anywhere else: the mind takes on the properties of what it is placed in, so a mind set in something with no edges eventually has none. Then the last word takes the ladder away. Having got you into the sky, the verse asks you to enter the spaceless, which means the sky was a support too and now goes the way of the others. Practically, this wants real sky — cloudless, no horizon line, nothing for the eye to work on. Like the moonless dark of the sixty-fourth portal, the requirement is a field with no features in it.

Word by word

nitye — constant, eternal (loc. sg.); nirāśraye — without support (loc. sg.); śūnye — empty (loc. sg.); vyāpake — pervading (loc. sg.); kalana+ujjhite — abandoned by reckoning, free of limiting activity (loc. sg.; all five agreeing with bāhyākāśe); bāhya+ākāśe — in the outer space, the open sky (m. loc. sg.); manaḥ — the mind (n. acc. sg.); kṛtvā — having placed (gerund, √kṛ); nirākāśam — the spaceless (n. acc. sg.); samāviśet — one should enter fully (3rd sg. opt., sam+ā+√viś)

Open portal 105 in the reader →

106Abandoning what the mind touches

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 129 · dhāraṇā 106 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

यत्र यत्र मनो याति तत् तत् तेनैव तत्क्षणम्

परित्यज्यानवस्थित्या निस्तरङ्गस् ततो भवेत्

yatra yatra mano yāti tat tat tenaiva tatkṣaṇam
parityajyānavasthityā nistaraṅgas tato bhavet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Wherever the mind goes — that very thing, with that very mind, that very instantlet it go; by settling nowhere, one thereby becomes waveless

Translation · colloquial

Wherever the mind goes, that very thing, with that very mind, that very instant,let go of it; and by settling nowhere at all, you become waveless.

Practice

Sit and let the mind move as it will. Wherever it goes — a plan, a sound, a memory — drop that very thing, with that same mind, in that same instant. Do not choose a better object; the method offers none. It is refusal to alight. And it is not one clean refusal: Śivopādhyāya writes it as gone again, and again drawn back, an indefinite series rather than a single sitting, which is why he reaches for the word abhyāsa — practice, the thing you do repeatedly. Settling nowhere, the mind runs out of places to make waves, and what remains is waveless.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Patañjali cited by number; Gītā quoted by number; Gītā 6.35 paraphrased. Footnote, plate 0121 line 2: MSS ka read निरालम्बनतायुक्त्या.

Commentary

The first half of this verse is word for word the first half of the portal thirteen doors back, and the second half says the opposite. There, wherever the mind goes is the state of Śiva, so let it go. Here, wherever the mind goes, drop that, instantly. The book sets the two readings of one line a few pages apart, ranks neither, and leaves you to find out which one is yours today — exactly as it did with the rising desire that was led back to its source in one verse and ridden in another. Note the instrument: with that very mind. There is no second, cleaner faculty standing by to do the releasing; the mind that grabbed is the mind that lets go, which is why this can be done at all. And note that no better object is offered, ever. The method is only the refusal to alight, and the result is the ocean image this book has used before — no waves, because nothing has landed on the water.

Word by word

yatra yatra — wherever (adv., distributive); manaḥ — the mind (n. nom. sg.); yāti — goes (3rd sg. pres., √yā); tat tat — that very [thing] (n. acc. sg., distributive); tena+eva — with that very [mind] (instr. sg.); tat+kṣaṇam — at that instant (adverbial acc.); parityajya — abandoning (gerund, pari+√tyaj); anavasthityā — through not settling [anywhere] (f. instr. sg. of anavasthiti); nistaraṅgaḥ — waveless (m. nom. sg.); tataḥ — thereby, then; bhavet — one becomes (3rd sg. opt., √bhū)

Open portal 106 in the reader →

107Repeating the name Bhairava

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 130 · dhāraṇā 107 · Sound · āṇavopāya · with a support · subtle → causal

भया सर्वं रवयति सर्वदो व्यापको ऽखिले

इति भैरवशब्दस्य सन्ततोच्चारणाच् छिवः

bhayā sarvaṃ ravayati sarvado vyāpako 'khile
iti bhairavaśabdasya santatoccāraṇāc chivaḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

By the Light he makes all things resound; giver of all; pervading the whole —so, from unbroken utterance of the word Bhairava, one becomes Śiva

Translation · colloquial

By the light he makes everything sound out; he gives everything; he fills the whole of it.So, from saying the word Bhairava without a break, you become Shiva.

Practice

Utter the word Bhairava, aloud or within, and keep the utterance unbroken. Let the name carry what Śivopādhyāya hears inside it: the light of consciousness, the power of action that makes all things resound, and the giving and pervading that the second line names. You are not describing him from outside; the continuous sounding itself does the work. Sustained without gap, the verse says, it leaves you Śiva.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Four plates of philology, and the place in the whole commentary where he is most openly in dispute with his own tradition — against a widely attested variant, against the ancient भरण-रवण-वमन derivation, and against Kṣemarāja by name. Footnotes: 0122 line 9, MSS sa and pu lack भैरव … विमृशति; 0122 line 20, MSS ka and pu variant, blotted and unresolved; 0123 line 12, ga and pu read भरणपोषणेति; 0124 line 3, ga and pu read ईरयति कम्पयतीति; 0124 line 4, ka, kha and pu read तं वातीति; 0124 line 5, ka and pu read निमीलनमेव विधेयमिति; 0124 line 18, ka and pu read भाजनमिति.

Commentary

The first line is a nirukta, a meaning-etymology, and should not be mistaken for linguistics: what a derivation of this kind does is load the syllables, so that whoever utters the word sounds all its senses at once — he by whose light everything cries out, the giver of all, the one who pervades. Meaning is packed into a sound so the sound can carry it. Śivopādhyāya spends four plates on precisely this and is openly at odds with his own tradition, arguing against the old bearing-and-crying derivation and against Kṣemarāja by name. On the philology he is right and our earlier note was not: the name is not built out of fear. The fear is a separate claim and it survives. Bhairava is the terrifying form, and this is nearly the only place in the hundred and twelve where the book lets that show; everywhere else he is simply awareness and the name has gone quiet. The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom — the line is the Bible's and not the tantra's, and it is exact here. Met early and from outside, he terrifies, and goes on terrifying until he is found within and known as oneself. That thou art. The terror was never in the etymology; it is in the distance. The practice lacks a good deal: no rosary, no count, no seed-syllable, no hour. One word, and one requirement — that the uttering not break.

Word by word

bhayā — by the Light [of consciousness] (f. instr. sg. of bhā, a regular feminine ā-stem; the vivṛti glosses bhayā saṃvitprakāśarūpayā and again bhayā jñānātmikayā śaktyā, and the word carries the nirukti the verse is built on — bhā + ai + rava. Śivopādhyāya names the rival reading bhriyāt, found in numerous manuscripts, and rejects it as na sādhu; an earlier conjecture bhiyā 'with fear' was withdrawn on that evidence); sarvam — everything (n. acc. sg.); ravayati — makes resound (3rd sg. pres. causative, √ru; the vivṛti glosses it ravayati vimṛśati — makes resound, that is, brings to awareness); sarva+daḥ — giver of all (m. nom. sg.); vyāpakaḥ — pervading (m. nom. sg.); akhile — in the whole (loc. sg.); iti — thus [runs the derivation]; bhairava+śabdasya — of the word Bhairava (m. gen. sg.); santata+uccāraṇāt — from unbroken utterance (n. abl. sg.); śivaḥ — [one becomes] Śiva (m. nom. sg., predicate; bhavati understood)

Open portal 107 in the reader →

108Where I and mine point

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 131 · dhāraṇā 108 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

अहं ममेदम् इत्यादि प्रतिपत्तिप्रसङ्गतः

निराधारे मनो याति तद्ध्यानप्रेरणाच् छमी

ahaṃ mamedam ityādi pratipattiprasaṅgataḥ
nirādhāre mano yāti taddhyānapreraṇāc chamī

Translation · literal Sanskrit

'I', 'this is mine', and the rest — when such convictions comethe mind goes to what has no support; driven on by meditation on that, one is stilled

Translation · colloquial

'I', and 'this is mine', and the rest of it: when such certainties come up,the mind goes to what has no support; driven on by holding to that, you go quiet.

Practice

Catch the mind in the act of saying 'I,' 'this is mine.' Instead of following what the thought claims, look for what it stands on. The small I finds nothing under it — that much the search does reliably. But do not stop at the vacancy, because Śivopādhyāya does not: what the looking arrives at is not an absence but a thing, the full I, which was never the one making the claim. Let the momentum of that very looking carry you. Meditation on the supportless, pressed on, ends in calm.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

प्रेमैव ब्रह्म केवलम्, and three quoted verses on self-love, one of them Upaniṣadic in substance. Maheśvarānanda named. Footnote, plate 0125 line 4: MSS ga and pu read सत्ताया.

Commentary

The same search that an earlier portal ran on an ordinary cognition, now run on the one case where everybody is certain of the answer. I; this is mine. Do not argue with the claim and do not analyse it — look for what it rests on, and keep looking. What the verse reports is that the mind, hunting for the owner, arrives at the supportless, which is the word this book has been circling since the frozen gaze. Note the mechanism named in the last line: preraṇā, a driving-on, a momentum. The practice is not the conclusion that no one is there — that is a thought like any other, and cheaply had. It is the impetus of the failing search, which carries past the point where the searching stops. Self-enquiry as taught in the last century has the same shape and arose independently; nothing is being claimed about influence in either direction.

Word by word

aham — I (nom. sg.); mama — mine (gen. sg.); idam — this (n. nom. sg.); iti+ādi — and so forth, such [thoughts]; pratipatti+prasaṅgataḥ — on the occasion of [such] cognition, conviction (abl. in -taḥ); nirādhāre — into what has no support (loc. sg.); manaḥ — the mind (n. nom. sg.); yāti — goes (3rd sg. pres., √yā); tat+dhyāna+preraṇāt — through the driving-on of meditation on that (n. abl. sg.); śamī — possessed of calm, stilled (m. nom. sg.)

Open portal 108 in the reader →

109Words held moment by moment

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 132 · dhāraṇā 109 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · subtle → causal

नित्यो विभुर् निराधारो व्यापकश् चाखिलाधिपः

शब्दान् प्रतिक्षणं ध्यायन् कृतार्थो ऽर्थानुरूपतः

nityo vibhur nirādhāro vyāpakaś cākhilādhipaḥ
śabdān pratikṣaṇaṃ dhyāyan kṛtārtho 'rthānurūpataḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

Eternal; all-present; without support; pervading; sovereign of the whole —meditating on the words moment by moment, one's aim is fulfilled — true to their meaning

Translation · colloquial

Everlasting, present everywhere, standing on nothing, spread through all, master of the whole:hold the words moment by moment, true to what they mean, and what you came for is done.

Practice

Take the words themselves: eternal, all-present, without support, pervading, sovereign of the whole. Say them, moment after moment, as words that mean something rather than a chant emptied of sense — Śivopādhyāya takes them as words to be recited and not merely held in mind. Let each one open toward what it names. Meditated this way, the verse says, they fulfil themselves in you — the aim is accomplished in proportion to the meaning.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Pāñcarātra cited कटाक्षेण; Bhaṭṭanārāyaṇa's Stavacintāmaṇi. Footnote, plate 0126 line 1: MSS ka and pu read निराकार for the mūla's निराधारो — a variant on the verse, not on the comment. Further footnotes: line 4, ka and pu अर्थानुरूपक; line 5, kha and pu नित्यत्वादिस्वरूपत; line 6, kha and pu कृतार्थः कृतकृत्य.

Commentary

The five words listed here are the five predicates hung on the open sky a few portals back, lifted out and given to you as words. And the last phrase is the whole point, easy to skim past: the aim is accomplished in conformity with the meaning. What you get is proportional to how much of the sense is actually alive in you while you hold them. Set that against the portal two doors earlier, where an unbroken utterance of a single name does the work whether or not you are attending to what it means. The book offers both and does not choose — sound that operates on its own terms, and language that operates only through understanding — which is the same even-handedness it showed with desire, and with the wandering mind. Note the frequency word. Moment by moment: not a phrase said once and rested in, but a meaning that has to be renewed continuously because meanings go dead in seconds.

Word by word

nityaḥ — eternal (m. nom. sg.); vibhuḥ — all-present (m. nom. sg.); nirādhāraḥ — without support (m. nom. sg.); vyāpakaḥ — pervading (m. nom. sg.); ca — and; akhila+adhipaḥ — sovereign of the whole (m. nom. sg.); śabdān — the words (m. acc. pl.); prati+kṣaṇam — moment by moment (adverbial); dhyāyan — meditating on (pres. part., m. nom. sg., √dhyai); kṛta+arthaḥ — one whose aim is accomplished (m. nom. sg.); artha+anurūpataḥ — in conformity with [their] meaning (adverbial abl.)

Open portal 109 in the reader →

110A conjuror's show

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 133 · dhāraṇā 110 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

अतत्त्वम् इन्द्रजालाभम् इदं सर्वम् अवस्थितम्

किं तत्त्वम् इन्द्रजालस्य इति दार्ढ्याच् छमं व्रजेत्

atattvam indrajālābham idaṃ sarvam avasthitam
kiṃ tattvam indrajālasya iti dārḍhyāc chamaṃ vrajet

Translation · literal Sanskrit

All this stands unreal, wearing the look of a conjuror's showwhat reality has a conjuror's show? — holding this firmly, one goes to peace

Translation · colloquial

All of this stands here unreal, wearing the look of a conjuror's show;and what is real about a conjuror's show? Hold that firmly, and you go to peace.

Practice

Look at whatever stands before you and try the thought on it, firmly: all this is unreal, wearing the look of a conjuror's show. Then press the question the verse asks — what reality does a conjuror's show have? Do not answer it cleverly; let it stand. It is the firmness of this seeing, kept up against habit, that carries you into peace.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

The heading is the citable thing here: वेदान्ताभिमताम् इन्द्रजालधारणाम् आह. It is the tradition's own commentator naming the Vedāntin provenance of this portal.

Commentary

The conjuror returns, and this verse disagrees with the earlier one. There the show was fully real as appearance and only wrongly attributed; here the first word is flatly unreal, and this is as close as the collection comes to the illusionism of the Vedāntins, which sits awkwardly in a tradition insisting the world is Śiva's actual self-expression. We take the earlier verse as the book's settled view and this one as a device — note that what is offered is a question, and that the operative word is the same one used for the great identity claim: from firmness. You are not asked to believe a metaphysics. You are asked to put a question to whatever is in front of you and keep putting it, without letting the mind close it with a clever answer. And the promise is correspondingly modest. Not liberation, not Bhairava's own form. Peace.

Word by word

atattvam — not the real, unreality (n. nom. sg.); indrajāla+ābham — having the look of a conjuror's show (n. nom. sg.); idam sarvam — all this (n. nom. sg.); avasthitam — stands, is situated (past part., n. nom. sg.); kim — what? (n. nom. sg.); tattvam — reality (n. nom. sg.); indrajālasya — of a conjuror's show (n. gen. sg.); iti — thus [asking]; dārḍhyāt — from firmness [of conviction] (n. abl. sg.); śamam — to peace (m. acc. sg.); vrajet — one should go (3rd sg. opt., √vraj)

Open portal 110 in the reader →

111The self without change

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 134 · dhāraṇā 111 · Awareness · śāktopāya · a means of mind · causal

आत्मनो निर्विकारस्य क्व ज्ञानं क्व च वा क्रिया

ज्ञानायत्ता बहिर्भावा अतः शून्यम् इदं जगत्

ātmano nirvikārasya kva jñānaṃ kva ca vā kriyā
jñānāyattā bahirbhāvā ataḥ śūnyam idaṃ jagat

Translation · literal Sanskrit

For the changeless ātman, where is knowledge — where, for that matter, actionouter things hang upon knowing; hence this world is empty

Translation · colloquial

For a self that never changes, where is knowing, and where, for that matter, is doing?Outer things hang upon being known; and so this world is empty.

Practice

Consider the ātman — the self — as this verse presents it: without change. Ask where, in what does not change, knowing could take place, or doing. Then notice that every outer thing hangs on being known; withdraw the knowing and nothing of the world stands on its own. Hold both sides until the conclusion is seen rather than argued: this world is empty. The verse ends there. Śivopādhyāya does not — what settles after that conclusion, he says, is pure consciousness, and the attaining of the state beyond which there is nothing further.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

The Candrajñāna, quoted at length. Its final line — तस्माच् छून्यं जगद् ध्यायेद् यत्र नश्येत् कदाचन — is the instruction he takes the verse to be giving.

Commentary

An argument in two moves: nothing can happen in what does not change, so neither knowing nor doing belongs to the self; and everything outer depends on being known, so with the knowing withdrawn nothing stands. Hence emptiness. A reader who has been paying attention will notice this contradicts the portal where you claimed identity with the doer of all — where agency was precisely what made this tradition's absolute different from the Vedāntin's. The contradiction is in the text and not in our reading of it. The honest thing to say is what the whole collection has been demonstrating: this is an anthology of doorways, assembled by people who cared whether a door opened and not whether the doors could be arranged into a system. A changeless self is the premise that works for this practice. Twenty-five verses ago an acting self was the premise that worked for that one. Both are here, and the book does not appear to be troubled.

Word by word

ātmanaḥ — of the self (m. gen. sg.); nirvikārasya — without change (m. gen. sg.); kva — where? (rhetorical, twice); jñānam — knowledge (n. nom. sg.); ca vā — and, for that matter; kriyā — action (f. nom. sg.); jñāna+āyattāḥ — dependent on knowing (m. nom. pl.); bahir+bhāvāḥ — outer things, external existents (m. nom. pl.); ataḥ — hence; śūnyam — empty (n. nom. sg.); idam — this (n. nom. sg.); jagat — world (n. nom. sg.)

Open portal 111 in the reader →

112No bondage, no release

Vijñāna Bhairava, verse 135 · dhāraṇā 112 · Awareness · śāmbhavopāya · no means · causal

न मे बन्धो न मोक्षो मे भीतस्यैता विभीषिकाः

प्रतिबिम्बम् इदम् बुद्धेर् जलेष्व् इव विवस्वतः

na me bandho na mokṣo me bhītasyaitā vibhīṣikāḥ
pratibimbam idam buddher jaleṣv iva vivasvataḥ

Translation · literal Sanskrit

No bondage is mine, no release is mine — bogeys, these, for someone afraidall this is a reflection in the understanding, the way the sun lies in water

Translation · colloquial

No bondage is mine, and no release is mine either: these are bogeys, and only for someone afraid.All of it is a reflection in the understanding, the way the sun lies in water.

Practice

Say it as your own, and test it: no bondage was ever mine, no release either — those are bogeys, frightening only to one already frightened. Then look at the whole scene of bound-and-freed the way you look at the sun lying in water: a reflection of the understanding, vivid, and not the thing itself. Let hope and fear stand down together. And then, because this is where he chooses to close the hundred and twelve, let go of the understanding the whole scene is being reflected in.

Śivopādhyāya’s commentary

Kalhaṇa the poet quoted by name, with the scarecrow-and-elephants verse; the sage Śuka; the Bṛhadāraṇyaka's from a second, fear arises. Footnote, plate 0128 line 2: MSS ga and pu read बालस्यैता, these are bogeys for A CHILD, where the KSTS prints भीतस्यैता, for one who is afraid. Our English follows the printed text per the standing rule, but the variant is worth the margin: it says in one word what our commentary says in a phrase about the word being nursery-grade. And his series does not stop where the text's own count does: immediately after this verse he heads the next one 'having spoken of the abandonment of the inner instrument, he states the dhāraṇā of the abandonment of the outer instruments' — so VBT 135 is for him the inner-instrument dhāraṇā and VBT 136 its outer counterpart. The hundred and twelve are the text's own count, stated in the verses that follow; the division into dhāraṇās past that point is his.

Commentary

The hundred and twelve end not on a technique but on the withdrawal of the frame the techniques were set in. Bondage and release are named vibhīṣikā, and Śivopādhyāya's gloss is better than our nursery-grade: the straw man in the field, whose whole office is to frighten sparrows. Kalhaṇa, the one poet he names, extends it exactly — set in the forest, what could it do against trampling wild elephants? Gauḍapāda makes the same move in the Māṇḍūkya Kārikā, where there is no cessation and no arising, no one bound and no one seeking, no one freed; that comparison is ours and not his. The simile does the rest. The sun in water is not a false sun; it is a real reflection of a real sun, in the wrong place — and, he adds, the wrong way up. He names the water, too: it is the buddhi, and the two images floating in it are I am bound and I am free. Which is why he does not stop where we stop. Let one abandon the intellect itself, he says: the last move is to drop the surface on which the whole scene of bound-and-free is being cast. That, and not a hundred-and-thirteenth practice, is the end of the book.

Word by word

na — not; me — mine, for me (gen. sg.); bandhaḥ — bondage (m. nom. sg.); na — nor; mokṣaḥ — release, liberation (m. nom. sg.); me — for me (gen. sg.); bhītasya — for one who is frightened (m. gen. sg.); etāḥ — these (f. nom. pl.); vibhīṣikāḥ — bogeys, scare-figures (f. nom. pl.); pratibimbam — a reflection (n. nom. sg.); idam — this (n. nom. sg.); buddheḥ — of the understanding (f. gen. sg.); jaleṣu — in waters (n. loc. pl.); iva — as, like; vivasvataḥ — of the sun (m. gen. sg.)

Open portal 112 in the reader →
